The Maxwell–Higgs System with Scalar Potential on Subextremal Kerr Spacetimes: Nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness
Bobby Eka Gunara, Mulyanto, Fiki Taufik Akbar

Abstract
We construct nonlinear wave operators and prove small-data asymptotic completeness for the Maxwell–Higgs system on the domain of outer communications of every four-dimensional subextremal Kerr black hole ( 𝒟 M , a , g M , a ) (\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a}) with M > 0 M>0 and | a | < M |a|<M , for gauge-invariant nonnegative scalar potentials P P satisfying Assumption 1.1 with mass parameter m 2 ≥ 0 m^{2}\geq 0 . The massless case m = 0 m=0 is unconditional on the full subextremal range. For m 2 > 0 m^{2}>0 the same conclusions follow assuming the massive linear package 𝖫𝗂𝗇 k ( m ) \mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)} for the linear comparison system (in particular, no exponentially growing modes); this fails for an open set of masses due to superradiant instability [ 1 ] . We work in the radiative (charge-free) regime; stationary Coulomb (Kerr–Newman) modes are treated separately. Asymptotic states are described by gauge-covariant radiation fields on ℐ ± ∪ ℋ ± \mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm} (and, when m > 0 m>0 , an additional timelike/Dollard channel), yielding a gauge-invariant nonlinear scattering map on the residual-gauge quotient. The scattering map is a small-data bijection, is Fréchet differentiable at 0 0 with derivative equal to linear Kerr scattering, admits a quadratic (Born) expansion with an O ⁡ ( ‖ U ‖ 3 ) O(\|U\|^{3}) remainder in the natural asymptotic topology, and is real-analytic for analytic P P . The nonlinear argument is presented as a transfer principle from a black-box linear estimate package for inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon and charge-free Maxwell fields, verified here in the massless Kerr case (and proved self-contained in Schwarzschild).



Mathematics Subject Classification.
35Q61, 35L05, 83C57.

Keywords.
Maxwell–Higgs system; Kerr black holes; Schwarzschild spacetime; Radiation fields; Nonlinear scattering.

1
Introduction and main results

1.1
Setting and equations

We study the Maxwell–Higgs system on the domain of outer communications of asymptotically flat stationary black holes, with particular emphasis on the Kerr family.
The main conceptual point is that the background geometry enters the small-data nonlinear theory only through the black-box linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
(Definition
1.5
), leading to the modular scattering theorem (Theorem
1.8
).
As an application, we obtain nonlinear scattering and Coulomb asymptotics on the full
subextremal
Kerr range
|a|<M
in the massless regime
m=0
, and we also state the corresponding massive extension for
m^{2}>0
under a linear Klein–Gordon package assumption (Theorem
1.1
).
To keep the geometric bookkeeping and commutator calculations transparent, we carry out the detailed quantitative analysis in the Schwarzschild model
a=0
(Sections
7
–
10
),
and we verify the required linear package on Kerr in Section
6
by appealing to the available linear decay and scattering theory.
We also record the Lorenz-gauge formulation and an admissible class of Cauchy data on Kerr in Section
2
.

Fix Kerr parameters
M>0
and
a
with
|a|<M
.
We work on the Kerr domain of outer communications

\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{M,a}=\{(t,r,\theta,\varphi):r>r_{+}\},\qquad r_{+}=M+\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}},

(1.1)

equipped with the Kerr metric.
For energy estimates across the future event horizon we use the standard horizon-regular ingoing Kerr (Kerr–star) coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, related to Boyer–Lindquist coordinates
(t_{\mathrm{BL}},r,\theta,\varphi_{\mathrm{BL}})
by

\mathrm{d}t=\mathrm{d}t_{\mathrm{BL}}+\frac{r^{2}+a^{2}}{\Delta}\,\mathrm{d}r,\qquad\mathrm{d}\varphi=\mathrm{d}\varphi_{\mathrm{BL}}+\frac{a}{\Delta}\,\mathrm{d}r.

Since the change of variables depends only on
r
, the coordinate vector fields
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{\varphi}
coincide with the stationary and axial Killing fields of Kerr (equivalently,
\partial_{t_{\mathrm{BL}}}
and
\partial_{\varphi_{\mathrm{BL}}}
).
In these coordinates the metric reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=-\Bigl(1-\frac{2Mr}{\Sigma}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t^{2}+2\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}r-\frac{4Mar\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}\varphi-2a\sin^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\varphi

(1.2)

\displaystyle+\Sigma\,\mathrm{d}\theta^{2}+\Bigl(r^{2}+a^{2}+\frac{2Ma^{2}r\sin^{2}\theta}{\Sigma}\Bigr)\sin^{2}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\varphi^{2},

where

\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta,\qquad\Delta=r^{2}-2Mr+a^{2}.

The Schwarzschild case is recovered by setting
a=0
, in which
r_{+}=2M
and (
1.2
) reduces to the ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein form of the Schwarzschild metric.

For the global Cauchy evolution we work with a horizon-regular
spacelike
time function built from the Kerr–star coordinates.
We set

t^{\star}:=t-r,

(1.3)

and denote by
\Sigma_{\tau}:=\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
its level sets.
Since
r
is independent of
t
, one has
\partial_{t^{\star}}=\partial_{t}
, so the stationary Killing field is still represented by
\partial_{t^{\star}}
in the
(t^{\star},r,\theta,\varphi)
chart.
These hypersurfaces are smooth spacelike Cauchy hypersurfaces for
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
which intersect
\mathcal{H}^{+}
transversely and extend to spatial infinity.
We use
\tau
(rather than
t
) as the global time parameter in energy estimates on Kerr.

Let
A
be a
U(1)
connection
1
–form with curvature
F=\mathrm{d}A
(in local coordinates
F_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}A_{\mu}
), and let
\phi:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{C}
be a complex scalar field.
We write
D_{\mu}\phi=\nabla_{\mu}\phi-iA_{\mu}\phi
for the gauge-covariant derivative.
The system arises from the Lagrangian density

\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-D_{\mu}\phi\,\overline{D^{\mu}\phi}-P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(1.4)

and the Euler–Lagrange equations take the form

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}F_{\alpha\gamma}

\displaystyle=-i\bigl(D_{\gamma}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\gamma}\phi}\bigr),

(1.5)

\displaystyle D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi

\displaystyle=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

(1.6)

A robust decay theory on Kerr must account for several geometric effects:
(i) the
redshift
near the event horizon
r=r_{+}
, which yields coercive energy control,
(ii)
trapping
in the photon region (the trapped set of null geodesics), which in the Schwarzschild limit
a=0
reduces to the photon sphere
r=3M
, and
(iii) in the rotating case
a\neq 0
,
superradiance
, which requires a careful choice of energies and multipliers in the linear theory.

1.2
Notation and conventions

We collect here the notation most frequently used in the statement of the main results and in the Kerr sections (Sections
2
–
6
).
Unless stated otherwise, all geometric objects (connections, Hodge star, volume forms, etc.) are defined with respect to the relevant background metric
g
.

Coordinates, hypersurfaces, and regions.

We work in ingoing Kerr (Kerr–star) coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
, and we use the horizon-regular spacelike time function
t^{\star}=t-r
from (
1.3
).
We write

\Sigma_{\tau}:=\{t^{\star}=\tau\},\qquad S_{\tau,r}:=\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r=\mathrm{const}\}.

For
\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}
we set

\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}):=\bigcup_{\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\Sigma_{\tau}

for the spacetime slab between
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
and
\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}
.
In the far region we use retarded/advanced times

u:=t^{\star}-r,\qquad v:=t^{\star}+r,

and the outgoing/incoming vector fields

L:=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\partial_{r},\qquad\underline{L}:=\partial_{t^{\star}}-\partial_{r}.

Vector fields, commutators, and inequalities.

We denote by
T:=\partial_{t^{\star}}
the stationary Killing field and by
\Phi:=\partial_{\varphi}
the axial Killing field.
The symbol
N
denotes a fixed globally timelike redshift multiplier (agreeing with
T
for large
r
).
We write
\mathcal{Z}
for a fixed collection of smooth commutator vector fields (typically built from
T
,
\Phi
, and angular derivatives) used to define higher-order energies.
We write
A\lesssim B
to mean
A\leq C\,B
for a constant
C
depending only on fixed parameters such as
(M,a)
, the commutation order
k
, and the small loss parameter
\delta>0
.
The notation
A\simeq B
means
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
.

Derivatives, gauge-covariant fields, and currents.

We use the Lorentzian signature
(-,+,+,+)
and the Einstein summation convention.
We denote by
\nabla
the Levi–Civita connection and by
\square_{g}=\nabla^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}
the wave operator.
Given a potential
A
we write
F=\mathrm{d}A
for its curvature and
D_{\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}-iA_{\mu}
for the gauge-covariant derivative.
We reserve the letter
F
for the curvature
2
–form; when discussing inhomogeneous scalar wave/Klein–Gordon equations we denote the forcing term by
\mathfrak{F}
.
If
\Sigma
is a spacelike hypersurface with future unit normal
n_{\Sigma}
, then for any current
J
we write

\int_{\Sigma}J\cdot n_{\Sigma}
as shorthand for
\int_{\Sigma}J_{\mu}n_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma}
, where
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma}
is the induced volume form.
The Maxwell–Higgs current is

J_{\mu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\mu}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\mu}\phi}\bigr)=2\,\Im(\overline{\phi}\,D_{\mu}\phi).

Scattering maps.

The linear scattering maps are denoted
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, with ranges
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}
(Definition
10.7
).
The nonlinear scattering maps are
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
, their inverses
\mathscr{W}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}=(\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}})^{-1}
are the nonlinear wave operators, and the two-sided nonlinear scattering map is

\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{nl}}:=\mathscr{S}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}\circ\mathscr{W}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}.

Reserved symbols.

We reserve
\delta>0
for the small loss parameter in the Kerr decay hierarchy,
\kappa_{0}\in(0,1)
for the dyadic/cone localization parameter in the Schwarzschild model, and
\Delta
for differences of two solutions/iterates in stability and contraction arguments.

1.3
Relation to the literature

The physical-space vector field method provides a robust mechanism for boundedness and decay of linear waves on black hole exteriors, combining redshift estimates near the horizon with Morawetz and
r^{p}
hierarchies controlling trapping.
For Maxwell fields on Schwarzschild, local energy decay and pointwise decay were established by Blue
[
2
]
and further refined, in particular with Morawetz-type inequalities, by Andersson–Bäckdahl–Blue
[
3
]
.
For scalar waves
\square_{g}u=0
, the redshift and decay framework was developed by Dafermos–Rodnianski
[
4
]
, while inhomogeneous estimates and applications to semilinear problems were studied by Blue–Sterbenz
[
5
]
.
Analytic scattering theories on Schwarzschild for linear Klein–Gordon type equations go back to the works of Dimock–Kay
[
6
]
and Bachelot
[
7
]
, and conformal/characteristic scattering theories were later developed by Nicolas
[
8
]
.
A recent conformal scattering theory for finite-energy Maxwell
potentials
on asymptotically flat spacetimes was obtained by Nicolas–Taujanskas
[
9
]
.
For Kerr exteriors, a definitive physical-space scattering theory for the scalar wave equation in the full subextremal regime
|a|<M
was proved by Dafermos–Rodnianski–Shlapentokh-Rothman
[
10
]
.

On the nonlinear side, small-data global existence and decay for semilinear wave equations on Schwarzschild were proved by Blue–Sterbenz
[
5
]
, and more recently geometric scattering constructions for nonlinear wave equations on Schwarzschild have been developed (see for instance Ngoc–Xuan
[
11
]
).
For coupled gauge theories in Minkowski space, the Maxwell–Klein–Gordon (MKG) system has a well-developed global Cauchy theory; in particular, it admits a detailed asymptotic description in Lorenz gauge and exhibits a weak-null-structure
[
12
]
.
The associated final-state problem (“scattering from infinity”) in Lorenz gauge was solved for the massless MKG system by He
[
13
]
, and for the massive system by Chen
[
14
]
; see also the recent construction of solutions scattering to
large
characteristic data by Dai–Mei–Wei–Yang
[
15
]
.
Related nonlinear scattering theories for Maxwell–scalar systems on other curved backgrounds include the conformal scattering construction on de Sitter space by Taujanskas
[
16
]
.

Comparison with Minkowski scattering from infinity.

The Lorenz-gauge final-state constructions for the Maxwell–Klein–Gordon system in Minkowski space
[
13
,
14
,
15
]
provide a conceptual template for our wave-operator theorem, but the Schwarzschild geometry forces several genuinely new features.
First, the natural “scattering boundary” is
two-component
: in addition to null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
one must control radiation through the event horizons
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, and the redshift effect is essential for coercive estimates up to
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
.

\mathcal{H}^{+}
\mathcal{H}^{-}
\mathcal{I}^{+}
\mathcal{I}^{-}
i^{+}
i^{-}
\mathcal{D}
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
\Sigma_{\tau}

Figure 1:
Schematic picture of the domain of outer communications
\mathcal{D}
and its two-component scattering boundary
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
.

Second, the trapped set (photon region)—which in the Schwarzschild limit
a=0
reduces to the photon sphere
r=3M
— prevents the use of global conformal Killing fields, so one must combine Morawetz estimates and an
r^{p}
hierarchy in physical space to obtain decay with quantitative control of trapping losses.
Third, the conserved electromagnetic charges produce stationary Coulomb modes; a Coulomb subtraction is therefore indispensable before any radiative decay or scattering statement.
Finally, to formulate radiation fields intrinsically for a gauge theory, we use parallel transport along null generators to define gauge-covariant asymptotic data, rather than fixing a preferred gauge at infinity.

The present paper complements these developments by giving, to our knowledge, the first complete nonlinear scattering and wave-operator theory for a
(3+1)
-dimensional gauge–scalar system on a
rotating
asymptotically flat black hole exterior: namely, the full subextremal Kerr family in the massless regime (and, for
m^{2}>0
, in any parameter regime for which the corresponding massive linear Klein–Gordon package holds).
In particular, we construct nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
and formulate the scattering theory intrinsically on the gauge quotient in the radiative (charge-free) regime.
The Schwarzschild analysis provides a self-contained proof of the required linear package and yields additional massive/timelike-channel refinements.

1.4
Main conceptual contributions

From the perspective of nonlinear decay and scattering on black hole exteriors, the main new features of this paper can be summarized as follows:

•

A modular transfer principle from linear to nonlinear scattering.

We isolate the background-dependent input as a black-box linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
for the decoupled linear comparison system (charge-free Maxwell together with
(\square_{g}-m^{2})
) and show that it yields small-data global existence, decay, and nonlinear scattering for the Maxwell–Higgs system with mass parameter
m^{2}\geq 0
in Assumption
1.1
. In the massless case
m=0
, subextremal Kerr scattering follows by verifying
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
(equivalently
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(0)}
) in the available linear theory. For
m^{2}>0
on Kerr, the corresponding package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
is conditional due to the superradiant obstruction; see Remark
1.4
.

•

Nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness on Kerr and Schwarzschild.

We construct nonlinear wave operators and prove small-data asymptotic completeness for the Maxwell–Higgs system on every subextremal Kerr exterior in the massless regime
m=0
and, under the explicit massive linear package hypothesis
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
, also for massive potentials with
m^{2}>0
. In the Schwarzschild model
a=0
we moreover verify the massive linear hypothesis and treat general nonnegative potentials (including the massive timelike/Dollard channel).
In particular, we obtain a two-sided nonlinear scattering operator relating past and future asymptotic data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
(and, when
m>0
, the timelike channel).

•

Gauge-covariant radiation fields and the radiative (charge-free) regime.

We define radiation data in a gauge-invariant fashion (via parallel transport along null generators) and formulate the scattering theory intrinsically on the radiative sector, in which the Maxwell field is charge-free and admits a global Lorenz-gauge potential.
(Stationary Coulomb modes are discussed separately; see Remark
2.1
.)

•

Intrinsic scattering on the gauge quotient and tangency to linear theory.

Although the construction uses Lorenz gauge at the level of potentials, the radiation fields are defined in a gauge-covariant fashion, so the nonlinear wave operators and scattering maps descend to canonical maps on the quotient of small Cauchy data by gauge.
Moreover, the scattering operator is Fréchet differentiable at the trivial solution and is tangent to the linear scattering map.

•

Born approximation and higher-order structure of the scattering operator.

We identify the leading quadratic contribution to the nonlinear scattering maps and obtain an
O(\|U\|^{3})
remainder in the asymptotic topology, yielding twice Fréchet differentiability at the origin and an explicit formula for the second differential (a quadratic “scattering amplitude”).

•

Real-analyticity and a convergent Born series for scattering.

For the analytic model potentials (and, more generally, for any real-analytic gauge-invariant
\widetilde{P}
near
0
), the nonlinear scattering map admits an
absolutely convergent
power-series expansion at the origin. In particular, the Kerr scattering map and wave operators are real analytic near the vacuum, and all higher differentials can be identified by iterated Duhamel/Born expansions (Proposition
1.12
).

•

A robust physical-space decay mechanism including trapping and redshift.

We prove uniform boundedness of commuted energies, an integrated local energy decay/Morawetz estimate controlling the trapped set (the photon sphere
r=3M
in Schwarzschild and the photon region on Kerr), and an
r^{p}
–hierarchy yielding quantitative decay for the radiative field.

•

Massive potentials and the timelike channel.

For potentials with
m^{2}>0
we identify and control the additional timelike scattering component, leading to a nonlinear analogue of Dollard-type modified scattering.

We emphasize that the detailed model potentials in Assumption
1.1
are included for concreteness.
In the small-data regime, the estimates use only smoothness, gauge invariance, nonnegativity, and the vanishing of
P
at the vacuum; see Remark
1.1
.

1.5
Structural assumption on the potential

To control the nonlinear potential terms in the energy method we assume that
P
is nonnegative, gauge invariant, and vanishes at the vacuum.

Assumption 1.1

(Scalar potential)
.

The scalar potential
P:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{R}
is gauge invariant and depends only on the scalar
|\phi|^{2}
, i.e.
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
for some
\widetilde{P}:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
.
Assume that
\widetilde{P}\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
and
\widetilde{P}\geq 0
(after fixing the irrelevant additive constant so that the potential energy density vanishes in the vacuum configuration).

Moreover, we assume that there exist a constant
m^{2}\geq 0
, an integer
N_{P}\geq 0
, and a function
Q\in C^{\infty}([0,\infty))
such that

\widetilde{P}^{\prime}(s)=m^{2}+s\,Q(s)\qquad\text{for all }s\geq 0,

(1.7)

and, for every integer
0\leq j\leq k+3
, there exists a constant
C_{P,j}
such that

|Q^{(j)}(s)|\leq C_{P,j}\,(1+s^{N_{P}})\qquad\text{for all }s\geq 0.

(1.8)

(In particular, the nonlinear part of the force term
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P
is at least cubic in
\phi
and has at most polynomial growth in
|\phi|
.)

For concreteness one may keep in mind the following model potentials:

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}(s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\alpha_{n}s^{n},\qquad\alpha_{n}\geq 0,\ \ N\in\mathbb{N},

(1.9)

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}(s)

\displaystyle=c_{3}\bigl(1-\cos(\eta\sqrt{s})\bigr),\qquad c_{3}\geq 0,\ \eta>0,

(1.10)

\displaystyle\widetilde{P}(s)

\displaystyle=c_{4}\bigl(1-e^{-\lambda s}\bigr),\qquad c_{4}\geq 0,\ \lambda>0,

(1.11)

which all satisfy (
1.7
)–(
1.8
) for suitable choices of
m^{2}\geq 0
,
N_{P}
,
Q
, and constants
C_{P,j}
.
(Adding a constant to
\widetilde{P}
does not change (
1.6
), and we fix this constant in the standard way so that the potential energy density vanishes in the vacuum.)

Remark 1.1

(Potential force bounds)
.

Gauge invariance implies the identity

\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}^{\prime}(|\phi|^{2})\,\phi.

(1.12)

By Assumption
1.1
we can write this
exactly
as

\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=m^{2}\phi+\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi),\qquad\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi):=Q(|\phi|^{2})\,|\phi|^{2}\phi.

(1.13)

Using (
1.8
) with
j=0
one obtains the global polynomial bound

|\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)|\leq C_{P,0}\,(1+|\phi|^{2N_{P}})\,|\phi|^{3}\qquad\text{for all }\phi\in\mathbb{C}.

(1.14)

For the polynomial model (
1.9
) this is consistent with the exact formula

\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)=\sum_{n=2}^{N}n\alpha_{n}\,|\phi|^{2n-2}\phi,

i.e.
\mathcal{R}_{P}
is a finite sum of odd powers
|\phi|^{2n-1}
with constant coefficients.

Moreover, by the chain rule and (
1.8
), every commuted term
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
(with
\mathcal{Z}
from the admissible structure in Definition
1.3
) is a finite sum of products with at least three factors drawn from
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi,\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi}\}
, with coefficients depending only on
N_{P}
and finitely many constants
C_{P,j}
.
Consequently, the potential contribution can be treated on the same footing as the gauge nonlinearities in the bootstrap and scattering arguments, without imposing any separate
a priori
restriction on the pointwise size of
|\phi|
beyond what is already obtained from the energy/Sobolev estimates.

1.6
Main results on Kerr and Schwarzschild spacetimes

We state our main results in terms of the global horizon-regular spacelike foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
(on Kerr given by
t^{\star}=t-r
) and in terms of gauge-covariant radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
. In the Schwarzschild model
a=0
we also record explicit pointwise decay rates in Eddington–Finkelstein double-null coordinates; see Corollaries
1.16
–
1.17
below. In the Kerr setting, far-region decay estimates compatible with the
r^{p}
hierarchy are recorded in Section
4
.

Throughout,
A\lesssim B
means
A\leq CB
for a constant
C
depending only on the fixed background parameters (in particular
M
and, on Kerr,
a
), on the fixed differentiation order
k
(and, when relevant, on finitely many bounds for the potential
P
).

A basic obstruction to decay for Maxwell fields on stationary asymptotically flat black-hole exteriors (including Kerr and Schwarzschild) is the presence of stationary Coulomb modes.
We therefore formulate the radiative decay and scattering theory for the charge-free part of the Maxwell field; for general data one subtracts the stationary Coulomb field determined by the conserved electric and magnetic charges (see Theorem
1.1
and Remark
2.1
).

Definition 1.1

(Electromagnetic charges)
.

Let
S_{\tau,r}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the sphere
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r=\mathrm{const}\}
with area form
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\tau,r}
.
The electric and magnetic charges of
F
are

Q_{e}(\tau,r):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{S_{\tau,r}}{}^{\star}\!F,\qquad Q_{m}(\tau,r):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{S_{\tau,r}}F,

where
⋆
denotes the Hodge dual on
(\mathcal{D},g)
.
By the Maxwell equation,
Q_{e}(\tau,r)
and
Q_{m}(\tau,r)
are independent of
(\tau,r)
; we write
(Q_{e},Q_{m})
for these conserved charges.
We say that the data are
charge-free
if
Q_{e}=Q_{m}=0
(equivalently, if the limiting charges at infinity vanish on the initial slice).

Remark 1.2
.

If the asymptotic charges are nonzero then
F
contains a stationary Coulomb component of size
\sim r^{-2}
.
The decay statements below concern the charge-free (radiative) regime; one may alternatively subtract the Coulomb part and prove decay for the remainder.

Nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness on subextremal Kerr.

In Section
10.6
we implement the construction of gauge-covariant radiation fields and nonlinear scattering maps in the Schwarzschild model; since the argument uses only the (possibly massive) linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
, the same construction applies on Kerr once
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
is available (unconditionally for
m=0
, and conditionally for
m^{2}>0
; see Remark
1.4
) (Section
6
). We denote the forward and backward nonlinear scattering maps on Kerr by

\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}

in terms of the asymptotic data on the (possibly extended) scattering boundary

\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}_{M,a},\qquad\partial_{\pm}^{(0)}\mathcal{D}_{M,a}=\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm},

where for
m^{2}>0
one also adjoins the timelike/Dollard channel at
i^{\pm}
as in Definition
10.7
.
Solving the nonlinear final-state problem we obtain the inverse maps, i.e. the (nonlinear) wave operators, and hence a two-sided nonlinear scattering operator and small-data asymptotic completeness.
The associated linear asymptotic data spaces
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}
(the ranges of the linear scattering maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}
) are defined in Definition
10.7
; here
m^{2}\geq 0
is the mass parameter appearing in Assumption
1.1
(equivalently, in (
1.7
)).
In particular, the linear comparison dynamics is the decoupled system consisting of the charge-free Maxwell equations and the scalar Klein–Gordon equation
(\square_{g_{M,a}}-m^{2})\varphi=0
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
, with asymptotic data posed on the extended scattering boundary
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
(Definition
10.7
).

Theorem 1.1

(Nonlinear wave operators and small-data asymptotic completeness on subextremal Kerr)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and assume that the scalar potential
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
.
Let
m^{2}\geq 0
be the mass parameter in Assumption
1.1
(equivalently, in (
1.7
)).
Fix Kerr parameters
M>0
and
a
with
|a|<M
.

Assume that the Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
satisfies the massive linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
of Definition
1.5

for the decoupled linear comparison system (charge-free Maxwell together with
(\square_{g_{M,a}}-m^{2})\varphi=0
).
(For
m=0
this hypothesis holds on the full subextremal Kerr range by
[
17
,
10
]
for the scalar wave equation and by
[
18
]
for Maxwell; see Remark
1.3
.)
There exists
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}}>0
(depending on
M,a,k,m
, on
N_{P}
, and on finitely many constants
C_{P,j}
in (
1.8
)) such that the following hold.
All implicit constants depend only on
M,a,k,m
and on
P
through these structural bounds.

(i)

(
Forward wave operator.
)
For every
\mathcal{R}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{+,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}})
there exists a unique global charge-free solution
(F,\phi)
of the Maxwell–Higgs system on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
admitting a Lorenz-gauge potential
A
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
such that

\mathscr{S}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(F,\phi)=\mathcal{R}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}.

Moreover, if
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
denotes the unique linear comparison solution corresponding to
\mathcal{R}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, then
(F,\phi)
scatters to
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
on
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
in the sense encoded by the
\mathfrak{R}_{+,M,a}^{(m)}
-topology.

(ii)

(
Backward wave operator.
)
For every
\mathcal{R}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{-,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}})
there exists a unique global charge-free solution
(F,\phi)
of the Maxwell–Higgs system on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
admitting a Lorenz-gauge potential
A
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
such that

\mathscr{S}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(F,\phi)=\mathcal{R}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}},

and which scatters to the corresponding linear comparison solution on
\partial_{-}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
.

(iii)

(
Asymptotic completeness and homeomorphism.
)
The maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
restrict to homeomorphisms between a neighbourhood of
0
in the completed Kerr data space
\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
of Definition
2.1
and the neighbourhoods
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}})
.
Their inverses

\mathscr{W}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}:=(\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}})^{-1}

are the (nonlinear) wave operators.
In particular, the two-sided nonlinear scattering operator

\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{nl}}:=\mathscr{S}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}\circ\mathscr{W}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}:\mathfrak{R}_{-,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{+,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}})

is a homeomorphism and small-data asymptotic completeness holds.

(iv)

(
Gauge quotient.
)
The maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
,
\mathscr{W}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{nl}}
depend only on the gauge-equivalence class of the underlying Cauchy data (Definition
1.2
) and hence descend to homeomorphisms on the corresponding quotient phase spaces.

(v)

(
Tangency to linear scattering.
)
The maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}:\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\to\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}
are Fréchet differentiable at
0
, and

D\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(0)=\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}.

Equivalently, the two-sided nonlinear scattering operator is Fréchet differentiable at
0
with

D\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{nl}}(0)=\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}},\qquad\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=\mathscr{S}_{+,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}\circ(\mathscr{S}_{-,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}})^{-1}.

(vi)

(
Quadratic expansion (Born approximation) at the origin.
)
There exists a continuous symmetric bilinear map

\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}:\ \mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\times\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}

such that for all sufficiently small
U\in\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
,

\bigl\|\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)-\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}(U)-\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}(U,U)\bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}}\ \lesssim\ \|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}}^{3}.

In particular
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
are twice Fréchet differentiable at
0
with

D^{2}\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(0)[U_{1},U_{2}]=2\,\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}(U_{1},U_{2}).

(vii)

(
Real-analyticity and convergent Born series.
)
Assume in addition that the potential
P
is real analytic in a neighborhood of
\phi=0
(in particular, this holds for the model potentials in Assumption
1.1
).
Then there exist continuous symmetric
n
–linear maps

\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}^{(n)}:\ (\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}))^{n}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)},\qquad n\geq 1,

with
\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}^{(1)}=\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}^{(2)}=\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}
, such that for all sufficiently small
U\in\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
one has the absolutely convergent expansion

\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}^{(n)}(U,\dots,U)\quad\text{in }\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)},

and for every
N\geq 1
,

\Bigl\|\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)-\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathscr{B}_{\pm,M,a}^{(n)}(U,\dots,U)\Bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}}\ \lesssim_{N}\ \|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}}^{N+1}.

In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
and
\mathscr{W}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
are real analytic near
0
. (An abstract proof is given in Proposition
1.12
.)

Corollary 1.2

(Schwarzschild as a special case)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and assume that the scalar potential
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
.
Let
m^{2}\geq 0
be the corresponding mass parameter.
Then the conclusions of Theorem
1.1
hold on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
(i.e. the case
a=0
),
with the stationary Coulomb field playing the role of
F^{(a)}_{Q_{e},Q_{m}}
.
When
m^{2}>0
this includes the timelike/Dollard channel in the scalar asymptotic data; see Theorem
1.14
for the detailed Schwarzschild scattering statement.

Remark 1.3

(Linear package on Kerr)
.

The nonlinear argument of this paper is modular: on any fixed black-hole exterior for which the (possibly massive) linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
of Definition
1.5

holds for the decoupled linear comparison system with the same mass parameter
m
and for charge-free Maxwell fields (after Coulomb subtraction), the small-data global existence and scattering conclusions follow.

On subextremal Kerr the massless package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(0)}
(equivalently
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
) is available in the full range
|a|<M
: for scalar waves by
[
17
,
10
]
, and for Maxwell fields by
[
18
]

(building on the Teukolsky estimates
[
19
,
20
]
); see Proposition
6.1
.
For
m^{2}>0
, one would need the corresponding package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
for the massive Klein–Gordon equation (including the timelike/Dollard channel); see Remark
1.4
.
In the very slowly rotating regime, an alternative vector-field approach is provided by Andersson–Blue
[
21
]
.

Remark 1.4

(Massive potentials on Kerr)
.

When
m^{2}>0
, the scalar component contains a massive Klein–Gordon operator.
On Kerr backgrounds with
a\neq 0
the massive linear Klein–Gordon equation exhibits a superradiant instability for an open set of masses,
so one cannot expect an unconditional small-data scattering theorem in this setting without additional restrictions; see
[
1
]
.
Accordingly, the
m^{2}>0
part of Theorem
1.1
is conditional on the availability of the corresponding linear massive Klein–Gordon decay and scattering theory (in particular, on the absence of exponentially growing modes). The massless case
m=0
is unconditional on the full subextremal Kerr family.

Main regional decay theorems: far-away region and trapped region

The Maxwell–Higgs system is nonlinear. In Lorenz gauge (
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
), the potential formulation reduces to a coupled system of wave equations on the fixed background,

\square_{g_{M,a}}A_{\nu}=J_{\nu}[\phi;A],\qquad\square_{g_{M,a}}\phi=\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}[\phi;A],

so the linear decay theory must be applied in an
inhomogeneous
form.

The key analytic inputs are therefore:
(i) far-away-region decay estimates (Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
hierarchy) for waves with forcing and for charge-free Maxwell fields with current, and
(ii) trapped/compact-region integrated local energy decay (Morawetz) with trapping weights.
On Kerr, the corresponding quantitative statements are proved in Propositions
4.3
and
4.5
(far-away region) and in Proposition
5.5
(trapped region).
Since Schwarzschild corresponds to the Kerr parameter
a=0
, these statements also apply, without change in form, on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
.
For the reader’s convenience we record them here in theorem form.

Theorem 1.3

(Far-away region: scalar decay with forcing and tame Maxwell–Higgs source bounds)
.

Fix Kerr parameters
M>0
and
a
with
|a|<M
(this includes Schwarzschild
a=0
).
Let
0<\delta<1
and
k\geq 2
.
Let
\psi
be a smooth solution of

\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{D}_{M,a}.

Then there exists
R\gg 1
(depending only on
M
and
a
) such that for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,

\displaystyle E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2},

(1.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{-1-\delta}\,|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\psi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}.

(1.16)

Here
E^{N}_{k}
is the commuted
N
–energy, and

\mathcal{B}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau):=\sup_{p\in[0,2]}\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau),

where
\mathcal{R}_{k,p}
is the far-field
r^{p}
bulk/flux functional in Lemma
1.7
;
the admissible source norm
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is as in Definition
1.4
.

Moreover, let
(A,\phi)
be a Lorenz-gauge solution of the Maxwell–Higgs system on a slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
.
The nonlinearities are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}

\displaystyle:=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+i(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,\phi+\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(1.17)

\displaystyle J_{\nu}

\displaystyle:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\cdot\bar{\phi}-\phi\cdot\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr),\qquad D:=\nabla+iA.

(1.18)

In Lorenz gauge,
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
.

For integers
m\geq 0
define the coupled source-weighted energy functionals on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\mathcal{E}_{N,m}^{(\mathrm{A})}[A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\mathcal{E}_{N,m}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}),

(1.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}r^{1+\delta}\Bigl(|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}A|^{2}+|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}A|^{2}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}r^{1+\delta}\Bigl(|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}+|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}\Bigr).

(1.20)

For convenience set, for
E_{A},E_{H}\geq 0
,

\mathcal{P}_{k,P}(E_{A},E_{H}):=E_{A}+E_{A}^{2}+E_{H}+E_{A}E_{H}+\bigl(1+E_{H}^{N_{P}}\bigr)^{2}E_{H}^{2}.

(1.21)

Then, for every integer
k\geq 6
, the Maxwell–Higgs nonlinearities satisfy the tame source bounds

\displaystyle\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau),

(1.22)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,P}\mathcal{P}_{k,P}\!\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau),\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr).

(1.23)

Theorem 1.4

(Far-away region: Maxwell energy decay with current)
.

Fix Kerr parameters
M>0
and
a
with
|a|<M
(this includes Schwarzschild
a=0
).
Let
0<\delta<1
and
k\geq 2
.
Let
G
be a smooth charge-free Maxwell field solving

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0,\qquad\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0,

(1.24)

on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
.
Then there exists
R\gg 1
such that for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
one has

E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}.

(1.25)

Theorem 1.5

(Trapped/compact region: integrated local energy decay and tame Maxwell–Higgs source bounds)
.

Fix Kerr parameters
M>0
and
a
with
|a|<M
(this includes Schwarzschild
a=0
).
Fix
k\geq 0
and let
\psi
solve the inhomogeneous wave equation

\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{D}_{M,a}.

Then for every
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{K}_{R}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}w_{\mathrm{trap}}\;\mathfrak{e}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi]\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau

(1.26)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,R}E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}.

Here
\mathcal{K}_{R}
denotes a fixed compact region containing the trapped set (for
R
sufficiently large one may take
\mathcal{K}_{R}=\{r\leq R\}
),
and
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
is a standard trapping weight degenerating only on the photon region (in Schwarzschild at
r=3M
).

An analogous statement holds for charge-free Maxwell fields
G
solving
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
,
with
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}
in place of
\mathfrak{e}
and
\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
in place of
\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
.

Assume in addition that
k\geq 6
and let
(A,\phi)
be a Lorenz-gauge Maxwell–Higgs solution on the slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
With
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
and
J
as in (
1.17
)–(
1.18
), the nonlinearities satisfy the tame bounds
(
5.8
)–(
5.9
) (equivalently, (
1.22
)–(
1.23
) with
\tau
replaced by
\tau_{1}
),
in terms of the coupled energy functionals
\mathbf{E}
and
\mathbf{F}
.

Remark 1.5

(Schwarzschild specialization)
.

All estimates above hold, without change in form, on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
(i.e.
a=0
).
In the Schwarzschild part of the paper (Sections
7
–
10
) we give a self-contained proof of the corresponding linear package and derive explicit pointwise decay rates.
In particular, in Schwarzschild the trapped set is located at the photon sphere
r=3M
, and the trapping weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
degenerates only there.

1.7
Gauge invariance and structure of the Kerr scattering map

The proofs in this paper are carried out in Lorenz gauge at the level of the potential
A
.
However, the Maxwell–Higgs system is gauge invariant and the asymptotic data are defined intrinsically via parallel transport.
In particular, the nonlinear scattering theory obtained above is canonical on the quotient by gauge.
We record three structural consequences which sharpen the conceptual content of Theorem
1.1
.

Definition 1.2

(Gauge group and quotient phase space)
.

Let
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
be a Kerr-star Cauchy hypersurface and let
\chi:\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
be a real-valued function.
At the level of Cauchy data for a Lorenz-gauge potential
A
and scalar
\phi
we define the induced gauge transformation by

(A,\nabla_{n}A,\phi,D_{n}\phi)\longmapsto\bigl(A+\mathrm{d}\chi,\ \nabla_{n}A+\mathrm{d}(n\chi),\ e^{i\chi}\phi,\ e^{i\chi}(D_{n}\phi+i(n\chi)\phi)\bigr),

where
n
is the future unit normal to
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
the collection of such
\chi
for which the transformed data remain in the completed Kerr data space
\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
of Definition
2.1
, and we write

\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})/\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})

for the corresponding quotient phase space.

Corollary 1.6

(Gauge-invariant wave operators and scattering on Kerr)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.1
, the homeomorphisms
\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
, the wave operators
\mathscr{W}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{full}}
, and the two-sided nonlinear scattering operator
\mathscr{S}_{M,a}^{\mathrm{nl}}
descend to well-defined homeomorphisms on the quotient phase spaces

\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})/\mathcal{G}^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}}),

and are therefore independent of the choice of Lorenz-gauge representative.

Remark 1.6

(Quadratic remainder and tangency)
.

A general quadratic remainder estimate for the nonlinear scattering maps, implying the Fréchet differentiability statements in Theorem
1.1
(v) (and in Theorem
1.8
), is proved in Proposition
1.10
; see in particular (
1.32
).
A sharper
second-order
expansion (Born approximation) identifying the leading bilinear contribution and giving an
O(\|U\|^{3})
remainder is proved in Proposition
1.11
; see (
1.33
). If, in addition,
P
is real analytic near
0
, then all higher-order terms can be identified and the resulting Born series converges, so the scattering maps are real analytic near the origin; see Proposition
1.12
.

Framework and Schwarzschild refinements.

Theorem
1.1
is obtained by combining the robust nonlinear argument of this paper with the available linear decay and scattering theory on Kerr.
To isolate the minimal linear inputs needed for the nonlinear wave-operator construction, we formulate below the massless linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
and its massive extension
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
, and we prove a transfer principle (Theorem
1.8
) that upgrades these linear bounds to nonlinear scattering for the Maxwell–Higgs system with potentials satisfying Assumption
1.1
.
In the Schwarzschild case
a=0
we give a self-contained verification of these packages and, moreover, we treat a broad class of smooth nonnegative gauge-invariant potentials satisfying Assumption
1.1
with
m^{2}\geq 0
,
including the massive timelike/Dollard channel; see Theorem
1.14
.

1.8
A linear-package framework and applications to Kerr

A black-box linear estimate package.

The nonlinear part of our argument is robust and uses the background geometry only through a finite list of
linear
estimates for inhomogeneous scalar waves and Maxwell fields.
We isolate these inputs as the massless package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
below, and we also record the corresponding massive variant
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
(Definition
1.5
) for the Klein–Gordon operator
(\square_{g}-m^{2})
.
Conceptually,
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
consists of
(i) coercive redshift energy boundedness,
(ii) integrated local energy decay/Morawetz control with a controlled degeneration at trapping,
(iii) an
r^{p}
hierarchy in the asymptotically flat region yielding radiation fields, and
(iv) linear scattering/final-state well-posedness.
The Schwarzschild sections of this paper provide a self-contained proof of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
(and more), while on Kerr the package is available by the definitive linear decay and scattering literature.

Definition 1.3

(Admissible stationary black-hole exterior)
.

An
admissible stationary black-hole exterior
consists of an asymptotically flat stationary black-hole exterior
(\mathcal{D},g)
together with an
admissible structure

\bigl(\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}},\,\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),\,r,\,N,\,\mathcal{Z}\bigr)

satisfying the following properties.

(G1)

Foliation and slabs.

\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
is a smooth spacelike foliation of
\mathcal{D}
by Cauchy hypersurfaces.
For
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
, the corresponding slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
has boundary components

\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
,
\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}
, and the portions of the event horizons and null infinities

\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

(G2)

Asymptotic radius and stationarity.

In the asymptotically flat region there is an area radius function
r
and a complete stationary Killing field
T

which is timelike for
r
sufficiently large.

(G3)

Redshift multiplier.

There exists a globally timelike future-directed multiplier field
N
which agrees with
T
for
r
sufficiently large
and is a redshift vector field in a neighbourhood of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(and, by time-reversal, of
\mathcal{H}^{-}
).

(G4)

Commutations.

\mathcal{Z}
is a fixed finite collection of admissible commutation operators (first-order vector fields and, when needed,
second-order symmetry operators) used to define higher-order energies and to commute with the linear equations.

Whenever we speak of an admissible exterior we implicitly fix one such admissible structure.

Remark 1.7

(Choice of commutators for the nonlinear system)
.

The abstract package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
allows, when needed, second-order symmetry operators in the commutator family
\mathcal{Z}
(for instance the Carter operator for scalar waves on Kerr).
In the nonlinear Maxwell–Higgs argument of this paper we commute only with
first-order
vector fields (stationary symmetries, rotations, and redshift-type vector fields).
All product/Moser estimates and nonlinear commutator bookkeeping are applied only to these first-order commutations, while any higher-order symmetry operators enter solely through the
linear
bounds encoded in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
.

Let
(\mathcal{D},g)
be an admissible stationary black-hole exterior in the sense of Definition
1.3
,
and fix an admissible structure
(\{\Sigma_{\tau}\},r,N,\mathcal{Z})
.
For a complex scalar
\psi
we write the (real) stress–energy tensor

\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]:=\Re\bigl(\nabla_{\mu}\psi\,\overline{\nabla_{\nu}\psi}-\tfrac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{\alpha}\psi\,\overline{\nabla_{\alpha}\psi}\bigr),\qquad J^{X}_{\mu}[\psi]:=\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]X^{\nu},

and we define the
N
–energy flux through
\Sigma_{\tau}
by

E^{N}[\psi](\tau):=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}J^{N}[\psi]\cdot n_{\Sigma_{\tau}}.

For a
2
–form
G
we use the Maxwell stress–energy tensor

\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[G]:=G_{\mu\alpha}G_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}-\tfrac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}G_{\alpha\beta}G^{\alpha\beta},\qquad J^{X}_{\mu}[G]:=\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}[G]X^{\nu},

and we similarly define
E^{N}[G](\tau)
.
We also consider higher-order energies obtained by commuting with
\mathcal{Z}
.
Given
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}E^{N}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi](\tau),\qquad E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}E^{N}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}G](\tau),

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}
denotes a composition of admissible commutators.

Massive Klein–Gordon energies.

Fix
m^{2}\geq 0
.
For the linear Klein–Gordon operator
(\square_{g}-m^{2})
we also use the modified stress–energy tensor

\mathbb{T}^{(m)}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]:=\Re\!\left(\nabla_{\mu}\psi\,\overline{\nabla_{\nu}\psi}\right)-\tfrac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Bigl(\nabla^{\alpha}\psi\,\overline{\nabla_{\alpha}\psi}+m^{2}|\psi|^{2}\Bigr),\qquad J^{X,(m)}_{\mu}[\psi]:=\mathbb{T}^{(m)}_{\mu\nu}[\psi]X^{\nu},

and define the corresponding fluxes through
\Sigma_{\tau}
by

E^{X,m}[\psi](\tau):=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}J^{X,(m)}[\psi]\cdot n_{\Sigma_{\tau}},\qquad E^{X,m}_{k}[\psi](\tau):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}E^{X,m}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi](\tau).

When
m=0
these coincide with the wave energies
E^{X}[\psi]
and
E^{X}_{k}[\psi]
.

Definition 1.4

(Linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
(\mathcal{D},g)
be an admissible stationary black-hole exterior with admissible structure as in Definition
1.3
.
We say that
(\mathcal{D},g)
satisfies the
analytic
linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
if there exist constants
C>0
and
R>0
and, for every
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
, a pair of nonnegative functionals

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},\qquad\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},

called the
source norm
and the
solution/bulk norm
, such that the following hold.

Admissibility of the norms.

The families
\mathcal{S}_{k}
and
\mathcal{X}_{k}
satisfy the following structural properties.

(A1)

Time monotonicity.

If
[\tau_{0}^{\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime}]\subset[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
, then

\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0}^{\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime})}\leq\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},\qquad\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0}^{\prime},\tau_{1}^{\prime})}\leq\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},

and similarly for Maxwell fields and currents.

(A2)

Time localization.

For
\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}
there exists a universal constant
C_{\mathrm{loc}}
such that

\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{2})}^{2}\leq\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2},

and

\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{2})}^{2}\leq C_{\mathrm{loc}}\Bigl(\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}+\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{2}\Bigr),

with the analogous property for Maxwell fields.

(A3)

Coercivity and radiation fields.

Finiteness of
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
controls the order-
k

N
–energy fluxes through
\Sigma_{\tau}
for
\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
.
Moreover,
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
contains a far-field component sufficient to yield existence of order-
k
radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

together with quantitative control of the corresponding asymptotic energy fluxes.
The same holds for charge-free Maxwell fields.

(L1)

Inhomogeneous energy–Morawetz–
r^{p}
estimate.

Let
\psi
solve the scalar wave equation
\square_{g}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Then

\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\leq C\Bigl(E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\Bigr).

(1.27)

An analogous estimate holds for (charge-free) Maxwell fields
G
solving

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
,
\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0
, namely

\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\leq C\Bigl(E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau_{0})+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\Bigr).

(L2)

Linear scattering and final-state well-posedness.

The Cauchy-to-radiation map extends to an isomorphism between the order-
k
energy space of Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}

and a Hilbert space of linear asymptotic data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
,
and the linear final-state problem on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
is well posed with estimates compatible with (L1).

All Maxwell estimates in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
are understood for the
radiative
(charge-free) part of the field:
for general data with nonzero electric/magnetic charges one first subtracts the corresponding stationary Coulomb solution.

Definition 1.5

(Massive linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Let
(\mathcal{D},g)
be an admissible stationary black-hole exterior with admissible structure as in Definition
1.3
.
We say that
(\mathcal{D},g)
satisfies the
massive
linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
if there exist constants
C>0
and
R>0
and, for every
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
, a pair of nonnegative functionals

\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(m)}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},\qquad\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}^{(m)}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})},

called the
source norm
and the
solution/bulk norm
, such that the admissibility properties (A1)–(A3) of Definition
1.4
hold with
\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(m)}
and
\mathcal{X}_{k}^{(m)}
in place of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
and
\mathcal{X}_{k}
, and with the (possibly extended) scattering boundary
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
in place of
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
in (A3).

Moreover, the following linear estimates hold.

(Lm1)

Inhomogeneous energy–Morawetz–
r^{p}
estimate for
(\square_{g}-m^{2})
.

Let
\psi
solve the Klein–Gordon equation
(\square_{g}-m^{2})\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Then

\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}^{(m)}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\leq C\Bigl(E^{N,m}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(m)}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\Bigr).

(1.28)

An analogous estimate holds for (charge-free) Maxwell fields
G
solving

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
,
\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0
,
as in (L1) of Definition
1.4
.

(Lm2)

Linear scattering and final-state well-posedness.

The Cauchy-to-asymptotic map for the decoupled linear comparison system (charge-free Maxwell together with
(\square_{g}-m^{2})
) extends to an isomorphism between the order-
k
energy space of Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}

and the linear asymptotic data spaces
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}
on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
of Definition
10.7
,
and the corresponding linear final-state problem on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
is well posed with estimates compatible with (Lm1).

When
m=0
we may choose
\mathcal{S}_{k}^{(0)}=\mathcal{S}_{k}
and
\mathcal{X}_{k}^{(0)}=\mathcal{X}_{k}
so that
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(0)}
coincides with
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
.

Lemma 1.7

(Concrete realization of the admissible norms on Schwarzschild and Kerr)
.

On the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
and on each subextremal Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
with
|a|<M
,
one admissible choice of the norms in Definition
1.4
is given by the weighted physical-space norms below.
Fix
\delta>0
and a cutoff radius
R>0
.
For a source term
\mathfrak{F}
set

\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}:=\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}r^{1+\delta}\,|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\mathfrak{F}|^{2}.

(1.29)

There exists a smooth nonnegative weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
which vanishes only on the trapped set (the photon sphere on Schwarzschild, the photon region on Kerr).
Fix one such
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
.
For a scalar
\psi
define

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}:={}

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\leq R\}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\Bigl(w_{\mathrm{trap}}\,|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}+r^{-1-\delta}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}+r^{-3-\delta}|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\sup_{p\in[0,2]}\,\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

(1.30)

Here, for each
p\in[0,2]
,
\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
denotes the standard Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
bulk/flux functional
in the asymptotically flat region
\{r\geq R\}
.
In particular, finiteness of
\sup_{p\in[0,2]}\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[\psi]
yields existence of radiation fields and controls the corresponding weighted energy fluxes through
\mathcal{I}^{+}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

(and, by time-reversal, through
\mathcal{I}^{-}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
).

For Maxwell fields
G
with source current
J
one uses the same source norm (
1.29
) with
\mathfrak{F}
replaced by
J
,
and one defines
\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
by replacing
|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}
and
|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}
in (
1.7
)
by
|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}G|^{2}
and using the corresponding
r^{p}
functional
\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[G]
.
These choices satisfy the admissibility properties
(A1)–(A3)
.

Theorem 1.8

(Nonlinear scattering from the linear package)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and assume that the scalar potential
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
.
Let
m^{2}\geq 0
be the mass parameter in Assumption
1.1
(equivalently, in (
1.7
)).
Let
(\mathcal{D},g)
be an admissible stationary black-hole exterior in the sense of Definition
1.3
satisfying
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
in the sense of Definition
1.5
.
Assume that the admissible Lorenz-gauge initial data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
are
charge-free
(Definition
1.1
) and have sufficiently small order-
k
energy.
Then there exists
\varepsilon_{*}>0
(depending only on
k
, the fixed geometric parameters of
\mathcal{D}
in Definition
1.3
, on
N_{P}
, and on finitely many constants
C_{P,j}
in (
1.8
)) such that every such data set with energy
\leq\varepsilon_{*}
generates a unique global smooth Lorenz-gauge solution
(A,\phi)
on
\mathcal{D}
, with curvature
F=\mathrm{d}A
.
All implicit constants in the conclusions depend only on the same parameters.

The corresponding field pair
(F,\phi)
satisfies uniform higher-order energy boundedness, integrated local energy decay, and quantitative decay as encoded by the solution norm
\mathcal{X}_{k}^{(m)}
in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
.
Moreover,
(F,\phi)
admits gauge-covariant radiation fields on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
and scatters to the unique solution of the linearised comparison system determined by its asymptotic data.
In particular, the forward and backward nonlinear scattering maps and nonlinear wave operators are well-defined and yield small-data asymptotic completeness.
Equivalently, the forward/backward nonlinear scattering maps and nonlinear wave operators are well-defined and are homeomorphisms between neighbourhoods of
0
in the completed (charge-free) Cauchy data space and in the corresponding linear asymptotic data spaces.

Moreover, the scattering maps depend only on the gauge-equivalence class of the initial data and hence descend to the quotient by the residual gauge group.
Since the nonlinear terms vanish to second order at
(F,\phi)=(0,0)
, the resulting scattering maps are Fréchet differentiable at the trivial solution, and their differentials coincide with the corresponding linear scattering maps.

Moreover, the scattering maps admit a quadratic expansion at the origin: there exist continuous symmetric bilinear maps

\mathscr{B}_{\pm}:\ \mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\times\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}

such that for all sufficiently small Cauchy data
U
,

\bigl\|\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)-\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}(U)-\mathscr{B}_{\pm}(U,U)\bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}\lesssim\|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{3}.

In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
are twice Fréchet differentiable at
0
.

If, in addition, the potential
P
is real analytic near
\phi=0
, then the scattering maps and wave operators are real analytic near
0
and admit an absolutely convergent Born series expansion in the small data; see Proposition
1.12
.

Remark 1.8
.

The proof of Theorem
1.8
is carried out in the Schwarzschild model
a=0
in Sections
7
–
10.6
, where we keep all commutator computations explicit.
Each step uses only the robust mechanisms encoded in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
; once
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
is available on a given background, the same bootstrap and final-state argument applies.

Tame estimates for the nonlinear potential term.

We record for later use a quantitative commutator estimate for the nonlinear remainder
\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
in (
1.13
). This is the only point where the coefficients of the scalar potential enter the nonlinear error analysis.

Lemma 1.9

(Tame commutator bounds for the potential force)
.

Assume that
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
and let
\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
be defined by (
1.13
).
Fix an integer
k\geq 0
and let
\mathcal{Z}
be any collection of
first-order
admissible commutators (for instance, the families used in the Kerr and Schwarzschild parts of this paper).
Then there exists a constant
C=C(k,N_{P},\{C_{P,j}\}_{0\leq j\leq k})
such that for every smooth
\phi
and every hypersurface
\Sigma
in the admissible foliation,

\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\leq C\,(1+\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}^{2N_{P}})\,\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}^{2}\,\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

(1.31)

The same bound holds with
L^{2}(\Sigma)
replaced by
L^{2}(\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}))
and
L^{\infty}(\Sigma)
replaced by
L^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}))
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)=Q(|\phi|^{2})\,|\phi|^{2}\phi
and set
s:=|\phi|^{2}
.
By Leibniz, each commuted term
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
is a finite sum of products obtained by distributing the derivatives among the three factors
\phi
,
\bar{\phi}
, and
Q(s)
.
We estimate each such product by placing exactly one factor in
L^{2}
and all remaining factors in
L^{\infty}
; after summing over
|\alpha|\leq k
this yields a tame/Moser-type estimate.

It therefore suffices to bound derivatives of the composite coefficient
Q(s)
.
By repeated application of the chain rule, for each multi-index
\beta
the term
\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}(Q(s))
is a finite linear combination of expressions of the form

Q^{(p)}(s)\,\prod_{j=1}^{p}\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma_{j}}s,\qquad 1\leq p\leq|\beta|,\qquad|\gamma_{1}|+\cdots+|\gamma_{p}|=|\beta|.

Since
s=\phi\bar{\phi}
, each
\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}s
is a finite sum of products of two factors drawn from
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\delta}\phi,\ \mathcal{Z}^{\delta}\bar{\phi}\}
.
Using (
1.8
) and the pointwise bound
s^{N_{P}}\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2N_{P}}
, we obtain the uniform estimate

\|\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}(Q(s))\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\leq C\,(1+\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}^{2N_{P}})\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta_{1}+\cdots+\delta_{m}=\beta\\
m\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\delta_{j}}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)},

where
C
depends only on
k,N_{P}
and finitely many constants
C_{P,j}
.

Inserting this into the Leibniz expansion for
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
and estimating all but one factor in
L^{\infty}
yields (
1.31
).
(The spacetime version is identical.)
∎

Remark 1.9

(Energy-only form of the tame bound)
.

Throughout this paper we work with
k\geq 6
, so the uniform Sobolev/Moser estimates on the hypersurfaces in the admissible foliation (see Lemma
5.1
on Kerr and Lemma
A.1
on Schwarzschild) control the
L^{\infty}
norms of
\phi
and of a finite number of its commuted derivatives in terms of the order-
k
energy.
Consequently, the right-hand side of (
1.31
) is a polynomial function of the bootstrap energy, and no separate
a priori
pointwise restriction on
|\phi|
is assumed.

Proposition 1.10

(Quadratic scattering remainder and differentiability at the origin)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and, for simplicity of notation, consider the charge–free sector after Coulomb subtraction.
Let
U
be sufficiently small admissible Cauchy data in the order–
k
energy space on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
, and let
(F,\phi)
be the corresponding nonlinear solution.
Let
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
be the solution of the linear comparison system with the
same
initial data
U
.
Then the difference
(F-F^{\mathrm{lin}},\,\phi-\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
satisfies the quadratic estimate

\bigl\|\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)-\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}(U)\bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}\ \lesssim\ \|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{2},

(1.32)

where
\mathcal{H}^{k}
denotes the relevant (renormalised) Cauchy data norm.
In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
are Fréchet differentiable at
0
with

D\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(0)=\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}
.

Proof.

Subtracting the linearised comparison system from the nonlinear Maxwell–Higgs equations yields an inhomogeneous linear system for
(F-F^{\mathrm{lin}},\,\phi-\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
whose source terms are at least quadratic in
(F,\phi)
.
Applying the inhomogeneous estimates in the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
and using the small-data bounds on
(F,\phi)
from the nonlinear bootstrap gives

\|(F-F^{\mathrm{lin}},\,\phi-\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\lesssim\|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{2}

uniformly in
\tau_{1}
.
Passing to the limit
\tau_{1}\to\pm\infty
in the
r^{p}
hierarchy and using the definition of the asymptotic data norms yields (
1.32
).
The Fréchet differentiability at
0
follows by evaluating (
1.32
) on rescaled data
\lambda U
and dividing by
\lambda
.
∎

Proposition 1.11

(Second-order expansion of the scattering map (Born approximation))
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and work in the charge–free sector after Coulomb subtraction.
There exists a continuous symmetric bilinear map
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}:\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\times\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})\to\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}
with the following properties.
For
U_{1},U_{2}
in the Cauchy data space let
(F_{j}^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi_{j}^{\mathrm{lin}})
denote the corresponding linear comparison solutions.
Let
(F_{12}^{(2)},\varphi_{12}^{(2)})
be the unique solution of the
inhomogeneous
linear comparison system with zero initial data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
and source equal to the quadratic part of the Maxwell–Higgs nonlinearity bilinearised at
(0,0)
and evaluated on
(F_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi_{1}^{\mathrm{lin}})
and
(F_{2}^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi_{2}^{\mathrm{lin}})
.
Then
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}(U_{1},U_{2})
is precisely the asymptotic data of
(F_{12}^{(2)},\varphi_{12}^{(2)})
on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.
Moreover, for all sufficiently small
U
one has the cubic remainder estimate

\bigl\|\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)-\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}(U)-\mathscr{B}_{\pm}(U,U)\bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}\ \lesssim\ \|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{3}.

(1.33)

In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
are twice Fréchet differentiable at
0
with

D^{2}\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(0)[U_{1},U_{2}]=2\,\mathscr{B}_{\pm}(U_{1},U_{2})
.

Proof.

Write the Lorenz-reduced Maxwell–Higgs system schematically as
\mathcal{L}u=\mathcal{N}(u)
, where
u
collects the unknowns
(F,\phi)
,
\mathcal{L}
is the linear comparison operator, and
\mathcal{N}
is the nonlinearity.
Since the Lorenz-reduced Maxwell–Higgs system is semilinear with smooth nonlinearities, the map
\mathcal{N}
is
C^{\infty}
between the Banach spaces encoded by
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
.
Moreover
\mathcal{N}(0)=0
and
D\mathcal{N}(0)=0
: the gauge-coupling terms are at least quadratic, and the nonlinear remainder of the potential force
\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)
in (
1.13
) is at least cubic by (
1.14
) (the linear term
m^{2}\phi
is included in the linear comparison operator).
Define the symmetric bilinear form
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}:=\tfrac{1}{2}D^{2}\mathcal{N}(0)
.
By the Banach-space Taylor theorem with integral remainder (apply the fundamental theorem of calculus to
s\mapsto\mathcal{N}(su)
) we have, for
u
in a sufficiently small neighbourhood of
0
,

\mathcal{N}(u)=\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(u,u)+\mathcal{R}^{(3)}(u),\qquad\mathcal{R}^{(3)}(u)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{(1-s)^{2}}{2}\,D^{3}\mathcal{N}(su)[u,u,u]\,\mathrm{d}s.

(1.34)

In particular,

\|\mathcal{R}^{(3)}(u)\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}}^{3},

with an implicit constant depending only on uniform bounds for
D^{3}\mathcal{N}
on that neighbourhood.

Let
u^{\mathrm{lin}}
be the linear comparison solution with initial data
U
and let
u^{(2)}
be the solution of the inhomogeneous linear comparison system with zero initial data and source
\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(u^{\mathrm{lin}},u^{\mathrm{lin}})
.
Define the second-order approximation
u^{\mathrm{app}}:=u^{\mathrm{lin}}+u^{(2)}
and set
w:=u-u^{\mathrm{app}}
.
Then
w
solves an inhomogeneous linear comparison system with zero initial data and source

\mathcal{N}(u)-\mathcal{N}^{(2)}(u^{\mathrm{lin}},u^{\mathrm{lin}}),

which can be written as a sum of terms that are at least cubic in
u^{\mathrm{lin}}
plus terms containing
w
with coefficients controlled by the small solution
u
.
Using the small-data bounds for
u
and the inhomogeneous estimate in the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
, one obtains

\|w\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\lesssim\|U\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}^{3}
uniformly in
\tau_{1}
.
Passing to radiation fields in the
r^{p}
hierarchy and using the definition of the asymptotic data norms yields (
1.33
).
Finally, the identification of
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}
with the asymptotic data of
u^{(2)}
follows from the construction of
u^{(2)}
.
∎

Proposition 1.12

(Real-analyticity and a convergent Born series for the scattering map)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.8
and assume moreover that the potential
P
(equivalently
\widetilde{P}
) is real analytic in a neighborhood of
\phi=0
.
Then there exist continuous symmetric
n
–linear maps

\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(n)}:\ (\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}))^{n}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)},\qquad n\geq 1,

with
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(1)}=\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(2)}=\mathscr{B}_{\pm}
from Proposition
1.11
, such that for all sufficiently small Cauchy data
U
the series

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(n)}(U,\dots,U)

(1.35)

converges absolutely in
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}
and equals
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)
.
Moreover, there exist constants
C_{0},C_{1}>0
such that for all
n\geq 1
and all
U_{1},\dots,U_{n}\in\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
,

\bigl\|\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(n)}(U_{1},\dots,U_{n})\bigr\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}\leq C_{0}\,(C_{1})^{n}\,\prod_{j=1}^{n}\|U_{j}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}.

(1.36)

In particular,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
is real analytic near
0
.

Proof.

We sketch a standard analytic-implicit-function argument in the Banach spaces provided by the linear package.
Let
X
be the complete solution space on
\mathcal{D}
with norm
\|\cdot\|_{X}:=\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},+\infty)}
(or
\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},-\infty)
for the past problem), and let
Y:=\mathcal{H}^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
be the Cauchy data space.
By
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
, the linear comparison dynamics defines a bounded linear map

\mathcal{U}:Y\to X,\qquad\|\mathcal{U}(U)\|_{X}\lesssim\|U\|_{Y},

and the inhomogeneous linear solver with zero data defines a bounded linear map

\mathcal{T}:\mathcal{S}_{k}\to X,\qquad\|\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{F})\|_{X}\lesssim\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}}.

Write the Lorenz-reduced Maxwell–Higgs system schematically as
\mathcal{L}u=\mathcal{N}(u)
, where
u
collects the unknowns and
\mathcal{L}
is the linear comparison operator.
The nonlinearity
\mathcal{N}
vanishes to second order at
u=0
and, under the real-analyticity assumption on
P
, it is real analytic as a map
u\mapsto\mathcal{N}(u)
in a neighborhood of
0
in
X
, with values in the source space
\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
Define

\mathcal{F}(u,U):=u-\mathcal{U}(U)-\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{N}(u)),

a map from a neighborhood of
(0,0)
in
X\times Y
into
X
.
Since
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{T}
are bounded linear and
\mathcal{N}
is analytic,
\mathcal{F}
is real analytic.
Moreover,

\partial_{u}\mathcal{F}(0,0)=\mathrm{Id}_{X},

which is invertible.
By the analytic implicit function theorem in Banach spaces, there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a unique real-analytic map
U\mapsto u(U)
from
\{U\in Y:\|U\|_{Y}<\varepsilon\}
into
X
such that
\mathcal{F}(u(U),U)=0
, i.e.
u(U)
is the unique small global solution.

The nonlinear scattering map is obtained by applying the (bounded linear) asymptotic-data extraction map

\mathscr{A}_{\pm}:X\to\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}

(which is part of the
r^{p}
hierarchy and linear scattering theory contained in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
).
Therefore,

\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(U)=\mathscr{A}_{\pm}(u(U))

is real analytic as a composition of analytic and bounded linear maps.
Consequently it admits a convergent power-series expansion at the origin with symmetric multilinear coefficients
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(n)}
.
The multilinear bound (
1.36
) follows from Cauchy estimates for analytic maps (equivalently, from the majorant-series bounds arising from iterating the fixed point equation).
Finally,
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(1)}=\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and
\mathscr{B}_{\pm}^{(2)}=\mathscr{B}_{\pm}
agree with the linear and bilinear coefficients identified in Propositions
1.10
and
1.11
.
∎

Corollary 1.13

(Analyticity of the wave operators)
.

In the setting of Proposition
1.12
, the forward/backward wave operators
\mathscr{W}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}=(\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}})^{-1}
and the two-sided nonlinear scattering operator
\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{nl}}
are real analytic near the origin (on the gauge quotient as well).

Proof.

By Proposition
1.12
,
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
is analytic near
0
and satisfies
D\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}(0)=\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, which is an isomorphism.
The analytic inverse function theorem therefore yields an analytic inverse map in a (possibly smaller) neighborhood of
0
, i.e. analytic wave operators.
∎

Schwarzschild model: nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness.

We now record the detailed Schwarzschild scattering theorem, which is proved in Sections
7
–
10.6
and allows general masses
m^{2}\geq 0
.
In the massive case the scalar component exhibits an additional timelike/Dollard channel.

Theorem 1.14

(Nonlinear wave operators and small-data asymptotic completeness)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and assume that the scalar potential
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
.
Let
m^{2}\geq 0
be as in Assumption
1.1
(equivalently, in (
1.7
)).
There exists
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}>0
(depending on
M,k
, on
N_{P}
, and on finitely many constants
C_{P,j}
in (
1.8
)) such that the following hold.
All implicit constants depend only on
M,k
and on
P
through these structural bounds.

(i)

(
Forward wave operator.
)
For every
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{+}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})
there exists a unique global charge–free solution
(F,\phi)
of the Maxwell–Higgs system admitting a Lorenz-gauge potential
A
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
such that

\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}(F,\phi)=\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}.

Moreover, if
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
is the unique linear comparison solution corresponding to
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}
, then
(F,\phi)
scatters to
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
on
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
in the sense of Theorem
10.13
.

(ii)

(
Backward wave operator.
)
For every
\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{-}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})
there exists a unique global charge–free solution
(F,\phi)
such that
\mathscr{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{full}}(F,\phi)=\mathcal{R}_{-}^{\mathrm{lin}}
and which scatters to the corresponding linear comparison solution on
\partial_{-}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.

(iii)

(
Homeomorphism property.
)
The maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
restrict to homeomorphisms between a neighbourhood of
0
in the nonlinear Cauchy data space and the neighbourhoods
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})
.
Their inverses

\mathscr{W}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}:=(\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}})^{-1}

are the (nonlinear) wave operators.

Radiation fields and nonlinear scattering.

The scattering theory is summarized by Theorem
1.14
and is proved in Section
10.6
.
In particular, we first construct radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(Theorems
10.4
and
10.6
), we identify the corresponding linear scattering theory for the linearized comparison system (Theorem
10.7
), and we then solve the nonlinear final-state problem to show that every small nonlinear solution scatters to a unique linear solution (Theorem
10.13
).
When
m^{2}>0
, the scalar scattering description includes a timelike channel and a Dollard-type phase correction; see Corollary
10.14
and Section
10.6
.

Consequences for the Schwarzschild Cauchy problem.

As part of the proof of Theorem
1.14
we establish robust boundedness and decay estimates for the Cauchy evolution from small admissible Lorenz-gauge data.
For the reader primarily interested in the forward Cauchy problem and quantitative decay, we record the following consequences.

Schwarzschild double-null notation.

We now specialize to the Schwarzschild model
a=0
and use the standard Schwarzschild time coordinate
t
and Regge–Wheeler tortoise coordinate
r^{\ast}
.
We introduce Eddington–Finkelstein null coordinates

v=t+r^{\ast},\qquad w=t-r^{\ast},

(1.37)

and we set
v_{+}:=\max\{1,v\}
and
w_{+}:=\max\{1,|w|\}
.
Our estimates split naturally into a far region
r\geq R
(for
R
sufficiently large) and the region
2M<r\leq 3M
capturing trapping and near-horizon effects.

Schwarzschild energies.

Let
\Sigma_{t}=\{t=\mathrm{const}\}
with future unit normal
n_{\Sigma_{t}}
, and let
T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]
denote the Maxwell–Higgs energy–momentum tensor associated to (
1.4
).
For a vector field
X
, define the energy flux

E^{X}[F,\phi](t):=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,X^{\nu}\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}}.

(1.38)

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}
be the rotation fields on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and set

\mathcal{Z}:=\{\partial_{t},\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}\}
.
For a multi-index
\alpha
we write
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}
for a product of these vector fields.
For an integer
k\geq 6
, we define the higher-order energy

\mathcal{E}_{k}(t):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}E^{\hat{t}}\!\big[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F,\;\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi\big](t),

(1.39)

where
\hat{t}
denotes the unit timelike vector field in the standard Schwarzschild orthonormal frame (see Section
7.1
).

Corollary 1.15

(Small-data global existence and energy bounds)
.

Assume that the scalar potential
P
satisfies Assumption
