1.1
.
Fix an integer
k\geq 6
.
There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if the initial data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
are admissible in Lorenz gauge (Section
7.2
), are charge-free in the sense of Definition
1.1
, and satisfy

\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0},

(1.40)

then the Maxwell–Higgs system admits a unique global smooth solution
(F,\phi)\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
.
Moreover, the commuted energies remain uniformly bounded:

\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

and the solution satisfies the integrated local energy decay and pointwise decay estimates stated in Corollaries
1.16
and
1.17
.

Corollary 1.16

(Pointwise decay in the far region)
.

Assume that
P
satisfies Assumption
1.1
.
Let
(F,\phi)
be a global charge-free solution arising from data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
with
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
k\geq 6
.
Then there exist
R\gg 1
and a constant
C=C(M,k,P)
such that for all
r\geq R
,

\displaystyle|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}(w,v,\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{1+v_{+}},

(1.41)

\displaystyle|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}(w,v,\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{1+|w|},

(1.42)

\displaystyle|\phi(w,v,\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{1+v_{+}},

(1.43)

\displaystyle|D\phi(w,v,\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{1+v_{+}}.

(1.44)

Corollary 1.17

(Pointwise decay near trapping and the horizon)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global solution arising from sufficiently small charge-free data satisfying (
1.40
) with
k\geq 6
.
Set
v_{+}:=\max\{1,v\}
and
\mathcal{E}:=\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
.
Then in the region
2M<r\leq 3M
the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)\mathcal{E}^{1/2},

(1.45)

\displaystyle|\phi|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(1+\left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\right)^{1/2}\mathcal{E}^{1/2},

(1.46)

\displaystyle|D\phi|

\displaystyle\lesssim E_{4}^{1/2},

(1.47)

where

E_{4}\equiv\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{\,2n-1}+\displaystyle\sum_{n=2}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{\,2n-2}+\displaystyle\sum_{n=3}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{\,2n-3}+\mathcal{E},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{V4}},\\[8.00003pt]
\left(\dfrac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{E},&\text{if $P$ is \eqref{sine} or \eqref{toda}}.\end{cases}

(1.48)

In particular, along outgoing null hypersurfaces of fixed
w
, these estimates yield decay as
v\to+\infty
.

Remark 1.10
.

Since
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}\ll 1
, the quantity
E_{4}
in Corollary
1.17
satisfies the schematic bound

E_{4}\ \lesssim\ \Bigl(1+\bigl(\tfrac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

and hence
|D\phi|\lesssim\bigl(1+\tfrac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\bigr)\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}
in
2M<r\leq 3M
.
We keep the more detailed expression for
E_{4}
only to record the dependence on the potential when tracking nonlinear terms in the bootstrap argument.

Corollary 1.18

(General small data and Coulomb asymptotics)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global solution arising from sufficiently small admissible data in Lorenz gauge, without assuming the charge-free condition of Definition
1.1
.
Let
Q_{e},Q_{m}
denote the (conserved) asymptotic electric and magnetic charges determined by the initial data, and let
F_{Q}
be the corresponding stationary Coulomb
2
–form on Schwarzschild.
Then the radiative remainder

\widetilde{F}:=F-F_{Q}

is charge-free and satisfies the same pointwise decay estimates as in Corollaries
1.16
and
1.17
(with
F
replaced by
\widetilde{F}
).
In particular,
F
converges pointwise to
F_{Q}
as
v\to+\infty
along outgoing null hypersurfaces.

Proof.

The Coulomb field
F_{Q}
is a stationary sourceless Maxwell solution on Schwarzschild with charges
(Q_{e},Q_{m})
.
Since the Maxwell equation is linear in
F
, the remainder
\widetilde{F}=F-F_{Q}
satisfies the same Maxwell equation with source
J(\phi)
and has vanishing asymptotic charges.
Applying Corollaries
1.16
and
1.17
to
(\widetilde{F},\phi)
yields the stated decay for
\widetilde{F}
.
∎

1.9
Roadmap of the proof

The proof is organised in two layers.
First, one verifies the appropriate black-box linear estimate package for the decoupled comparison system: the massless package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
for
(\square_{g}\psi=\mathfrak{F})
(Definition
1.4
) when
m=0
, and, when
m^{2}>0
, its massive analogue
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
for
(\square_{g}-m^{2})\psi=\mathfrak{F}
(Definition
1.5
).
Second, one runs a robust nonlinear bootstrap together with a nonlinear
final-state
construction; this step uses the background only through the corresponding linear package.
In Schwarzschild we give a self-contained verification of these packages (including the massive/timelike-channel refinements when
m^{2}>0
) and keep all commutator computations explicit, while on Kerr the massless verification is recorded in Section
6
and the massive case is conditional (Remark
1.4
).
All arguments are carried out in physical space and are compatible with the presence of the event horizon and the trapped set.
The main steps are:

•

Gauge fixing, constraint propagation, and Coulomb renormalization.

We work in Lorenz gauge and impose admissibility conditions on the initial data that ensure propagation of the constraints (Section
2
for Kerr and Section
7.2
in the Schwarzschild model).
We focus on the radiative (charge-free) sector, in which the Maxwell field admits a global potential and the scattering boundary data are purely radiative. Stationary Coulomb modes are separated off at the linear level (Remark
2.1
) and serve as canonical reference states.

•

Energy identities and commutation.

Using the gauge-invariant stress–energy tensor we derive energy flux identities for the stationary Killing field
T
(and the redshift multiplier
N
) and for commuted fields (Section
3
on Kerr and Section
8.1
in the Schwarzschild model).

•

Redshift and Morawetz across the horizon and the photon sphere.

A redshift multiplier yields non-degenerate control up to
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, while a Morawetz multiplier gives integrated local energy decay through the trapped region (the photon region; in Schwarzschild this is located at
r=3M
) (Section
8.3
).

•

r^{p}
hierarchies and decay towards null infinity.

In the region
r\geq R
we use an
r^{p}
hierarchy to obtain decay of energy fluxes and control of the radiative field along outgoing null hypersurfaces (Section
9
).

•

Gauge-covariant radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
.

Decay and integrated estimates imply the existence of radiation fields.
We define them intrinsically via parallel transport along null generators, which produces gauge-covariant asymptotic data suitable for scattering.

•

Final-state problem and wave operators.

We solve the nonlinear final-state problem by a contraction argument around the linear comparison dynamics, using the decay estimates to control the nonlinear source terms.
This yields nonlinear scattering, wave operators, and (when
m^{2}>0
) the additional timelike/Dollard channel (Section
10.6
).

1.10
Organization of the paper

Sections
2
–
5
prepare the Kerr setting for the nonlinear scattering and wave-operator statement of Theorem
1.1
.
Section
2
fixes the Lorenz gauge and introduces the admissible Kerr Cauchy data spaces on Kerr–star slices.
Section
3
records the stress–energy currents, the associated energy identities on Kerr, and formulates the redshift, Morawetz, and
r^{p}
integrated decay estimates in the abstract form of the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
.
Section
4
records the far-region decay consequences of the
r^{p}
hierarchy for
inhomogeneous
scalar waves and for charge-free Maxwell fields with current.
The main quantitative statements are Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
, proved in Propositions
4.3
and
4.5
; for convenience they are stated in Subsubsection
1.6
. From these, the far-region pointwise decay corollaries follow by Sobolev on
S_{\tau,r}
.
Section
5
complements this with decay in the trapped and near-horizon region, where redshift and trapping must be combined; the key compact-region input is the trapped-set integrated decay estimate of Theorem
1.5
, proved as Proposition
5.5
(see also Subsubsection
1.6
).
Since Schwarzschild corresponds to the Kerr parameter
a=0
, the regional decay statements of Theorems
1.3
,
1.4
, and
1.5
(proved on Kerr as Propositions
4.3
,
4.5
, and
5.5
) also apply verbatim on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
; the Schwarzschild part of the paper provides a self-contained proof and derives the explicit pointwise decay estimates of Corollaries
1.16
and
1.17
.
Section
6
then verifies
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
on the full subextremal Kerr family
|a|<M
by appealing to the available linear decay and scattering theory for scalar waves and Maxwell fields.

The remainder of the paper specialises to the Schwarzschild model
a=0
, where we keep all computations explicit and give a self-contained proof of the decay and scattering estimates underlying the framework theorem.
Section
7
fixes the gauge, collects geometric conventions, and specifies the class of admissible initial data.
Section
8
introduces the energy functionals and proves the basic energy identities, including redshift and Morawetz estimates.
Section
9
derives pointwise decay in the far region
r\geq R
.
Section
10
obtains decay estimates in the region
2M<r\leq 3M
, where trapping and redshift effects must be combined.
Section
10.6
constructs radiation fields, identifies the linear comparison scattering theory, and solves the nonlinear final-state problem, yielding nonlinear scattering and wave operators (including the massive Schwarzschild extension).

1.11
Proof map for the main results

For the convenience of the reader and referee we record the logical dependencies of the main theorems.

•

The Kerr scattering theorem (Theorem
1.1
) is obtained by applying the abstract nonlinear transfer principle (Theorem
1.8
) on the Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
. In the massless case
m=0
this uses the verification of the massless linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}=\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(0)}
on subextremal Kerr (Proposition
6.1
) together with the Kerr gauge/data setup of Section
2
. For
m^{2}>0
the same implication holds under the explicit hypothesis that Kerr satisfies the massive package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
(Remark
1.4
).

•

The abstract framework theorem (Theorem
1.8
) is proved in the Schwarzschild model in Sections
7
–
10.6
.
The proof is written so as to use only the axioms of the corresponding linear package (
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
when
m=0
, and
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
when
m^{2}>0
) and the gauge structure of the Maxwell–Higgs system; once the relevant package is verified on a background, the same nonlinear bootstrap and final-state argument applies.

•

The Schwarzschild part of the paper provides a self-contained proof of the massless linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
for scalar waves and charge-free Maxwell fields and, in addition, verifies the massive refinements needed for
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}^{(m)}
when
m^{2}>0
(including the timelike/Dollard channel), leading to the stronger Schwarzschild scattering statement recorded in Theorem
1.14
.

We also emphasize the division between
linear inputs
and the
nonlinear mechanism
.
On Kerr, we treat the linear package as a black box coming from the literature (summarized in Section
6
);
on Schwarzschild we provide the detailed physical-space derivation, which also serves as a transparent blueprint for other backgrounds once the corresponding linear estimates are available.

2
Gauge fixing and initial data on Kerr

In this section we describe the gauge fixing and the admissible Cauchy data on subextremal Kerr exteriors that underlie the nonlinear scattering and wave-operator statement of Theorem
1.1
.
Throughout
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
denotes the Kerr domain of outer communications introduced in Section
1
, equipped with the Kerr–star foliation

\Sigma_{\tau}=\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
from (
1.3
).
We write
n=n_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
for the future unit normal to
\Sigma_{\tau}
and
\gamma
for the induced Riemannian metric.

2.1
Gauge symmetry and Lorenz gauge

The Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) is invariant under the
U(1)
gauge symmetry

(A,\phi)\longmapsto(A+\mathrm{d}\chi,\;e^{i\chi}\phi),

(2.1)

for any smooth real-valued function
\chi
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
.
The curvature
2
–form
F=\mathrm{d}A
is gauge invariant and the covariant derivative transforms as

D_{\mu}(e^{i\chi}\phi)=e^{i\chi}(D_{\mu}\phi)
.

For the nonlinear analysis we work in the Lorenz gauge

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0.

(2.2)

Since Kerr is Ricci-flat, (
2.2
) reduces the Maxwell equation to a covariant wave equation for the potential:
if
F=\mathrm{d}A
and (
2.2
) holds, then

\square_{g_{M,a}}A_{\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

(2.3)

Conversely, if
(A,\phi)
solves (
2.3
) and the Lorenz constraints in
(
2.6
) below hold on one Cauchy slice, then (
2.2
) propagates and
(F,\phi)
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
solves (
1.5
)–(
1.6
).
This follows from current conservation: taking the divergence of (
2.3
) yields a homogeneous wave equation for
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}
.

2.2
Cauchy data and constraint equations

Fix
\tau_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
A smooth Cauchy data set for (
2.3
) consists of

\bigl(A|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}},\;\nabla_{n}A|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}},\;\phi|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}},\;D_{n}\phi|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\bigr),

(2.4)

where
n
is the future unit normal to
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
Let
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
be a local orthonormal frame tangent to
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
We define the electric field on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
by
E_{i}:=F(n,e_{i})
.
The Maxwell equation implies the Gauss constraint

\nabla^{i}E_{i}=-\,J_{\mu}n^{\mu}\qquad\text{on }\Sigma_{\tau_{0}},

(2.5)

where
\nabla^{i}
denotes the Levi–Civita connection of
(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}},\gamma)
.
In Lorenz gauge we further impose the standard compatibility conditions

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\big|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}=0,\qquad\nabla_{n}\bigl(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\bigr)\big|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}=0,\qquad\nabla^{i}E_{i}=-\,J_{\mu}n^{\mu}.

(2.6)

On a Ricci-flat background, these conditions propagate by a standard hyperbolic constraint argument.

2.3
Admissible Kerr data spaces

The scattering maps in Theorem
1.1
are defined on a small neighbourhood of the origin in an order-
k
energy completion of Lorenz-compatible data.
For concreteness, we work with the fluxes of the Maxwell–Higgs stress–energy tensor through
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.

The stress–energy tensor associated to the Lagrangian (
1.4
) is

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]

\displaystyle=F_{\mu\alpha}F_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\Re\!\left(D_{\mu}\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\right)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Bigl(|D\phi|^{2}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr).

(2.7)

For solutions of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) one has
\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]=0
.
For a vector field
X
we define the corresponding energy current and flux by

J^{X}_{\mu}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]X^{\nu},\qquad E^{X}[F,\phi](\tau_{0}):=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}J^{X}[F,\phi]\cdot n.

We use
X=N
from Definition
1.3
and commute with the admissible family
\mathcal{Z}
.

Definition 2.1

(Admissible Kerr Cauchy data)
.

Fix
k\geq 6
, Kerr parameters
(M,a)
with
|a|<M
, and an initial slice
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
An
admissible Kerr initial data set in Lorenz gauge
is a smooth quadruple (
2.4
) on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
such that

(i)

the constraint system (
2.6
) holds (and its commuted versions up to order
k
),

(ii)

the associated charges
(Q_{e},Q_{m})
of Definition
1.1
vanish (charge-free case), and

(iii)

the order-
k
redshift energy flux

\mathcal{E}^{N}_{k,M,a}(\tau_{0}):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}J^{N}\!\bigl[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F,\;\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi\bigr]\cdot n

is finite, where
N
and
\mathcal{Z}
are taken from the admissible Kerr structure of Definition
1.3
.
We denote by
\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})
the completion of smooth admissible data in the norm

\|(A,\phi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}}:=\bigl(\mathcal{E}^{N}_{k,M,a}(\tau_{0})\bigr)^{1/2}
.

Remark 2.1

(Nonzero charges and Coulomb subtraction)
.

If
(Q_{e},Q_{m})\neq(0,0)
then
F
contains a stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb component
F^{(a)}_{Q_{e},Q_{m}}
.
In this case one works with the renormalised field
\widetilde{F}:=F-F^{(a)}_{Q_{e},Q_{m}}
, which is charge free.
Since the Kerr exterior has
H^{2}(\mathcal{D}_{M,a})\cong\mathbb{R}
generated by the magnetic charge, vanishing charges imply that
\widetilde{F}
is exact; hence
\widetilde{F}=\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}
globally, and the Lorenz gauge formulation applies to the radiative remainder.

2.4
Gauge fixing

For the purposes of this paper we restrict attention to initial data which already satisfy the Lorenz constraints (
2.6
).
Such a restriction is standard in the analysis of gauge-covariant hyperbolic systems.
One may gauge transform general smooth finite-energy data satisfying the Gauss constraint (
2.5
) into Lorenz gauge by solving for a gauge function
\chi
so that (
2.2
) holds (for instance by imposing (
2.6
) on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
and using the propagation of
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}
).
The residual gauge freedom inside Lorenz gauge consists of transformations (
2.1
) with
\square_{g_{M,a}}\chi=0
.

3
Energy identities and integrated decay estimates on Kerr

In this section we record the vector–field energy identities and the integrated decay estimates used in the Kerr part of the argument.
The identities are consequences of the divergence-free property of the Maxwell–Higgs stress–energy tensor (
2.7
), while the integrated decay estimates are used through the abstract linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
of Definition
1.4
.
Throughout we work on a fixed subextremal Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
with
|a|<M
and the Kerr–star foliation
\Sigma_{\tau}=\{t^{\star}=\tau\}
.

3.1
Energy currents and divergence identities

Let
T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]
be the stress–energy tensor (
2.7
).
For any smooth vector field
X
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
we define the associated current and bulk term by

J^{X}_{\mu}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,X^{\nu},\qquad K^{X}[F,\phi]:=\nabla^{\mu}J^{X}_{\mu}[F,\phi].

(3.1)

Writing
{}^{(X)}\pi_{\mu\nu}:=\nabla_{\mu}X_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}X_{\mu}
for the deformation tensor of
X
, the conservation law
\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]=0
yields the standard identity

K^{X}[F,\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\,T^{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\;{}^{(X)}\pi_{\mu\nu}.

(3.2)

In particular, if
X
is Killing then
{}^{(X)}\pi\equiv 0
and the corresponding energy flux is conserved.

Let
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
denote the slab region bounded by
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
,
\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}
, the future event horizon segment
\mathcal{H}^{+}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, and future null infinity segment
\mathcal{I}^{+}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Integrating (
3.2
) and applying Stokes’ theorem gives the basic energy identity

E^{X}[F,\phi](\tau_{1})+\mathcal{F}^{X}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}[F,\phi](\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathcal{F}^{X}_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}[F,\phi](\tau_{0},\tau_{1})=E^{X}[F,\phi](\tau_{0})+\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}K^{X}[F,\phi]\;\mathrm{d}V_{g_{M,a}},

(3.3)

where
E^{X}
is the flux through
\Sigma_{\tau}
defined in Section
2.3
and
\mathcal{F}^{X}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}
,
\mathcal{F}^{X}_{\mathcal{I}^{+}}
denote the corresponding fluxes through
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
When
X
is future causal and tangent to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, these boundary fluxes are nonnegative.

3.2
Redshift energy and superradiance

On Kerr the stationary Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
(equivalently,
\partial_{t_{\mathrm{BL}}}
) fails to be everywhere timelike:
T
becomes spacelike in the ergoregion, which is the geometric origin of superradiance.
A standard way to obtain a globally coercive energy is to employ a
redshift multiplier

N
as in Definition
1.3
: one chooses
N
to be future timelike everywhere, to agree with
T
for large
r
, and to coincide near the horizon with the future horizon generator

K:=T+\Omega_{\mathcal{H}}\,\Phi,\qquad\Omega_{\mathcal{H}}:=\frac{a}{r_{+}^{2}+a^{2}},

(3.4)

where
\Phi=\partial_{\varphi}
(equivalently,
\partial_{\varphi_{\mathrm{BL}}}
) is the axial Killing field and
r_{+}=M+\sqrt{M^{2}-a^{2}}
.
With this choice, the flux of
J^{N}
through
\Sigma_{\tau}
defines a nondegenerate energy controlling
|F|^{2}+|D\phi|^{2}
uniformly up to the horizon, while the horizon flux is compatible with the positivity of the
K
–energy on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
The superradiant part of the dynamics is treated in the linear theory through frequency analysis (for waves and Maxwell via the Teukolsky equation), but in this paper we only use its consequences through the estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
.

3.3
Integrated local energy decay and the
r^{p}
hierarchy

The nonlinear argument treats the Maxwell–Higgs system as a perturbation of the linear scalar wave and Maxwell systems, with the nonlinearities viewed as inhomogeneous source terms after commuting with the admissible vector fields
\mathcal{Z}
.
Accordingly, the key input is an
inhomogeneous
integrated decay estimate which simultaneously provides
(i) boundedness of the nondegenerate energy, (ii) a Morawetz (integrated local energy decay) bulk term which degenerates only at the trapped set (photon region), and (iii) an
r^{p}
hierarchy in the asymptotically flat region controlling the radiation fluxes through
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.

In the framework of Definition
1.4
this input is encapsulated by the inequality (
1.27
) for a suitable admissible norm pair
(\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{S}_{k})
.
For emphasis, we record a representative schematic form of the estimate on Kerr.

A representative Morawetz bulk density in Kerr–star coordinates.

To make the content of Proposition
3.1
more concrete, we record a typical choice of the local energy bulk terms appearing in
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}}
.
In Kerr–star coordinates
(t,r,\theta,\varphi)
for
g_{M,a}
, cf. (
1.2
), and with the spacelike time function
t^{\star}=t-r
from (
1.3
), one has the volume form

\mathrm{d}V_{g_{M,a}}=\Sigma\sin\theta\,\mathrm{d}t^{\star}\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}\varphi,\qquad\Sigma=r^{2}+a^{2}\cos^{2}\theta.

Let
S_{\tau,r}:=\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r=\mathrm{const}\}
and let
\not{\nabla}
denote the induced Levi–Civita connection on
S_{\tau,r}
.
On each compact region
\{r\leq R\}
the induced metric on
S_{\tau,r}
is uniformly comparable to the round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
, so in estimates one may freely replace
|\not{\nabla}f|^{2}
by
(\partial_{\theta}f)^{2}+\sin^{-2}\theta\,(\partial_{\varphi}f)^{2}
at the expense of constants depending on
(M,a,R)
.

With these conventions, a representative Morawetz (integrated local energy) density for the scalar wave equation is the quadratic form

\mathfrak{e}[f]:=|\partial_{t^{\star}}f|^{2}+|\partial_{r}f|^{2}+r^{-2}|\not{\nabla}f|^{2}.

Then the bulk part of
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
may be chosen so as to control, schematically,

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\Bigl(w_{\mathrm{trap}}\;\mathfrak{e}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi]+r^{-1-\delta}\,\mathfrak{e}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi]+r^{-3-\delta}|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}V_{g_{M,a}},

(3.5)

where
w_{\mathrm{trap}}\geq 0
is a trapping weight degenerating only on the photon region.
On Schwarzschild one may take
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
to be a smooth function of
r
vanishing only at the photon sphere
r=3M
.
On Kerr, the trapped set is a subset of phase space; in the full subextremal theory the Morawetz estimate is obtained by a microlocal multiplier and (
3.5
) should be interpreted as the physical-space avatar of a bulk term which degenerates only on trapped null geodesics.

In the far region
r\geq R
one may take the
r^{p}
part of
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}}
to coincide (up to equivalence) with the standard Dafermos–Rodnianski functional.
In terms of the asymptotic retarded coordinate
u=t^{\star}-r
and the asymptotically outgoing vector field
L_{\mathrm{out}}=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\partial_{r}
, this functional controls, schematically,

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p-1}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\Bigl(|L_{\mathrm{out}}(r\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi)|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}(r\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi)|^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\omega,

(3.6)

together with the corresponding weighted fluxes through
\mathcal{I}^{+}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

A representative Maxwell bulk density.

Let
G
be a smooth, charge-free Maxwell field on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
and fix a smooth null frame
(L,\underline{L},e_{1},e_{2})
adapted to the foliation and the spheres
S_{\tau,r}
.
In the far region one may take
L=L_{\mathrm{out}}=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\partial_{r}
and
\underline{L}=\partial_{t^{\star}}-\partial_{r}
; on compact sets any two such frames give equivalent norms.
Define the null components of
G
by

\alpha_{A}[G]:=G(L,e_{A}),\qquad\underline{\alpha}_{A}[G]:=G(\underline{L},e_{A}),\qquad\rho[G]:=\frac{1}{2}\,G(L,\underline{L}),\qquad\sigma[G]:=\frac{1}{2}\,{}^{\star}G(L,\underline{L}),

where
⋆
denotes the Hodge dual with respect to
g_{M,a}
and
A\in\{1,2\}
.
Set the Maxwell local energy density

\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}[G]:=|\alpha[G]|^{2}+|\underline{\alpha}[G]|^{2}+|\rho[G]|^{2}+|\sigma[G]|^{2},

where the norms are taken with respect to the induced metric on
S_{\tau,r}
.
Then the bulk part of
\|G\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}
may be chosen so as to control, schematically,

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}\sum_{|\beta|\leq k}\Bigl(w_{\mathrm{trap}}\;\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta}G]+r^{-1-\delta}\,\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta}G]+r^{-3-\delta}\,|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta}G|^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}V_{g_{M,a}},

(3.7)

together with the corresponding redshift control on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
In the far region
r\geq R
one may formulate the
r^{p}
hierarchy in terms of the outgoing null component
\alpha[G]
(equivalently, in terms of the spin
+1
Teukolsky scalar); schematically,

\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{p-1}\sum_{|\beta|\leq k}\Bigl(|L_{\mathrm{out}}(r\,\alpha[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta}G])|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}(r\,\alpha[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{\beta}G])|^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\omega.

(3.8)

Proposition 3.1

(Redshift–Morawetz–
r^{p}
estimate on Kerr)
.

Fix
M>0
,
|a|<M
, and an integer
k\geq 0
.
Let
\psi
solve
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
, or let
G
be a charge-free Maxwell field solving
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
.
Then there exist admissible norms
(\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{S}_{k})
in the sense of Definition
1.4
such that for every
\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}
one has

\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\leq C\Bigl(E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\Bigr),

with the analogous estimate for
G
and
J
.
Moreover, one may take the concrete norms of Lemma
1.7
, whose bulk terms include a redshift contribution near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, a Morawetz contribution with trapping weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
in the interior, and a full Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
hierarchy in the far region
\{r\geq R\}
.

Proof.

For scalar waves, the corresponding redshift–Morawetz–
r^{p}
estimate in the full subextremal range
|a|<M
is proved in
[
17
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2]
, including higher-order commutations; the inhomogeneous estimate follows by the standard energy method and Duhamel’s principle in the same norms.
For charge-free Maxwell fields, after subtraction of the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb solution, the required physical-space energy boundedness and decay estimates are established in
[
18
, Theorem 5.1]
; the argument there relies on the Teukolsky estimates of
[
20
, Theorem A]
(and the associated frequency-space analysis
[
19
]
) for the extreme spin-
\pm 1
components.
The inhomogeneous estimate with current
J
is obtained by the same energy method.
Translating the norms in these references into the abstract framework of Definition
1.4
yields admissible norms
(\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{S}_{k})
.
The concrete choice of Lemma
1.7
is equivalent to the physical-space norms appearing in the cited works, and therefore also admissible.
∎

Remark 3.1

(Use in the nonlinear bootstrap)
.

In the Maxwell–Higgs system, commuting the Lorenz-reduced equations (
2.3
) with
\mathcal{Z}
produces source terms which are at least quadratic in
(F,D\phi,\phi)
.
In the small-data regime of Theorem
1.1
these quadratic terms are treated as inhomogeneities, and Proposition
3.1
(or, equivalently, the abstract estimate (
1.27
)) is applied on successive slabs
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
to close the energy and decay bootstrap.

4
Decay in the far region on Kerr

In this section we record decay estimates in the asymptotically flat region
\{r\geq R\}
of a fixed subextremal Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
.
The main quantitative conclusions in the far-away region are Proposition
4.3
(inhomogeneous scalar waves, together with the tame Maxwell–Higgs source bounds needed for the Higgs equation) and Proposition
4.5
(charge-free Maxwell fields with current). Their conclusions are summarised in Subsubsection
1.6
.

These estimates are standard consequences of the
r^{p}
hierarchy encoded in the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
(Definition
1.4
); in concrete form they correspond to the far-field terms (
3.6
)–(
3.8
).
We include them here in a form compatible with the Kerr–star foliation and with the gauge-covariant Maxwell–Higgs system, since far-field decay is the main analytic input for both the nonlinear final-state construction and the structure results for the scattering operator.

Black-box status.
The far-region decay statements recorded in this section are consequences of the
r^{p}
hierarchy and linear scattering theory contained in the package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
; in the Kerr case we do not reprove these estimates here, but rather record them in a form compatible with the nonlinear arguments and refer to Section
6
for the underlying linear theory.

Main far-region decay statements.

The nonlinear scattering and wave-operator arguments use two far-region inputs:

•

the inhomogeneous far-field decay estimate for scalar waves (and hence, via the tame bounds, for the Higgs equation), together with coercive control of the Maxwell–Higgs nonlinearities in the admissible source norm (Proposition
4.3
);

•

the corresponding inhomogeneous far-field decay estimate for charge-free Maxwell fields with conserved current (Proposition
4.5
).

Both propositions are consequences of the Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
hierarchy encoded in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
and hold for every subextremal Kerr exterior
|a|<M
, in particular for the Schwarzschild case
a=0
.
In the Schwarzschild part of the paper we provide a self-contained derivation of the underlying
r^{p}
hierarchy and the resulting pointwise far-region decay; see Section
9
and Corollary
1.16
.

Fix
R\gg 1
so that the stationary Killing field
T=\partial_{t^{\star}}
is uniformly timelike on
\{r\geq R\}
and the induced metrics on the spheres

S_{\tau,r}:=\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r=\mathrm{const}\}

are uniformly comparable to the round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
of radius
r
.

4.1
Outgoing geometry and Sobolev inequalities on
S_{\tau,r}

Introduce the retarded and advanced coordinates in the far region

u:=t^{\star}-r,\qquad v:=t^{\star}+r,

(4.1)

and set

L:=\partial_{t^{\star}}+\partial_{r}=\partial_{r}\big|_{u},\qquad\underline{L}:=\partial_{t^{\star}}-\partial_{r}.

In the asymptotically flat region
r\geq R
, the vector field
L
is future directed and outgoing null up to
O(r^{-1})
corrections, while
\underline{L}
is incoming.
We write
\not{\nabla}
for the Levi–Civita connection on
S_{\tau,r}
and
\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\tau,r}
for the induced area form.

Lemma 4.1

(Sobolev inequality on
S_{\tau,r}
)
.

There exists
R_{0}\gg 1
such that for every
R\geq R_{0}
there is a constant
C=C(M,a,R)
with the following property.
For every smooth complex-valued function
f
on
S_{\tau,r}
with
r\geq R
,

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(S_{\tau,r})}^{2}\leq C\,r^{-2}\sum_{j=0}^{2}\int_{S_{\tau,r}}|\not{\nabla}^{\,j}f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma_{\tau,r}.

(4.2)

Proof.

By asymptotic flatness, for
r\geq R
the rescaled metrics
r^{-2}\gamma|_{S_{\tau,r}}
form a uniformly smooth family on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
The standard Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
therefore yields (
4.2
) with constants uniform for
r\geq R
.
∎

4.2
Scalar far-field decay and the Higgs component

Coupled Maxwell–Higgs energy functionals and control of the curvature
F

Remark 4.1

(Curvature
F
versus forcing terms)
.

In the Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
), the symbol
F=\mathrm{d}A
denotes the electromagnetic curvature
2
–form.
Separately, in the
linear
wave estimates one often writes
\square_{g}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
for an
external forcing term
.
When both objects appear in the same discussion we reserve the letter
F
for the curvature and write the forcing as
\mathfrak{F}
.
In particular, any specialization to
\mathfrak{F}\equiv 0
refers to vanishing forcing and
never
to the (generically false) constraint
F=0
for Maxwell–Higgs solutions.

Proposition 4.2

(Coupled Maxwell–Higgs energies and coercive control of the curvature)
.

Let
(A,\phi)
be a smooth solution of the Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) on a globally hyperbolic region of a background spacetime
(\mathcal{D},g)
.
Let
F=\mathrm{d}A
and let

J_{\nu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr)=2\,\Im(\overline{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi)

(4.3)

be the gauge current, so that
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
.

Stress–energy tensors and energy fluxes.

Define the Maxwell and Higgs stress–energy tensors by

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu\nu}[F]

\displaystyle:=F_{\mu\alpha}F_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu\nu}[\phi,A]

\displaystyle:=\Re\!\bigl(D_{\mu}\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Bigl(D_{\alpha}\phi\,\overline{D^{\alpha}\phi}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr),

(4.5)

and set
\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu\nu}:=\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu\nu}+\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu\nu}
, which coincides with
T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]
from (
2.7
) when
g=g_{M,a}
.
For any vector field
X
define the associated currents

J^{X,(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu}:=\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu},\qquad J^{X,(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu}:=\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu},\qquad J^{X,(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu}:=\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu}.

For any spacelike hypersurface
\Sigma
with future unit normal
n_{\Sigma}
and induced volume form
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma}
we define the corresponding fluxes by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{X}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}J^{X,(\mathrm{M})}\!\cdot n_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma},

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{X}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}J^{X,(\mathrm{H})}\!\cdot n_{\Sigma}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{X}(\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{X}(\Sigma)+\mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{X}(\Sigma).

(i) Separate divergence identities and energy exchange.

Let
{}^{(X)}\pi_{\mu\nu}:=\nabla_{\mu}X_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}X_{\mu}
.
Then

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}J^{X,(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu\nu}\;{}^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}\;-\;F_{\nu\lambda}J^{\lambda}X^{\nu},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}J^{X,(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu\nu}\;{}^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}\;+\;F_{\nu\lambda}J^{\lambda}X^{\nu}.

(4.8)

In particular, the coupling term
F_{\nu\lambda}J^{\lambda}X^{\nu}
is the precise exchange of energy between the Maxwell and Higgs sectors, so the Maxwell and Higgs energies are not separately conserved in general.

(ii) Cancellation in the total energy.

Adding (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) yields

\nabla^{\mu}J^{X,(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu\nu}\;{}^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}.

(4.9)

In particular, if
X
is Killing then
\nabla^{\mu}J^{X,(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu}=0
.

(iii) Energy identity and coercive control of
F
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be any spacetime region with piecewise smooth boundary
\partial\mathcal{R}
and outward normal
n_{\partial\mathcal{R}}
.
Integrating (
4.9
) and applying Stokes’ theorem gives

\int_{\partial\mathcal{R}}J^{X,(\mathrm{MH})}\!\cdot n_{\partial\mathcal{R}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\partial\mathcal{R}}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathcal{R}}\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{\mu\nu}\;{}^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}\,\mathrm{d}V_{g}.

(4.10)

Assume in addition that
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\geq 0
and that
X
is future directed timelike on
\Sigma
.
Let
h_{\Sigma}:=g+n_{\Sigma}\otimes n_{\Sigma}
be the induced Riemannian metric.
Define the electric and magnetic parts of
F
with respect to
\Sigma
by

E_{\mu}:=F_{\mu\nu}n_{\Sigma}^{\nu},\qquad B_{\mu}:=({}^{\star}\!F)_{\mu\nu}n_{\Sigma}^{\nu},\qquad|F|_{\Sigma}^{2}:=|E|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}}+|B|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}},

(4.11)

and the tangential Higgs derivative by
(D^{\top}\phi)_{\mu}:=(\delta_{\mu}^{\nu}+n_{\Sigma\mu}n_{\Sigma}^{\nu})D_{\nu}\phi
.
Then there exists a constant
c_{g,X}>0
, depending only on the geometry and on the uniform timelikeness of
X
on
\Sigma
, such that

J^{X,(\mathrm{MH})}\!\cdot n_{\Sigma}=\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n_{\Sigma},X)\geq c_{g,X}\Bigl(|F|_{\Sigma}^{2}+|D_{n_{\Sigma}}\phi|^{2}+|D^{\top}\phi|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr)\qquad\text{pointwise on }\Sigma.

(4.12)

Consequently,

\int_{\Sigma}|F|_{\Sigma}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma}\ \lesssim_{g,X}\ \mathcal{E}^{(\mathrm{MH})}_{X}(\Sigma).

(4.13)

Proof.

We record the key identities.

Step 1: Divergence of the Maxwell stress–energy.

Using
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
and the Bianchi identity
\nabla_{[\alpha}F_{\beta\gamma]}=0
, a standard computation yields

\nabla^{\mu}\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}_{\mu\nu}[F]=-\,F_{\nu\lambda}J^{\lambda}.

Contracting with
X^{\nu}
and using

\nabla^{\mu}(\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu})=(\nabla^{\mu}\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu})X^{\nu}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{T}_{\mu\nu}\,{}^{(X)}\pi^{\mu\nu}

gives (
4.7
).

Step 2: Divergence of the Higgs stress–energy.

Using (
1.6
), its complex conjugate, and gauge-covariant Leibniz rules yields

\nabla^{\mu}\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}_{\mu\nu}[\phi,A]=+\,F_{\nu\lambda}J^{\lambda},

and hence (
4.8
).

Step 3: Total identity and Stokes’ theorem.

Adding (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) gives (
4.9
).
Integrating (
4.9
) over
\mathcal{R}
and applying the divergence theorem yields (
4.10
).

Step 4: Coercivity on spacelike hypersurfaces.

Let
n=n_{\Sigma}
.
With the decomposition (
4.11
) one has the standard identities

\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{M})}(n,n)=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(|E|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}}+|B|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}}\bigr),\qquad\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{H})}(n,n)=\tfrac{1}{2}\bigl(|D_{n}\phi|^{2}+|D^{\top}\phi|^{2}_{h_{\Sigma}}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\bigr),

and therefore
\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,n)
is a positive definite density controlling
|F|_{\Sigma}^{2}
and
|D\phi|^{2}
.
Write
X=\alpha n+V
on
\Sigma
, where
\alpha:=-g(X,n)>0
and
V
is tangent to
\Sigma
.
Since
X
is timelike,
\alpha>|V|_{h_{\Sigma}}
pointwise; uniform timelikeness gives
\alpha-|V|_{h_{\Sigma}}\geq c_{0}>0
on
\Sigma
.
Moreover,
\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}
satisfies the dominant energy condition when
P\geq 0
, and hence

|\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,V)|\leq\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,n)\,|V|_{h_{\Sigma}}
.
Consequently,

\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,X)=\alpha\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,n)+\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,V)\geq(\alpha-|V|_{h_{\Sigma}})\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,n)\geq c_{0}\,\mathbb{T}^{(\mathrm{MH})}(n,n),

which yields (
4.12
) and hence (
4.13
).
∎

Let
\psi
solve the inhomogeneous wave equation
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
and define the rescaled unknown
\Psi:=r\psi
.
The far-field component of the
\mathcal{X}_{k}
norm (Definition
1.4
) may be chosen so that, in particular, it controls an
r^{p}
hierarchy for
\Psi
as in (
3.6
).
In the homogeneous case
\mathfrak{F}\equiv 0
this implies polynomial decay for the (nondegenerate) energy along the Kerr–star foliation.

Proposition 4.3

(Main far-field scalar decay with forcing and Maxwell–Higgs source bounds)
.

This proposition proves Theorem
1.3
.

Fix
k\geq 2
and let
\psi
be a smooth solution to the inhomogeneous wave equation

\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}

(4.14)

on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
. This includes the Schwarzschild exterior
a=0
as a special case.
Then there exists
R\gg 1
such that for every
0<\delta<1
and all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
the following estimate holds.
Here we set the far-field
r^{p}
bulk/flux functional

\mathcal{B}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau):=\sup_{p\in[0,2]}\mathcal{R}_{k,p}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau),

(4.15)

with
\mathcal{R}_{k,p}
the standard Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
functional from Lemma
1.7
.

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)+\mathcal{B}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0},\tau)\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2},

(4.16)

and, for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\geq R
,

\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\geq R\}}r^{-1-\delta}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\psi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}.

(4.17)

Moreover, consider a Lorenz-gauge solution
(A,\phi)
of the Maxwell–Higgs system (
2.3
) on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
, with curvature
F=\mathrm{d}A
and nonlinearities

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}

\displaystyle:=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+i(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,\phi+\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(4.18)

\displaystyle J_{\nu}

\displaystyle:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr)=2\,\Im(\overline{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi),

(4.19)

where
D=\nabla+iA
is the gauge-covariant derivative.
In Lorenz gauge,
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
, so

\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,\phi+\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

Higher-order Maxwell–Higgs energies.

Define the higher-order Maxwell and Higgs energies, cf. Proposition
4.2
,

\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{M})}[F](\Sigma_{\tau}):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\mathcal{E}_{N}^{(\mathrm{M})}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F](\Sigma_{\tau}),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau}):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\mathcal{E}_{N}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau}),

and set
\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{MH})}:=\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{M})}+\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{H})}
.
In addition, define the componentwise wave energy of the gauge potential by

\mathcal{E}_{N,k}^{(\mathrm{A})}[A](\Sigma_{\tau}):=\sum_{\nu=0}^{3}E^{N}_{k}[A_{\nu}](\Sigma_{\tau}).

(4.20)

To control the nonlinear sources
J
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
in the admissible source norm
\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)
, introduce the following
source-weighted energy functionals
on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
: for integers
m\geq 0
set

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\mathcal{E}_{N,m}^{(\mathrm{A})}[A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}),

(4.21)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\mathcal{E}_{N,m}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}),

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}r^{1+\delta}\Bigl(|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}A|^{2}+|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}A|^{2}\Bigr),

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}r^{1+\delta}\Bigl(|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}+|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}\Bigr),

(4.24)

where
|\cdot|
denotes the pointwise norm induced by
g_{M,a}
.

Then, for every integer
k\geq 6
, the Maxwell–Higgs nonlinearities satisfy the tame source bounds.
For convenience define, for
E_{A},E_{H}\geq 0
,

\mathcal{P}_{k,P}(E_{A},E_{H}):=E_{A}+E_{A}^{2}+E_{H}+E_{A}E_{H}+\bigl(1+E_{H}^{N_{P}}\bigr)^{2}E_{H}^{2}.

(4.25)

Then

\displaystyle\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau),

(4.26)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,P}\mathcal{P}_{k,P}\!\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau),\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr).

(4.27)

In particular, if
\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)
and
\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)
are finite and the source-weighted energies
\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)
,
\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)
are finite, then
J
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
belong to the admissible source space
\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)
.

Proof.

The estimates (
4.16
)–(
4.17
) are the far-field part of the scalar linear package on Kerr, cf.
[
22
, Theorem 1.5 and Proposition 1.6]
.

We only prove the bounds for the Maxwell–Higgs source terms (
4.26
)–(
4.27
).
Fix
k\geq 6
and set
m:=k+2
.

Step 0:
L^{\infty}
control from slice energies.

By standard Sobolev embedding on the asymptotically flat hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}
(using Lemmas
5.1
and
4.1
to treat the compact and far regions), we have for every
\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\mathcal{E}_{N,k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}[A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})\leq\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau),

(4.28)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}+\|D\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\mathcal{E}_{N,k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})\leq\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

(4.29)

Step 1: the current
J_{\nu}
.

Since
J_{\nu}=2\,\Im(\overline{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi)
, for any multiindex
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq m
we apply
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}
and expand:

\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}J_{\nu}=2\,\Im\!\!\sum_{\beta+\gamma=\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi}\,D_{\nu}(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)\;+\;\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi],

(4.30)

where
\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi]
collects the commutator contributions from commuting
\mathcal{Z}
through the covariant derivative
D
.
Since
D=\nabla+iA
, each commutator
[D,\mathcal{Z}]
is a first-order operator with coefficients depending on the background and on
\nabla\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}A
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi]
is a finite sum of terms schematically of the form

(\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{1}}A)\,(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{2}}\phi)\,(\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{3}}\phi}),\qquad|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|\leq m-1.

(4.31)

Because
m=k+2
, in every product in (
4.30
)–(
4.31
) at least one factor carries at most
k
commutators and can be estimated in
L^{\infty}
using (
4.28
)–(
4.29
).
We thus obtain the pointwise tame bound

|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}J|\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}+\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}\Bigr)\sum_{|\beta|\leq m}\Bigl(|D\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi|+|\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi|\Bigr)

(4.32)

at each point of
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
.

Multiplying (
4.32
) by
r^{(1+\delta)/2}
, squaring, integrating over
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau)
, and summing over
|\alpha|\leq m
yields

\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}+\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

(4.33)

Finally, inserting (
4.28
)–(
4.29
) into (
4.33
) gives (
4.26
).

Step 2: the Higgs forcing
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
.

In Lorenz gauge we have
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
and therefore

\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,\phi+\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

Using
\nabla\phi=D\phi-iA\phi
we have the schematic pointwise bound

|\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}|\lesssim|A|\,|D\phi|+|A|^{2}|\phi|+|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})|.

(4.34)

We estimate the three contributions separately in the source norm
\mathcal{S}_{m}
.

(i) The term
A\,D\phi
.

Applying
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}
with
|\alpha|\leq m
and expanding by Leibniz’ rule gives products of the form

(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}A)\,D(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)
with
|\beta|+|\gamma|\leq m
, plus commutator terms coming from commuting
\mathcal{Z}
with
D
, which are lower order and can be treated in the same way (cf. Step 1).
Since
m=k+2
, in each product one factor carries at most
k
commutators.
Hence,

\displaystyle\|A\,D\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|D\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

(4.35)

Using (
4.28
)–(
4.29
) yields

\|A\,D\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)\,\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr).

(4.36)

(ii) The cubic term
A^{2}\phi
.

Applying
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}
and expanding by Leibniz’ rule yields products of the form

(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{1}}A)(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{2}}A)(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)
with
|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\gamma|\leq m
.
Again, since
m=k+2
, at least two factors carry at most
k
commutators and can be estimated in
L^{\infty}
.
Thus

\displaystyle\|A^{2}\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{4}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)

(4.37)

\displaystyle+\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\,\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

Invoking (
4.28
)–(
4.29
) gives

\displaystyle\|A^{2}\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\bigr)^{2}+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\,\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr)

(4.38)

\displaystyle\times\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau)+\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\Bigr).

(iii) The potential term
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
.

By Lemma
1.9
, for
|\alpha|\leq m
the commuted quantity
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
is a finite sum of products with at least three factors drawn from
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi,\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi}\}
, multiplied by smooth coefficients that grow at most like
(1+|\phi|^{2N_{P}})
.
Since
m=k+2
, in each such product at least two factors carry at most
k
commutators; estimating those factors in
L^{\infty}
and the remaining factor in
L^{2}
yields

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{k,P}

\displaystyle\Bigl(1+\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2N_{P}}\Bigr)^{2}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\times\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{4}\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

Using (
4.29
) gives

\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k,P}\Bigl(1+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)^{N_{P}}\Bigr)^{2}\,\bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau)\bigr)^{2}\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau).

(4.40)

Combining (
4.36
), (
4.38
), and (
4.40
), and recalling that
m=k+2
, yields (
4.27
) with
\mathcal{P}_{k,P}
as in (
4.25
).
∎

Remark 4.2

(Choice of the decay-loss parameter)
.

In the nonlinear wave-operator and final-state constructions we fix a single small
\delta>0
and use it consistently in the far-region and compact-region decay bounds.
The exponent
-2+2\delta
in the energy decay estimates (
4.16
) and (
4.42
) is chosen so that, after taking square roots and applying Sobolev on the spheres
S_{\tau,r}
, the resulting pointwise rates are exactly of order
(1+v_{+})^{-1+\delta}
(and hence
(1+\tau)^{-1+\delta}
on compact sets), which is the rate used to close the nonlinear source integrability estimates.

Corollary 4.4

(Pointwise decay in the far region for scalar waves)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.3
and for
k\geq 6
, there exist
R\gg 1
such that for every
0<\delta<1
one can find
C=C(M,a,k,\delta)
with the following property: for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\geq R
,

|\psi(\tau,r,\omega)|+|\nabla\psi(\tau,r,\omega)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+v_{+})^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr),\qquad v:=t^{\star}+r.

(4.41)

Proof.

The pointwise decay bounds for
\psi
and its first derivatives in the full subextremal Kerr exterior are proved in
[
17
, Corollary 3.1]
in the homogeneous case.
The extension to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
follows by the same vector-field argument (or, equivalently, a Duhamel representation), with the contribution of the source term controlled by
\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}
.
Restricting those estimates to the region
\{r\geq R\}
and translating to the Kerr–star advanced time
v=t^{\star}+r
gives (
4.41
).
∎

Remark 4.3

(Application to the Higgs field)
.

In Lorenz gauge, the Higgs field satisfies a covariant wave equation with source terms that are at least quadratic in
(F,D\phi,\phi)
; see (
2.3
).
In the small-data regime of Theorem
1.1
, these quadratic terms are treated as inhomogeneities and estimated using the source norm
\mathcal{S}_{k}
.
Applying Proposition
4.3
and Corollary
4.4
to the commuted Higgs equation yields far-region decay bounds of the same form as (
4.41
) (with an arbitrarily small loss in the exponent) for
\phi
and
D\phi
.

4.3
Far-field decay for charge-free Maxwell fields

Let
G
be a charge-free Maxwell field on
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
, solving

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
and
\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0
.
In the far region we use a null frame
(L,\underline{L},e_{1},e_{2})
adapted to the spheres
S_{\tau,r}
and write the corresponding null components

\alpha_{A}[G]=G(L,e_{A}),\qquad\underline{\alpha}_{A}[G]=G(\underline{L},e_{A}),\qquad\rho[G]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,G(L,\underline{L}),\qquad\sigma[G]=\tfrac{1}{2}\,{}^{\star}G(L,\underline{L}).

The
r^{p}
hierarchy for Maxwell fields (cf. (
3.8
)) implies the same polynomial decay mechanism as for scalar waves, after commuting with the admissible symmetry operators in
\mathcal{Z}
.

Proposition 4.5

(Main far-field energy decay for Maxwell fields with current)
.

This proposition proves Theorem
1.4
.

Fix
k\geq 2
and let
G
be a smooth charge-free Maxwell field solving the inhomogeneous Maxwell system on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
(including the Schwarzschild case
a=0
),

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0,

with a smooth current
J
satisfying the compatibility condition
\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0
.
Then there exists
R\gg 1
such that for every
0<\delta<1
and all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
the following estimate holds.

E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}.

(4.42)

In particular, in the Maxwell–Higgs system in Lorenz gauge (
2.2
) the wave equation (
2.3
) has forcing term
J=J[\phi;A]
and one
cannot
specialize to the case
J\equiv 0
.
The
L^{2}
size of this coupling term is controlled by the coupled Maxwell–Higgs energies; see (
4.26
).

Proof.

After subtraction of the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb solution, the nondegenerate energy boundedness and polynomial decay for charge-free Maxwell fields on subextremal Kerr are established in
[
18
, Theorem 5.1]
.
In the homogeneous case
J\equiv 0
, commuting with the admissible symmetry operators
\mathcal{Z}
and translating the energies used there to our
N
–energy yields the decay rate (
4.42
) without the source term.

For a general inhomogeneous current
J
, the same physical-space vector-field argument (equivalently, a Duhamel representation) yields the additional contribution
\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}
.
This is precisely the inhomogeneous estimate encoded in the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
; see Proposition
3.1
.

The final statement about Maxwell–Higgs follows from the energy/Sobolev control of the current established in Proposition
4.3
, namely (
4.26
).
∎

Corollary 4.6

(Pointwise decay in the far region for Maxwell)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.5
and for
k\geq 6
, there exist
R\gg 1
such that for every
0<\delta<1
one can find
C=C(M,a,k,\delta)
with the following property: for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\geq R
,

|\alpha[G]|+|\underline{\alpha}[G]|+|\rho[G]|+|\sigma[G]|\leq\frac{C}{(1+v_{+})^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr),\qquad v:=t^{\star}+r.

(4.43)

Proof.

Pointwise decay for the Maxwell system in the full subextremal Kerr exterior (after Coulomb subtraction) follows from the Teukolsky estimates for the extreme spin-
\pm 1
components
[
20
, Theorem A]
together with the reconstruction and transport identities for the middle components (see, for instance,
[
18
]
) in the homogeneous case.
The extension to the inhomogeneous system
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
follows by the same argument (or, equivalently, a Duhamel representation), with the contribution of the current controlled by
\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}
.
Restricting to
\{r\geq R\}
yields (
4.43
).
∎

Remark 4.4

(Application to Maxwell–Higgs)
.

For a Maxwell–Higgs solution
(F,\phi)
with (possibly nonzero) charges
(Q_{e},Q_{m})
, we first subtract the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb field and work with the radiative remainder

\widetilde{F}:=F-F^{(a)}_{Q_{e},Q_{m}}

(Remark
2.1
).
Then
\widetilde{F}
is charge-free but solves the
sourced
Maxwell system

\nabla^{\mu}\widetilde{F}_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}[\phi;A],\qquad\nabla_{[\alpha}\widetilde{F}_{\beta\gamma]}=0,

where the current
J[\phi;A]
is given by (
4.3
) and is nonzero in general.
Applying Proposition
4.5
and Corollary
4.6
to
\widetilde{F}
yields far-region decay for the radiative Maxwell field, with the current contribution controlled by the coupled Maxwell–Higgs energies via (
4.26
).
Remark
4.3
yields the corresponding far-region decay for
\phi
and
D\phi
.

5
Decay in the trapped and near-horizon region on Kerr

In this section we complement the far-field decay of Section
4
by recording decay estimates in the region where trapping and redshift effects must be combined, namely the compact set
\{r_{+}\leq r\leq R\}
of a fixed subextremal Kerr exterior.
The main quantitative conclusion in the trapped/compact region is the integrated local energy decay estimate of Proposition
5.5
, whose role in the nonlinear argument is discussed in Subsubsection
1.6
.

On Kerr the trapped set is not confined to a single radius; rather it is a codimension-one subset of phase space (the photon region).
This is reflected in physical space by the trapping weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
appearing in the Morawetz bulk norms in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
(Definition
1.4
); see also the representative bulk densities (
3.5
) and (
3.7
).
The bounds below are standard consequences of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
and the full-subextremal linear decay theory; we state them in a form convenient for the nonlinear bootstrap and for the construction of horizon radiation fields.

Black-box status.
The trapped-set and near-horizon decay statements recorded here are part of the standard redshift/Morawetz mechanism on subextremal Kerr. They are used only through the abstract norms of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
and are justified by the linear results cited in Section
6
.

Main compact-region decay statement.

The main quantitative input in the compact region is the trapped-set integrated decay estimate
Proposition
5.5
.
It controls the Morawetz bulk norm on
\mathcal{K}_{R}
and, in the Maxwell–Higgs system, yields tame bounds for the nonlinear sources
J
and
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
in the admissible forcing norm.
The near-horizon estimate (Proposition
5.2
) and its pointwise corollaries are preparatory: they provide the redshift control needed near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and are used later in the construction of horizon radiation fields.
As in Section
4
, the Schwarzschild case
a=0
is included; in Schwarzschild one may take the trapping weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
to vanish only at the photon sphere
r=3M
, and a self-contained proof of the corresponding compact-region decay is given in Section
10
(see also Corollary
1.17
).

Fix
R>r_{+}
, and for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
set

\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}:=\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}.

For a scalar field
\psi
and an integer
k\geq 0
we define the local (nondegenerate)
N
–energy on
\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}
by

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau;\,r\leq R):=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}}J^{N}_{\mu}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi]\,n^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}},

(5.1)

and similarly for Maxwell fields (with
J^{N}_{\mu}[G]
in place of
J^{N}_{\mu}[\psi]
).

5.1
Sobolev estimates on compact subsets of the Kerr–star slices

Lemma 5.1

(Sobolev on
\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}
)
.

Fix
R>r_{+}
.
There exists a constant
C=C(M,a,R)
such that for every smooth complex-valued function
f
on
\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}
,

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R})}^{2}\leq C\sum_{j=0}^{2}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}}|\nabla^{j}f|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}.

(5.2)

Proof.

Since
\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}
is a smooth compact
3
–manifold with boundary, the standard Sobolev embedding
H^{2}\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}
holds with constants depending only on finitely many bounds for the induced geometry.
By stationarity the induced geometries on
\Sigma_{\tau}^{\leq R}
are uniformly equivalent in
\tau
, which yields (
5.2
).
∎

5.2
Near-horizon decay from the redshift effect

Let
r_{0}>0
be fixed so that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{H}}:=\{r_{+}\leq r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}\}
is contained in the redshift region where the multiplier
N
from Definition
1.3
is uniformly timelike.
In this region the redshift identity yields a coercive bulk term controlling derivatives transverse to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, and hence nondegenerate decay statements.

Proposition 5.2

(Near-horizon energy decay for scalar waves with forcing)
.

Fix
k\geq 2
and let
\psi
solve the inhomogeneous wave equation

\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{D}_{M,a}.

Then for every
0<\delta<1
and all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
,

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau;\,r\leq r_{+}+r_{0})\lesssim_{M,a,k,\delta}(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2}.

(5.3)

Moreover, the corresponding redshift bulk term is integrable in time:

\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\infty}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\infty)}^{2}\Bigr).

(5.4)

Proof.

Since
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}\}\subseteq\Sigma_{\tau}
, we have the trivial bound

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau;\,r\leq r_{+}+r_{0})\leq E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau).

Applying Proposition
4.3
to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
gives

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)\lesssim(1+\tau)^{-2+2\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}^{2},

and hence (
5.3
).

For the integrated near-horizon bulk term, apply Proposition
3.1
with index
k+2
to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on the slab
(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
and use the concrete norms of Lemma
1.7
.
The redshift part of
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
controls

\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k+2}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2},

and hence in particular the sum over
|\alpha|\leq k
.
Therefore,

\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{H}}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}|\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi|^{2}\lesssim E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}.

Letting
\tau_{1}\to\infty
and using monotone convergence yields (
5.4
).
∎

Corollary 5.3

(Pointwise decay near the horizon for scalar waves)
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
5.2
and for
k\geq 6
, there exist
r_{0}>0
such that for every
0<\delta<1
one can find
C=C(M,a,k,\delta)
with the following property: for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}
,

|\psi|+|\nabla\psi|\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr).

(5.5)

Proof.

The pointwise decay bounds for
\psi
and its first derivatives in the full subextremal Kerr exterior are proved in
[
17
, Corollary 3.1]
in the homogeneous case.
The extension to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
follows by the same vector-field argument (or, equivalently, a Duhamel representation), with the contribution of the source term controlled by
\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}
.
Restricting to the compact region
\{r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}\}
and using
v=t^{\star}+r=\tau+O(1)
there yields (
5.5
).
∎

Corollary 5.4

(Pointwise decay near the horizon for Maxwell with current)
.

Fix
k\geq 6
and let
G
be a smooth charge-free Maxwell field solving the inhomogeneous Maxwell system

\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad\nabla_{[\alpha}G_{\beta\gamma]}=0

on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
with a smooth conserved current
\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0
.
Then for every
0<\delta<1
there exist
r_{0}>0
and
C=C(M,a,k,\delta)
such that for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}
,

|\alpha[G]|+|\underline{\alpha}[G]|+|\rho[G]|+|\sigma[G]|\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr).

(5.6)

Proof.

Fix
r_{0}>0
.
As in Corollary
5.3
, we apply a Sobolev inequality on the compact subset
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}\}
to the components of
G
in a smooth null frame.
Since the frame is smooth and
r
is bounded, the pointwise size of the null components is controlled by finitely many
L^{2}
norms of covariant derivatives of
G
on
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq r_{+}+r_{0}\}
, hence by
E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau;\,r\leq r_{+}+r_{0})^{1/2}
for
k\geq 6
.
Using
E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau;\,r\leq r_{+}+r_{0})\leq E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)
and taking square roots in Proposition
4.5
yields

E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)^{1/2}\lesssim(1+\tau)^{-1+\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)},

which gives (
5.6
).
∎

5.3
Decay in the trapped region

Set
\mathcal{K}_{R}:=\{r_{+}+r_{0}\leq r\leq R\}
, which contains the photon region and hence all trapping.
The integrated local energy decay component of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
controls a Morawetz bulk norm which degenerates precisely at trapping through the weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
.

Proposition 5.5

(Main trapped-region integrated decay and tame control of Maxwell–Higgs sources)
.

This proposition proves Theorem
1.5
.

Fix
k\geq 0
and let
\psi
solve the inhomogeneous wave equation

\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}\qquad\text{on }\mathcal{D}_{M,a}.

This includes the Schwarzschild exterior
a=0
as a special case.
Then for every
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{K}_{R}}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}w_{\mathrm{trap}}\;\mathfrak{e}[\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\psi]\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau

(5.7)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,R}E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}.

An analogous statement holds for charge-free Maxwell fields
G
solving
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
,
with
\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}
in place of
\mathfrak{e}
and
\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
in place of
\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
.

Assume in addition that
k\geq 6
and let
(A,\phi)
be a Lorenz-gauge solution of the Maxwell–Higgs system (
2.3
) on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,
with nonlinearities
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
and
J_{\nu}
defined by (
4.18
)–(
4.19
).
Define the source-weighted energy functionals
\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}
,
\mathbf{E}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}
,
\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}
,
\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}
as in
(
4.21
)–(
4.24
)
and the tame polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{k,P}
as in (
4.25
).
Then on the slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
the Maxwell–Higgs nonlinearities satisfy

\displaystyle\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

(5.8)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k,P}\mathcal{P}_{k,P}\!\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\times\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathbf{F}_{k+2}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr).

(5.9)

In particular, finiteness of these energy functionals implies that
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
and
J
are admissible sources in the sense of Definition
1.4
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.1
with index
k
to
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
on the slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.
For the concrete norms of Lemma
1.7
, the Morawetz bulk term in
\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}
contains the trapped-region contribution, i.e.

\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau_{1}}\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\mathcal{K}_{R}}w_{\mathrm{trap}}\;\mathfrak{e}[\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\psi]\lesssim\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}.

Using the estimate of Proposition
3.1
yields

\|\psi\|_{\mathcal{X}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau_{0})+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2},

which implies (
5.7
).
The Maxwell case is identical with
\psi,\mathfrak{F},\mathfrak{e}
replaced by
G,J,\mathfrak{e}_{\mathrm{Max}}
.

We now prove the tame source bounds
(
5.8
)–(
5.9
).
Fix
k\geq 6
and set
m:=k+2
.

Step 0:
L^{\infty}
control from slice energies.

By Sobolev embedding on
\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}}
(Lemma
5.1
on
\{r\leq R\}
together with Lemma
4.1
in the far region),
for every
\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
one has

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\mathcal{E}_{N,k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}[A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})\leq\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}+\|D\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{M,a,k}\mathcal{E}_{N,k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}[\phi,A](\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})\leq\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Step 1: the current
J_{\nu}
.

Since
J_{\nu}=2\,\Im(\overline{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi)
, for any multiindex
\alpha
with
|\alpha|\leq m
we commute and expand:

\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}J_{\nu}=2\,\Im\!\!\sum_{\beta+\gamma=\alpha}\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi}\,D_{\nu}(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)\;+\;\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi],

where
\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi]
collects the commutator contributions from commuting
\mathcal{Z}
through the covariant derivative
D
.
Since
D=\nabla+iA
, each commutator
[D,\mathcal{Z}]
is a first-order operator with coefficients depending on the background and on
\nabla\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{Z}A
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{C}_{\nu,\alpha}[A,\phi]
is a finite sum of terms schematically of the form

(\nabla\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{1}}A)\,(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{2}}\phi)\,(\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{3}}\phi}),\qquad|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\beta_{3}|\leq m-1.

Because
m=k+2
, in every product above at least one factor carries at most
k
commutators and can be estimated in
L^{\infty}
using Step 0.
We therefore obtain the pointwise tame bound, at each point of
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
,

|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}J|\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}+\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}\Bigr)\sum_{|\beta|\leq m}\Bigl(|D\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi|+|\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi|\Bigr).

Multiplying by
r^{(1+\delta)/2}
, squaring, integrating over
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, and summing over
|\alpha|\leq m
(using the definition (
1.29
)) yields

\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}+\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Inserting the Step 0 bounds and recalling
m=k+2
gives (
5.8
).

Step 2: the Higgs forcing
\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}
.

In Lorenz gauge,
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
, hence

\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,\phi+\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

Using
\nabla\phi=D\phi-iA\phi
we have the schematic pointwise bound

|\mathfrak{F}_{\phi}|\lesssim|A|\,|D\phi|+|A|^{2}|\phi|+|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})|.

We estimate the three contributions separately in
\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
.

(i) The term
A\,D\phi
.

Commuting
\mathcal{Z}^{\leq m}
through the product and using Leibniz’ rule yields terms of the form

(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}A)\,D(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)
with
|\beta|+|\gamma|\leq m
,
as well as lower-order commutator terms coming from
[D,\mathcal{Z}]
which have the same schematic structure as in Step 1 and are treated in the same way.
Since
m=k+2
, in each product one factor carries at most
k
commutators.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\|A\,D\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle+\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|D\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Applying Step 0 gives

\|A\,D\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr)\,\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr).

(ii) The cubic term
A^{2}\phi
.

Expanding
\mathcal{Z}^{\leq m}(A^{2}\phi)
by Leibniz’ rule yields products of the form

(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{1}}A)(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta_{2}}A)(\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi)
with
|\beta_{1}|+|\beta_{2}|+|\gamma|\leq m
.
Since
m=k+2
, at least two factors carry at most
k
commutators and can be estimated in
L^{\infty}
by Step 0, giving

\displaystyle\|A^{2}\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}\

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{4}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})

\displaystyle+\Bigl(\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}A(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\,\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Using Step 0 yields the schematic estimate

\displaystyle\|A^{2}\phi\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k}

\displaystyle\Bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\,\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times\Bigl(\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{A})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})+\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\Bigr).

(iii) The potential term
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
.

By Lemma
1.9
, for
|\alpha|\leq m
the commuted quantity
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
is a finite sum of products with at least three factors drawn from
\{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi,\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi}\}
,
multiplied by smooth coefficients which grow at most like
(1+|\phi|^{2N_{P}})
.
Since
m=k+2
, in each product at least two factors carry at most
k
commutators; estimating those factors in
L^{\infty}
and the remaining factor in
L^{2}
yields

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{k,P}\

\displaystyle\Bigl(1+\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{2N_{P}}\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\sup_{\tau^{\prime}\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]}\|\mathcal{Z}^{\leq k}\phi(\tau^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau^{\prime}})}^{4}\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Using Step 0 gives

\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\|_{\mathcal{S}_{m}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})}^{2}\lesssim_{M,a,k,P}\Bigl(1+\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})^{N_{P}}\Bigr)^{2}\,\bigl(\mathbf{E}_{k+4}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})\bigr)^{2}\,\mathbf{F}_{m}^{(\mathrm{H})}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}).

Combining (i)–(iii), and recalling
m=k+2
, yields (
5.9
) with
\mathcal{P}_{k,P}
as in (
4.25
).
∎

Corollary 5.6

(Pointwise decay on compact subsets)
.

Fix
R>r_{+}
and
k\geq 6
.
Let
\psi
solve
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}
and let
G
be a smooth charge-free Maxwell field on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
solving
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
with
\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0
.
Then for every
0<\delta<1
there exists
C=C(M,a,R,k,\delta)
such that for all
(\tau,r,\omega)
with
r\leq R
,

\displaystyle|\psi|+|\nabla\psi|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr),

(5.10)

\displaystyle|\alpha[G]|+|\underline{\alpha}[G]|+|\rho[G]|+|\sigma[G]|
