\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+\tau)^{1-\delta}}\Bigl(E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}\Bigr).

(5.11)

Proof.

Fix
R>r_{+}
.
On the compact subset
\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\}
, a standard Sobolev inequality gives

\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\})}\leq C\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}\|\nabla^{\alpha}f\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{\tau}\cap\{r\leq R\})}.

Applying this with
f=\psi
,
f=\nabla\psi
and with
f
equal to the null components of
G
(in a smooth null frame on
\{r\leq R\}
) shows that the left-hand sides of (
5.10
)–(
5.11
) are bounded by
C\,E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)^{1/2}
and
C\,E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)^{1/2}
, respectively, provided
k\geq 6
.
Taking square roots in Propositions
4.3
and
4.5
yields

E^{N}_{k}[\psi](\tau)^{1/2}\lesssim(1+\tau)^{-1+\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[\psi](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)},

and

E^{N}_{k}[G](\tau)^{1/2}\lesssim(1+\tau)^{-1+\delta}\,E^{N}_{k+2}[G](\tau_{0})^{1/2}+\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}(\tau_{0},\tau)}.

Substituting these bounds into the Sobolev estimate gives (
5.10
)–(
5.11
).
∎

Remark 5.1

(Application to Maxwell–Higgs on Kerr)
.

For a small-data Maxwell–Higgs solution
(F,\phi)
on Kerr with charges
(Q_{e},Q_{m})
, first subtract the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb field and apply the above estimates to the radiative Maxwell remainder
\widetilde{F}
and to the Higgs field
\phi
.
The bounds (
5.10
)–(
5.11
) provide the compact-region decay needed to control the nonlinear source terms in the final-state problem and to justify the definition of the horizon radiation field in the scattering framework.

In this application the Maxwell field is the radiative remainder
\widetilde{F}
after Coulomb subtraction, which satisfies a sourced Maxwell system with current
J(\phi)
.
The source contributions
\|J\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}}
and
\|\mathfrak{F}\|_{\mathcal{S}_{k+2}}
arising in (
5.10
)–(
5.11
) are controlled in the small-data regime by the coupled Maxwell–Higgs energies (Proposition
4.2
and (
4.26
)) together with the nonlinear source estimates used in the bootstrap.

6
Verification of the linear package on subextremal Kerr

In this section we record the linear input needed to apply the framework theorem (Theorem
1.8
) on Kerr backgrounds.
Throughout,
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
denotes the domain of outer communications of a Kerr spacetime with parameters
M>0
and
|a|<M
.

The package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
of Definition
1.4
is a concise way of encoding the linear decay and scattering theory required by the nonlinear bootstrap and final-state argument.
For scalar waves on Kerr, the full subextremal physical-space decay and scattering theory is due to Dafermos–Rodnianski–Shlapentokh-Rothman
[
17
,
10
]
.
For Maxwell fields, boundedness/decay and scattering for the full Maxwell system in the full subextremal range has recently been obtained by Benomio–Teixeira da Costa
[
18
]
, building on the full-subextremal Teukolsky estimates of Shlapentokh-Rothman–Teixeira da Costa
[
19
,
20
]
.
(For earlier results in the very slowly rotating regime see
[
21
]
.)

Proposition 6.1

(Verification of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
on Kerr)
.

Fix
M>0
,
|a|<M
, and an integer
k\geq 0
.
Then the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
of Definition
1.4
holds on the Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
for scalar waves and for the charge-free Maxwell system (after subtraction of the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb field).

Proof.

For scalar waves, the redshift estimate, nondegenerate energy boundedness, integrated local energy decay (Morawetz/ILED), and the Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
hierarchy required in (L1) are established in
[
17
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2]
.
The linear scattering map and well-posedness of the final-state problem, i.e. (L2), are proved in
[
10
, Theorem 8.2.1 and Theorem 9.5.2]
.
For Maxwell fields, after Coulomb subtraction, the corresponding physical-space energy boundedness and decay estimates required in (L1) are proved in
[
18
, Theorem 5.1]
, based on the spin-
\pm 1
Teukolsky estimates of
[
20
, Theorem A]
(and the associated frequency-space analysis
[
19
]
).
The scattering statement (L2) in the charge-free sector follows by combining the Teukolsky scattering theory with the standard tensorial reconstruction of the full Maxwell field.
A detailed correspondence of notation is recorded in Subsection
6.1
.
∎

6.1
A dictionary for the Kerr linear inputs

Proposition
6.1
is stated in the abstract language of the package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
in Definition
1.4
.
To make the interface with the cited linear literature transparent, we record here a short dictionary.
In
[
17
,
10
]
(scalar waves) and
[
18
]
(Maxwell), the linear theory is formulated in terms of concrete physical-space energies, integrated local energy decay (ILED/Morawetz) norms, and Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
hierarchies.
These are precisely the concrete realizations of
\mathcal{X}_{k}
and
\mathcal{S}_{k}
recorded in Lemma
1.7
.

Component of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}

Scalar waves
\square_{g_{M,a}}\psi=\mathfrak{F}

Maxwell (charge-free)
\nabla^{\mu}G_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}

(G1)–(G4) admissible structure

Kerr–star foliation
\{\Sigma_{\tau}\}
, area radius
r
, redshift multiplier
N
, and commutators generated by
T,\Phi
(and a second-order symmetry such as the Carter operator)

same background geometry; commutators taken as Lie derivatives along the same admissible family

(L1) redshift / boundedness

nondegenerate energy boundedness up to
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
and coercive redshift control near the horizons,
[
17
, Thm. 3.1]

nondegenerate energy boundedness for the radiative part after Coulomb subtraction,
[
18
, Thm. 5.1]

(L1) ILED/Morawetz

integrated local energy decay with a trapping weight
w_{\mathrm{trap}}
vanishing only on the photon region,
[
17
, Thm. 3.1]

integrated decay (with trapping degeneration) for the radiative Maxwell field,
[
18
, Thm. 5.1]

(L1)
r^{p}
hierarchy

Dafermos–Rodnianski
r^{p}
estimates in
\{r\gg 1\}
, yielding decay and existence of radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}
,
[
17
, Thm. 3.1]

spin-
\pm 1
Teukolsky
r^{p}
estimates and decay,
[
20
, Thm. A]
, transferred to
G
via reconstruction (see
[
18
]
)

(L2) linear scattering / final-state

isomorphism between order-
k
energy Cauchy data and radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
, with well-posed linear final-state problem,
[
10
, Thm. 8.2.1 & Thm. 9.5.2]

analogous scattering and final-state well-posedness in the charge-free sector, obtained from Teukolsky scattering and reconstruction,
[
20
,
19
]

For the scalar wave equation one may view (L1) as the combination of the global physical-space decay estimates (energy boundedness, ILED/Morawetz, and the
r^{p}
hierarchy) proved in
[
17
, Theorems 3.1–3.2]
, while (L2) is provided by the scattering theory and final-state well-posedness in
[
10
, Theorems 8.2.1 and 9.5.2]
.
For Maxwell fields, the corresponding decay package (after subtraction of the stationary Kerr–Newman Coulomb field) is proved in
[
18
, Theorem 5.1]
, and the extreme-component Teukolsky estimates used there are contained in
[
20
, Theorem A]
.
With these identifications, the abstract estimate (
1.27
) coincides with the concrete estimates in the cited works, up to the harmless equivalence of norms encoded in Lemma
1.7
.

Remark 6.1

(Commutations on Kerr)
.

In the definition of the higher-order energies in
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
one may take
\mathcal{Z}
to be generated by the stationary and axial Killing fields
T
and
\Phi
.
To recover full angular regularity for scalar waves one may additionally allow the Carter operator (or any equivalent second-order symmetry operator); see
[
23
]
.
The cited linear theories are formulated in terms of such commutations.

Remark 6.2

(Coulomb subtraction on Kerr)
.

For Maxwell fields on Kerr, the conserved electric and magnetic charges correspond to a stationary Kerr–Newman
2
–parameter family.
Subtracting this stationary Coulomb field yields a charge-free Maxwell field to which the radiative decay and scattering theory applies.
Since our nonlinear scattering results are formulated in the charge-free (radiative) sector, we do not pursue the additional long-range issues associated with nonzero charges in the coupled Maxwell–Higgs system in this work; see Remark
2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Fix
M>0
and
|a|<M
.
The Kerr exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
equipped with the Kerr–star time function
t^{\star}
from (
1.3
) and the admissible structure of Definition
1.3
is an admissible stationary black-hole exterior.
The Lorenz-gauge formulation and the admissible Kerr Cauchy data spaces are recorded in Section
2
, in particular Definition
2.1
.

Proposition
6.1
provides the linear package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
(for scalar waves and for charge-free Maxwell fields) on
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
.
Therefore Theorem
1.8
applies on
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
in the massless regime
m=0
. In the massive case
m^{2}>0
the same nonlinear transfer argument applies provided one has the corresponding linear estimate/scattering package for the massive Klein–Gordon equation on Kerr; see Theorem
1.1
and Remark
1.4
.
Relabelling the resulting scattering maps and wave operators by the Kerr parameters yields the existence, uniqueness, scattering, and homeomorphism statements (i)–(iii) of Theorem
1.1
.

The gauge-quotient property (iv) is part of Theorem
1.8
once the quotient phase space has been fixed (Definition
1.2
); see also Corollary
1.6
.
The tangency, quadratic/Born expansion, and real-analyticity statements (v)–(vii) follow from the corresponding structural conclusions in Theorem
1.8
together with Propositions
1.10
,
1.11
, and
1.12
(and Corollary
1.13
for the analyticity of the inverses).
∎

7
Schwarzschild model: gauge fixing and initial data

In this and the following sections we specialise to the Schwarzschild exterior
a=0
in order to keep the computations explicit.
We pursue two complementary goals.
First, we give a self-contained proof of the linear estimate package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
of Definition
1.4
for scalar waves and charge-free Maxwell fields on Schwarzschild.
Second, we implement the nonlinear bootstrap and final-state argument yielding Theorem
1.8
and, moreover, the stronger Schwarzschild-only wave-operator theorem allowing
m^{2}\geq 0
(Theorem
1.14
).
The Kerr theorem then follows by combining Theorem
1.8
with the verification of
\mathsf{Lin}_{k}
on Kerr recorded in Section
6
.

7.1
Geometry, frames, and notational conventions

Throughout we use the Regge–Wheeler tortoise coordinate
r^{\ast}
defined by
\frac{\mathrm{d}r^{\ast}}{\mathrm{d}r}=(1-\frac{2M}{r})^{-1}
and the double-null coordinates

v=t+r^{\ast},\qquad w=t-r^{\ast}.

We set
v_{+}:=\max\{1,v\}
,
w_{+}:=\max\{1,|w|\}
, and
\mu:=\frac{2M}{r}
.

We use the standard orthonormal frame adapted to Schwarzschild time slices:

\hat{t}:=\Bigl(1-\frac{2M}{r}\Bigr)^{-1/2}\partial_{t},\qquad\hat{r}:=\Bigl(1-\frac{2M}{r}\Bigr)^{1/2}\partial_{r},

and we let
\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2}
be an orthonormal frame tangent to the spheres
S_{t,r}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
(scaled by
r^{-1}
so that
|\hat{e}_{a}|_{g}=1
).
We also introduce the associated (future-directed) null frame

\hat{v}:=\hat{t}+\hat{r},\qquad\hat{w}:=\hat{t}-\hat{r}.

For any tensor
U
, we write
U_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}
for its components in the frame
(\hat{t},\hat{r},\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2})
, and similarly
D_{\hat{v}}\phi:=D\phi(\hat{v})
, etc.

Unless otherwise stated,
\nabla
denotes the Levi–Civita connection of
g
,
D
the gauge-covariant derivative, and
\not{\nabla}
the induced Levi–Civita connection on the spheres
S_{t,r}
.
For nonnegative quantities
A,B
we write
A\lesssim B
if
A\leq CB
for a constant
C
depending only on
M
and the fixed differentiation order (and, when relevant, on finitely many bounds for the potential
P
); the value of
C
may change from line to line.

A Schwarzschild notation dictionary for the vector-field method.

The Schwarzschild model sections (Sections
8
–
10.6
) repeatedly use the following objects.

•

Hypersurfaces and slabs.

We write
\Sigma_{t}:=\{t=\mathrm{const}\}
for the standard Schwarzschild slices.
For
t_{1}\leq t_{2}
we write
\mathcal{R}(t_{1},t_{2})
for the spacetime slab bounded by
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
,
\Sigma_{t_{2}}
, and (where appropriate) portions of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
and
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
We also use double-null hypersurfaces
\{v=\mathrm{const}\}
and
\{w=\mathrm{const}\}
(with
v=t+r^{\ast}
,
w=t-r^{\ast}
) in the far region.

•

Null and angular derivatives.

In the orthonormal/null frame of Section
7.1
we set
D_{\hat{v}}\phi:=D\phi(\hat{v})
and
D_{\hat{w}}\phi:=D\phi(\hat{w})
.
We denote by
\not{\nabla}
the Levi–Civita connection on the spheres
S_{t,r}
and by
\not{D}
the induced gauge-covariant angular derivative.

•

Currents and bulk terms.

Given a vector field
X
, we define the associated energy current and bulk density

J^{X}_{\mu}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,X^{\nu},\qquad K^{X}[F,\phi]:=\nabla^{\mu}J^{X}_{\mu}[F,\phi]=\tfrac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\pi^{\mu\nu}(X).

Applying the divergence theorem to
J^{X}
on
\mathcal{R}(t_{1},t_{2})
yields the standard identity relating boundary fluxes and spacetime bulk terms.

•

Main multipliers.

We use the stationary Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
for conserved energies,
a redshift multiplier
\hat{H}
near
\mathcal{H}^{+}
(Definition
8.1
),
a conformal/Morawetz multiplier
\hat{K}
controlling trapping (Definition
8.5
),
and auxiliary radial multipliers of the form
X_{f}=f(r^{\ast})\partial_{r^{\ast}}
(Definition
8.3
).

•

Commutations.

Unless explicitly stated otherwise, higher-order quantities are obtained by commuting with
\mathcal{Z}:=\{\partial_{t},\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}\}
; see (
1.39
).

7.2
Initial data, gauge choice, and smallness hypothesis

To give a precise meaning to the smallness condition (
1.40
) and to justify estimates involving the potential
A
, we fix a gauge and specify an admissible class of initial data on a Cauchy hypersurface.
Let
\Sigma_{t_{0}}=\{t=t_{0}\}\subset\mathcal{D}
be the standard Schwarzschild slice, equipped with induced Riemannian metric
\gamma
and volume form
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}
, and let
n
denote the future unit normal to
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.

Lorenz gauge.

Throughout the paper we work in the Lorenz gauge

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0.

(7.1)

Since Schwarzschild is Ricci-flat, in this gauge the Maxwell–Higgs system reduces to the quasilinear hyperbolic system

\displaystyle\square_{g}A_{\nu}

\displaystyle=J_{\nu},

(7.2)

\displaystyle D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi

\displaystyle=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}).

(7.3)

Let
E_{i}:=F(n,e_{i})
denote the electric field relative to
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
The Maxwell equation implies the Gauss constraint on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,

\nabla^{i}E_{i}=-\,J_{\mu}n^{\mu}.

(7.4)

Constraint compatibility.

We impose on the initial data the standard Lorenz compatibility conditions

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\big|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}(\nabla_{n}A_{\mu})\big|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\nabla^{i}E_{i}

\displaystyle=-\,J_{\mu}n^{\mu}.

(7.5)

Standard hyperbolic constraint propagation in Lorenz gauge implies that these conditions persist on the domain of existence.

Charge-free condition.

In addition to the constraints above, we assume the charge-free hypothesis of Definition
1.1
.
This excludes the stationary Coulomb solutions, which do not decay and are known to obstruct pointwise decay for Maxwell fields.

Admissible initial data.

An admissible initial data set consists of

\left(A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\;\nabla_{n}A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\;\phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\;D_{n}\phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\right),

(7.6)

smooth and satisfying the above compatibility conditions up to order
k
and with finite initial energy
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
(Definition
1.39
).

Local well-posedness and continuation.

In Lorenz gauge the system is quasilinear hyperbolic on a fixed background.
For admissible data with finite
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
, standard hyperbolic theory yields a unique local solution on a time interval
[t_{0},T)
.
Moreover, the solution extends beyond
T
provided

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},T)}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)<\infty.

(7.7)

Therefore, global existence follows once a uniform bound for
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
is established under the smallness condition (
1.40
).

8
Schwarzschild model: energy identities and integrated decay estimates

Organization of the multiplier computations.

The vector-field multipliers and divergence identities used below (redshift, Morawetz/trapping, and conformal energies) are standard in the physical-space approach to black hole decay.
For readability, we state the estimates in a modular way as propositions and highlight the logical dependencies needed for the nonlinear argument; longer component expansions are collected in the appendices referenced in the proofs.

8.1
Basic energy identity

In this section, we derive the fundamental energy identity for the
Maxwell–Higgs system in the Lorenz gauge (
7.1
). All computations are performed for smooth solutions

(A,\phi)
of the gauge-fixed system satisfying the constraint
equations stated in Section
7.2
. The Maxwell–Higgs energy-momentum tensor associated to the Lagrangian
(
1.4
) is given by

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]

\displaystyle=F_{\mu\alpha}F_{\nu}{}^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}F_{\alpha\beta}F^{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\Re\!\left(D_{\mu}\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\right)-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\Bigl(|D\phi|^{2}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr).

(8.1)

This tensor is gauge invariant and depends only on
(F,\phi)
.
For solutions of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
), it satisfies the
divergence identity

\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]=0.

(8.2)

Let
X
be a vector field. We define the associated energy current

J_{\mu}^{X}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]X^{\nu}.

(8.3)

Then

\nabla^{\mu}J_{\mu}^{X}=T_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{(\mu}X^{\nu)}=\frac{1}{2}T_{\mu\nu}\pi^{\mu\nu}(X),

(8.4)

where
\pi^{\mu\nu}(X)=\nabla^{\mu}X^{\nu}+\nabla^{\nu}X^{\mu}

is the deformation tensor of
X
. Let
\mathcal{R}
be a spacetime region bounded by spacelike and null hypersurfaces.
Applying the divergence theorem to
J^{X}
yields

\int_{\partial\mathcal{R}}J_{\mu}^{X}n^{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{R}}T_{\mu\nu}\nabla^{(\mu}X^{\nu)}.

(8.5)

In particular, if
X=T:=\partial_{t}
(the stationary Killing field of Schwarzschild),
then
\pi^{\mu\nu}(T)=0
, and we obtain the conserved energy identity

E^{T}[F,\phi](t_{2})=E^{T}[F,\phi](t_{1}).

(8.6)

Here the
T
–energy on a slice
\Sigma_{t}
is defined by

E^{T}[F,\phi](t):=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}J_{\mu}^{T}[F,\phi]\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}}=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,T^{\nu}\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}}.

(8.7)

In the standard Schwarzschild orthonormal frame
(\hat{t},\hat{r},\hat{e}_{1},\hat{e}_{2})
(Section
7.1
),
this flux is coercive in the form

E^{T}[F,\phi](t)\simeq\int_{\Sigma_{t}}(1-\mu)\,r^{2}\Bigl(\sum_{\hat{\mu}<\hat{\nu}}|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}|^{2}+|D_{\hat{t}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{r}}\phi|^{2}+|\not{D}\phi|^{2}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}r^{\ast}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma^{2},

(8.8)

where
|\not{D}\phi|^{2}:=|D_{\hat{e}_{1}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{e}_{2}}\phi|^{2}
and
\simeq
denotes equivalence up to constants depending only on
M
.
In particular,
E^{T}
is nonnegative but degenerates at the horizon because
1-\mu
vanishes at
r=2M
.
Since the energy-momentum tensor depends only on
(F,\phi)
,
the above identity is gauge invariant.
Moreover, because the Lorenz gauge condition and the Gauss constraint
propagate under the evolution (see Section
7.2
),
the conserved energy identity is consistent with the gauge-fixed
formulation used throughout the paper.

The rotation fields
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}
are Killing vector fields of the Schwarzschild spacetime.
In particular,

\nabla^{(\mu}\Omega^{\nu)}=0.

(8.9)

Since the rotation fields
\Omega_{j}
are Killing, no
metric
commutator
errors arise when commuting with geometric operators. For the gauge-covariant wave operator
\square_{A}:=D^{\mu}D_{\mu}
, the commutator
produces only lower-order terms coming from the curvature
F
. More precisely, for any smooth scalar
\psi
one has schematically

\square_{A}(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\psi)=\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}(\square_{A}\psi)+\mathcal{O}\!\left(F\cdot D\psi\right)+\mathcal{O}\!\left((DF)\cdot\psi\right),

(8.10)

and, for the Maxwell equation with source
J(\phi)
,

\nabla^{\mu}(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F)_{\mu\nu}=\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}J_{\nu}(\phi),

(8.11)

so that the commuted pair
(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F,\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\phi)

satisfies the Maxwell–Higgs system modulo nonlinear lower-order terms.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a smooth solution of (
1.5
)–(
1.6
).
Then the commuted pair
(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F,\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\phi)
satisfies the Maxwell–Higgs system modulo nonlinear commutator terms of lower order. In particular, the associated energy-momentum tensor satisfies

\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F,\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\phi]=\text{(lower order nonlinear terms)}.

(8.12)

Since the vector fields
\Omega_{j}
are Killing, the deformation tensor vanishes,
and applying the energy identity with
X=T
yields

E^{T}[\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F,\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\phi](t_{2})=E^{T}[\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}F,\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\phi](t_{1})+\int_{\mathcal{R}}\text{(controlled commutator terms)}.

(8.13)

The spacetime integral on the right-hand side consists of lower-order terms
of the schematic form
|F|\,|D\phi|^{2}+|DF|\,|\phi|\,|D\phi|
(and similar),
which are controlled by the bootstrap assumptions together with the Morawetz
and redshift estimates.
Thus, the higher-order energies defined in (
1.39
) are propagated along the evolution and can be estimated using the same vector-field method.

We now introduce the bootstrap energy

\mathcal{E}:=\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t),

(8.14)

where
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
is defined in (
1.39
). Under the smallness assumption
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
,
we will show below that
\mathcal{E}
remains uniformly bounded by the initial energy.

8.2
The Red-shift Estimates

Dyadic time decomposition.

Fix once and for all a parameter
0<\kappa_{0}<1
and define
t_{i}:=(1+\kappa_{0})^{i}t_{0}
, so that
t_{i+1}=(1+\kappa_{0})t_{i}
. This
\kappa_{0}
is used only for the timelike–cone localizations in the Schwarzschild model and is unrelated to the loss parameter
\delta>0
used in the Kerr sections and in the abstract norms.
The Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
becomes null at the event horizon
r=2M
, and the associated conserved
T
–energy therefore degenerates in a neighborhood of the horizon. This degeneration reflects the fact that
E^{T}
does not control derivatives transversal to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
. The geometric
redshift effect
yields a nondegenerate energy estimate in a fixed neighborhood of the horizon, obtained by using a timelike multiplier that is transversal to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Throughout this section we denote
\mu:=\frac{2M}{r}
.
Fix numbers
2M<r_{0}<r_{1}<3M
with
r_{0}
sufficiently close to
2M
, and let
\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}:[2M,\infty)\to[0,1]
be a smooth cutoff satisfying

\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}(r)=1\quad\text{for }2M\leq r\leq r_{0},\qquad\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}(r)=0\quad\text{for }r\geq r_{1}.

(We will always choose
r_{1}
small enough so that
r_{1}<3M
; in particular the support of
\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}
is contained in a near-horizon region.)

Definition 8.1
.

(Redshift multiplier.)

Work in advanced Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates
(v,r,\omega)
, where
v=t+r^{\ast}
and
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
In these coordinates the stationary Killing field is
T=\partial_{v}
and extends smoothly to the horizon.
Define the redshift vector field

\hat{H}:=T-\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}(r)\,\partial_{r},

(8.15)

where
\partial_{r}
is the coordinate vector field in the Eddington–Finkelstein chart.
Then
\hat{H}
is smooth, future-directed and timelike on
2M\leq r\leq r_{1}
, and
\hat{H}=T
for
r\geq r_{1}
.

The key point is that, in the region where
\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}\equiv 1
, the deformation tensor
\pi(\hat{H})
has a coercive sign due to the positive surface gravity of the Schwarzschild horizon; see e.g.
[
4
]
(and references therein). We now record the flux quantities needed for the dyadic argument.

Definition 8.2
.

(Null fluxes of the redshift current.)

Let
w=t-r^{\ast}
and
v=t+r^{\ast}
.
For
w_{i}
fixed and
v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}
, define the redshift flux across the outgoing null hypersurface
\{w=w_{i}\}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr):=\int_{v_{i}}^{v_{i+1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}J_{\mu}^{\hat{H}}[F,\phi]\,(\partial_{v})^{\mu}\,r^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,dv.

(8.16)

Similarly, for
v_{i}
fixed and
w_{i}\leq w\leq w_{i+1}
, define the flux across the ingoing null hypersurface
\{v=v_{i}\}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v_{i}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq w_{i+1}\bigr):=\int_{w_{i}}^{w_{i+1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}J_{\mu}^{\hat{H}}[F,\phi]\,(\partial_{w})^{\mu}\,r^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,dw.

(8.17)

By the dominant energy condition for
T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]
, these null fluxes are nonnegative.

Applying the divergence theorem to
J^{\hat{H}}
in a characteristic rectangle
[w_{i},w_{i+1}]\times[v_{i},v_{i+1}]
yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v_{i}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq w_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=w_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq w_{i+1}\bigr)+I^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq w_{i+1}\bigr).

(8.18)

where the bulk term is

I^{\hat{H}}(\cdots):=\int_{\mathcal{R}}K^{\hat{H}}[F,\phi]\,\text{dVol},\qquad K^{\hat{H}}[F,\phi]:=\frac{1}{2}\,T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,\pi^{\mu\nu}(\hat{H}),

(8.19)

and
\mathcal{R}
denotes the corresponding spacetime rectangle.
When
w_{i+1}=\infty
, the boundary term
\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}(w=\infty)
is the flux through the future event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
Additionally, we have the following estimates:

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
(w_{i},v_{i})
be such that
r(w_{i},v_{i})=r_{1}
and let
v_{i+1}\geq v_{i}
.
Then, on the outgoing null segment
\{w=w_{i},\ v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\}\subset\{r\geq r_{1}\}
, the redshift multiplier coincides with
T
, and hence

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)=\mathcal{E}^{T}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr).

(8.20)

Proof.

By construction
\chi_{\mathrm{hor}}(r)=0
for
r\geq r_{1}
, so
\hat{H}=T
on
\{r\geq r_{1}\}
.
Along
w=w_{i}
with
v\geq v_{i}
one has
r(w_{i},v)\geq r(w_{i},v_{i})=r_{1}
, and the claim follows from Definitions
8.2
.
∎

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{T}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr):=\int_{v_{i}}^{v_{i+1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}J_{\mu}^{T}[F,\phi]\,(\partial_{v})^{\mu}\,r^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,dv.

(8.21)

To control the estimate of
I^{\hat{H}}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}
we use, in addition to the redshift multiplier, auxiliary
radial
multipliers.

Definition 8.3

(Radial multipliers)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth bounded function of the tortoise coordinate
r^{\ast}
.
Define the associated radial vector field

X_{f}:=f(r^{\ast})\,\partial_{r^{\ast}}=-f(r^{\ast})\,\partial_{w}+f(r^{\ast})\,\partial_{v}.

(8.22)

We will use two fixed choices of profile, denoted
f_{C}
and
f_{G}
, and we write

\hat{C}:=X_{f_{C}},\qquad\hat{G}:=X_{f_{G}}.

(The two letters refer to different choices of
f
tailored to different estimates; the underlying radial form (
8.22
) is the same.)

Definition 8.4
.

The quantity
E^{\hat{C}}(t)
is the flux of the current
J^{\hat{C}}
through the time slice
\Sigma_{t}
:

E^{\hat{C}}(t):=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}J_{\mu}^{\hat{C}}[F,\phi]\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}},\qquad J_{\mu}^{\hat{C}}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,\hat{C}^{\nu}.

(8.23)

We will often localize the fields on the slice
\Sigma_{t_{i}}
to the timelike-cone region
|r^{\ast}|\lesssim t_{i}
using a smooth cutoff.

Fix a function
\hat{\chi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\hat{\chi}\equiv 1
on
[-1,1]
and
\hat{\chi}\equiv 0
on
\mathbb{R}\setminus[-\tfrac{3}{2},\tfrac{3}{2}]
.
For each dyadic time
t_{i}
define the rescaled cutoff

\hat{\chi}_{i}(r^{\ast}):=\hat{\chi}\!\left(\frac{2r^{\ast}}{t_{i}}\right).

On the slice
t=t_{i}
we define localized data

\hat{F}:=\hat{\chi}_{i}\,F\big|_{t=t_{i}},\qquad\hat{\phi}:=\hat{\chi}_{i}\,\phi\big|_{t=t_{i}}.

(8.24)

Thus
\hat{F}
and
\hat{\phi}
vanish for
|r^{\ast}|\geq\tfrac{3}{4}t_{i}
and coincide with
(F,\phi)
for
|r^{\ast}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}t_{i}
.
(The multiplication is understood componentwise in any fixed regular frame; since
\hat{\chi}_{i}
depends only on
r^{\ast}
, it generates only cutoff error terms supported where
\tfrac{1}{2}t_{i}\leq|r^{\ast}|\leq\tfrac{3}{4}t_{i}
.)

We now state the middle-region estimate used in the dyadic propagation argument.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
t_{i+1}=(1+\kappa_{0})t_{i}
.
Define the following middle-region spacetime functional:

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\hat{C}}(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1})

\displaystyle:=\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\!\int_{r_{0}^{\ast}}^{R_{0}^{\ast}}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}

(8.25)

\displaystyle\qquad\Bigl(|F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|^{2}+|F_{\hat{\theta}\hat{\varphi}}|^{2}+|D_{\hat{v}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{w}}\phi|^{2}+|\not{D}\phi|^{2}+P(\phi)\Bigr)\,\bigl|r^{\ast}-(3M)^{\ast}\bigr|

(8.26)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\ d\sigma^{2}\,dr^{\ast}\,dt.

(8.27)

Then for radii
2M<r_{0}\leq r_{1}<1.2\,r_{1}<3M
we have the localized estimate

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}(1-\mu)\Bigl(|\hat{F}_{\hat{t}A}|^{2}+|\hat{F}_{\hat{r}^{\ast}A}|^{2}+|D_{\hat{t}}\phi|^{2}+|\not{D}\phi|^{2}+P(\phi)\Bigr)\,\chi_{\mathrm{mid}}(r^{\ast})\,dr^{\ast}\,d\sigma^{2}

(8.28)

\displaystyle\ \lesssim\ E^{\hat{t}}_{\mathrm{cone}}(t_{i}),

(8.29)

where
\chi_{\mathrm{mid}}
denotes a smooth cutoff supported in the interval between
r_{1}^{\ast}
and
1.2\,r_{1}^{\ast}
, i.e. supported in
[\min\{r_{1}^{\ast},1.2\,r_{1}^{\ast}\},\,\max\{r_{1}^{\ast},1.2\,r_{1}^{\ast}\}]
(and equal to
1
on a slightly smaller subinterval).
We use the shorthand

|\hat{F}_{\hat{t}A}|^{2}:=|\hat{F}_{\hat{t}\hat{\theta}}|^{2}+|\hat{F}_{\hat{t}\hat{\varphi}}|^{2},\qquad|\hat{F}_{\hat{r}^{\ast}A}|^{2}:=|\hat{F}_{\hat{r}^{\ast}\hat{\theta}}|^{2}+|\hat{F}_{\hat{r}^{\ast}\hat{\varphi}}|^{2},\qquad|\not{D}\phi|^{2}:=|D_{\hat{\theta}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{\varphi}}\phi|^{2}.

Moreover,

E^{\hat{t}}_{\mathrm{cone}}(t_{i}):=\begin{aligned} E^{\hat{t}}\Bigl(&t=t_{i};\\
&r^{\ast}\in\bigl[(1-\kappa_{0})t_{i},(1+\kappa_{0})t_{i}\bigr]\Bigr).\end{aligned}

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.3
.
∎

The next Proposition provides the estimate needed to control
I^{\hat{H}}
with respect to the flux of
\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}
.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
w_{i}:=t_{i}-r_{1}^{\ast}
and
v_{i}:=t_{i}+r_{1}^{\ast}
. Then, for every
v_{i+1}\geq v_{i}
,

\displaystyle I^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigl(r\leq r_{1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=\infty\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{E}^{\hat{t}}\bigl(w=w_{i}\bigr)\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v_{i}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad+E^{\hat{t}}_{\mathrm{cone}}(t_{i}).

(8.30)

Proof.

Equation (
8.3
) is a direct consequence of (
8.19
) and (
8.20
).
∎

Proposition
8.3
provides a decomposition of the spacetime integral into boundary and error contributions along null hypersurfaces. Using this structure, we can propagate the energy estimate forward in
v
, leading to the following bound on the energy flux at
v_{i+1}

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
w_{i}:=t_{i}-r_{1}^{\ast}
and
v_{i}:=t_{i}+r_{1}^{\ast}
. For every
v_{i+1}\geq v_{i}
we have

\displaystyle\inf_{v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}}\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)

\displaystyle\ \lesssim\ \frac{1}{v_{i+1}-v_{i}}\,I^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\bigl(r\leq r_{1}\bigr)+\sup_{v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}}\mathcal{E}^{\hat{t}}\bigl(v=v\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)\bigl(r\geq r_{1}\bigr).

(8.31)

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.4
.
∎

The error term isolated in Proposition
8.3
and propagated in Proposition
8.4
can be estimated using the positivity of the associated bulk integral. In particular, the next result shows that
I^{\hat{H}}
is controlled by the initial weighted energy on the hypersurface
t=t_{0}
.

Proposition 8.5
.

For
w_{i}=t_{i}-r_{1}^{\ast}
and
v_{i}=t_{i}+r_{1}^{\ast}
, the redshift bulk term satisfies

0\leq I^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v_{i}\leq v\leq v_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(w_{i}\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)\bigl(r\leq r_{1}\bigr)\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E},

(8.32)

where
\mathcal{E}=\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
is the bootstrap energy defined above.

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.5
.
∎

8.3
Morawetz estimates (Integrated Local Energy Decay)

Although decay can be obtained in neighborhoods of the event horizon and in the asymptotically flat region, these estimates do not control the intermediate region near the photon sphere
r=3M
. The obstruction arises from trapped null geodesics, which allow energy to remain localized and prevent dispersion. To address this issue, we introduce the conformal vector field
{\hat{K}}
, whose associated current yields integrated local energy decay away from the trapping region. The degeneration of this estimate at
r=3M
gives rise to a trapping contribution, which we isolate and control below.

Definition 8.5

(Conformal/Morawetz multiplier)
.

We introduce the standard conformal (Morawetz) vector field

\hat{K}:=-\,w^{2}\,\partial_{w}-v^{2}\,\partial_{v}.

(8.33)

Definition 8.6

(Morawetz bulk term)
.

The Morawetz bulk term associated to
\hat{K}
on a time slab
\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}
is

\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}\bigl(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}\bigr):=\int_{\mathcal{R}_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}}K^{\hat{K}}[F,\phi]\,\text{dVol},\qquad K^{\hat{K}}[F,\phi]:=\frac{1}{2}\,T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,\pi^{\mu\nu}(\hat{K}).

(8.34)

Definition 8.7

(Conformal energy flux)
.

The associated (conformal) energy flux through a slice
\Sigma_{t}
is

E^{\hat{K}}(t):=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}J_{\mu}^{\hat{K}}[F,\phi]\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}},\qquad J_{\mu}^{\hat{K}}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,\hat{K}^{\nu}.

(8.35)

Combining the propagation estimate of Proposition
8.4
with the control of the error term established in Proposition
8.5
, and applying the inequality iteratively, we are now in a position to derive quantitative decay energy estimates. This leads to the following result.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
w_{0}(v):=v-2r_{1}^{\ast}
denote the
w
–coordinate of the intersection point of the ingoing null ray
\{v=\mathrm{const}\}
with the hypersurface
\{r=r_{1}\}
, and set
v_{+}:=\max\{1,v\}
.
Then, for all
v\geq v_{0}
,

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(v=v\bigr)\bigl(w_{0}(v)\leq w\leq\infty\bigr)\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}}{v_{+}^{2}},

(8.36)

and, for all
w
and
v\geq v_{0}
,

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}\bigl(w=w\bigr)\bigl(v-1\leq\bar{v}\leq v\bigr)\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}}{v_{+}^{2}}.

(8.37)

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.6
.
∎

Remark.

The conformal multiplier
\hat{K}
is introduced to capture polynomial time-weights, but the associated bulk term
K^{\hat{K}}[F,\phi]
(and hence
\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}
) is
not
sign-definite globally on the Schwarzschild exterior. In particular, one should not attempt to extract a positive spacetime integral from (
C.1
) in the full region
2M<r<\infty
without further localization/corrections.

In this work we avoid this issue by restricting all conformal/Morawetz estimates to a fixed compact region

\{\,r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{0}\,\},\qquad 2M<r_{0}<3M<R_{0},

and we control the near-horizon region
\{2M<r\leq r_{0}\}
by the redshift multiplier
\hat{H}
(Section
8.2
and Proposition
8.6
), while the far region
\{r\geq R_{0}\}
is treated by the
r^{p}
–weighted hierarchy. This is standard and eliminates the need to track the pointwise sign of each term in the explicit expression (
C.1
).

To control the estimate of
\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}
and
E^{\hat{K}}
we also use an auxiliary radial multiplier

\hat{G}:=X_{f_{G}}

of the form (
8.22
), where
f_{G}
is chosen so that the associated bulk term is nonnegative on the compact region
\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{0}\}
; see Definition
8.8
.

To accomplish this, it is necessary to have the space-time integral
\mathcal{J}^{\hat{G}}
, which is referred to as the functional energy and contains positive terms.

Definition 8.8

(Auxiliary positive bulk functional)
.

On a time slab
t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
we introduce the nonnegative functional

\mathcal{J}^{\hat{G}}\bigl(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}\bigr):=\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\int_{r^{\ast}=r_{0}^{\ast}}^{r^{\ast}=R_{0}^{\ast}}\int_{S^{2}}\Bigl(|F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|^{2}+|F_{\hat{\theta}\hat{\varphi}}|^{2}+|D_{\hat{v}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{w}}\phi|^{2}\Bigr)\,r^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\sigma^{2}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\ast}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(8.38)

Definition 8.9
.

The quantity
E^{\hat{G}}(t)
is the flux of the current
J^{\hat{G}}
through the time slice
\Sigma_{t}
:

E^{\hat{G}}(t):=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}J_{\mu}^{\hat{G}}[F,\phi]\,n_{\Sigma_{t}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t}},\qquad J_{\mu}^{\hat{G}}[F,\phi]:=T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,\hat{G}^{\nu}.

(8.39)

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}
be as in Definition
8.6
. Fix dyadic times
t_{i+1}=(1+\kappa_{0})t_{i}
and assume that the compact region
\{r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{0}\}
lies inside the timelike cone on
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
in the sense that

|r_{0}^{\ast}|+|R_{0}^{\ast}|\leq\kappa_{0}\,t_{i}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}\bigl(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim t_{i+1}\,\mathcal{J}^{\hat{G}}\bigl(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}\bigr)\bigl(r_{0}\leq r\leq R_{0}\bigr)

\displaystyle\lesssim t_{i+1}^{2}\Biggl[\frac{1}{t_{i}^{2}}\,E^{\hat{K}}(t_{i})+\frac{1}{t_{i}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}E_{\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}}^{\hat{K}}(t_{i})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{t_{i+1}^{2}}\,E^{\hat{K}}(t_{i+1})+\frac{1}{t_{i+1}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}E_{\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}}^{\hat{K}}(t_{i+1})\Biggr].

(8.40)

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.7
.
∎

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
E^{\hat{K}}(t)
be as in Definition
8.7
.
Assume
t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}
and
|r_{0}^{\ast}|+|R_{0}^{\ast}|\leq\kappa_{0}\,t_{i}
.
Then

E^{\hat{K}}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}(t).

(8.41)

Proof.

The detailed proof is deferred to Appendix
C.8
.
∎

Lemma 8.9

(Nonlinear commutator/source error control)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and let
(F,\phi)
be a smooth charge-free Lorenz-gauge solution on Schwarzschild on a slab
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t)
.
Set

\mathcal{E}:=\sup_{s\in[t_{0},t]}\mathcal{E}_{k}(s),

where
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is defined in (
1.39
).
Let
\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi]
denote the spacetime error density produced by (i) commuting the Maxwell–Higgs system with
\mathcal{Z}=\{\partial_{t},\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}\}
up to order
k
and (ii) applying the divergence identity (
8.6
) to the commuted fields.
Then there exists a constant
C=C(M,k,N_{P},\{C_{P,j}\}_{0\leq j\leq k})
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\int_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t)}\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi]\leq C\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(8.42)

In particular, if
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
is sufficiently small (depending on
C
), then the nonlinear commutator/source contribution is perturbative and can be absorbed in the global bootstrap closure; see Proposition
8.10
.

Proof.

We sketch the standard estimate underlying (
8.42
); the key point is that all nonlinearities are at least quadratic and contain at most one factor with
k
derivatives. (For the scalar equation we write
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=m^{2}\phi+(\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi)
with
m^{2}
, treating
m^{2}\phi
as part of the linear Klein–Gordon operator and
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi
as a nonlinearity of order
\geq 3
in
\phi
via (
1.14
); commuted versions are controlled by Lemma
1.9
.)
After commutation with
\mathcal{Z}
, the Maxwell equation has the schematic form

\nabla^{\mu}\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F_{\mu\nu}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta+\gamma=\alpha\\
|\beta|<|\alpha|\end{subarray}}\bigl(\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi\bigr)\,\overline{D_{\nu}\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi}+\bigl(D_{\nu}\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}\phi\bigr)\,\overline{\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}\phi},

and the scalar equation has a similar schematic structure with terms involving
F
and lower-order derivatives of
\phi
, as well as the potential term
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
. Using (
1.13
)–(
1.14
), we treat
m^{2}\phi
as part of the linear comparison operator and the remainder
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi
as a nonlinearity of order
\geq 3
in
\phi
.
When inserted into the divergence identity (
8.6
), every contribution to
\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi]
is therefore a sum of integrands of the form

(\text{lower-order coefficient})\times(\text{top-order energy density}),

for instance
|\phi|\,|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}
,
|D\phi|\,|\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|\,|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|
, or
|F|\,|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}
, and the analogous Maxwell terms.

We estimate the lower-order coefficients in
L^{\infty}
and the top-order factors in
L^{1}
using the redshift/Morawetz bulk integrals.
More precisely, since
k\geq 6
, Sobolev on
S^{2}
(applied to angular derivatives up to order
2
) and the definition of
\mathcal{E}
imply the uniform bound

\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t))}+\|D\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t))}+\|F\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t))}\lesssim\mathcal{E}^{1/2}.

(Here we use that on each
\Sigma_{s}
, the
L^{2}
-control of sufficiently many commuted derivatives of
(F,\phi)
provided by
\mathcal{E}_{k}(s)
controls the pointwise size via standard Sobolev inequalities on
\Sigma_{s}
, and we then take the supremum in
s\in[t_{0},t]
.)

With this
L^{\infty}
bound, each representative term is controlled as follows:

\int_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t)}|\phi|\,|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}\leq\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t)}|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\int_{\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t)}|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2},

and similarly for the other terms (using
ab\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(a^{2}+b^{2})
when needed).
Summing over
|\alpha|\leq k
, the spacetime integrals of
|D\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi|^{2}
and
|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F|^{2}
are exactly the quantities controlled by the redshift and Morawetz estimates established earlier (Propositions
8.1
–
8.6
and Proposition
8.8
), and hence are bounded by
C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
.
Combining these bounds yields (
8.42
).
∎

Finally, we have the following proposition

Proposition 8.10

(Bootstrap closure)
.

Let
k\geq 6
and define

\mathcal{E}:=\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t).

(8.43)

Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for sufficiently small initial data

\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, the redshift and Morawetz estimates imply

\mathcal{E}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(8.44)

Proof.

We combine the commuted energy identity (
8.6
) with the redshift control
(Propositions
8.1
–
8.6
) and the Morawetz control (Proposition
8.8
). More precisely, applying (
8.6
) to all commuted fields up to order
k
yields

\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})+C\int_{\mathcal{R}_{[t_{0},t]}}\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi],

(8.45)

where
\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi]
denotes the sum of nonlinear commutator/source terms arising from commuting the Maxwell–Higgs system with the vector fields in
\mathcal{Z}
. Proposition
8.6
provides quantitative control/decay of the redshift flux

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}
along null boundaries, while Proposition
8.8
yields the bound
E^{\hat{K}}(t)\lesssim\mathcal{E}
. By Lemma
8.9
, the spacetime error integral satisfies

\int_{\mathcal{R}_{[t_{0},t]}}\mathcal{N}_{k}[F,\phi]\leq C\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(8.46)

Consequently, for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})+C\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(8.47)

Taking the supremum over
t\geq t_{0}
in (
8.47
) gives

\mathcal{E}\leq\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})+C\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(8.48)

Set
A:=\mathcal{E}^{1/2}
.
Then (
8.48
) is equivalent to

A^{2}-C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\,A-\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq 0.

Solving this quadratic inequality yields

A\leq\frac{C\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})+\sqrt{C^{2}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{2}+4\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})}}{2}.

In particular, if
\varepsilon_{0}>0
is chosen so that
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}\leq 1/C^{2}
, then
A\leq 2\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}
and hence

\mathcal{E}\leq 4\,\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

This proves the claim.
∎

9
Schwarzschild model: decay in the far region

In this section we prove the pointwise decay estimates of Corollary
1.16
in the far region
r\geq R
for a fixed sufficiently large radius
R
. The precise value of
R
is not important; it is chosen so that the Morawetz bulk terms obtained in Section
8
have a favourable sign for
r\geq R
.

9.1
Decay for the gauge field

We derive pointwise decay for the (frame) components
F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}
in the far region
\{r\geq R\}
from Sobolev on spheres combined with the
r^{p}
–hierarchy and the conformal energy bounds for commuted fields.

Lemma 9.1

(Spherical Sobolev for
F
)
.

For each fixed
(t,r)
with
r\geq R
and for each pair of frame indices
(\hat{\mu},\hat{\nu})
,

r^{2}\,\bigl|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}(t,r,\omega)\bigr|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \sum_{|I|\leq 2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}(t,r,\omega^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime},

(9.1)

where
\Omega\in\{\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2},\Omega_{3}\}
are the rotational Killing fields and
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}
denotes an arbitrary iterated Lie derivative of order
|I|
.

Proof.

This is the standard Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
, applied to the tensor components of
F
on each sphere
\{t=\mathrm{const},\ r=\mathrm{const}\}
, together with the fact that the
H^{2}
–norm may be computed using the Lie derivatives along rotations.
∎

Lemma 9.2

(Far-region
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
bounds for commuted
F
)
.

For every multi-index
I
with
|I|\leq 2
and every
r\geq R
,

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}F(t,r,\omega)\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}}{t_{+}^{2}}+\frac{\mathcal{E}}{w_{+}^{2}},

(9.2)

where
t_{+}:=\max\{1,|t|\}
and
w_{+}:=\max\{1,|w|\}
.

Proof.

This is a standard consequence of the
r^{p}
–hierarchy and the boundedness of the conformal energy for the commuted fields
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}F
; see Proposition
8.1
together with the
r^{p}
–estimates obtained from the Morawetz identity in Proposition
8.7
.
∎

Combining Lemma
9.1
and Lemma
9.2
yields the pointwise estimate

\bigl|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}(t,r,\omega)\bigr|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{r}\Bigl(\frac{1}{t_{+}}+\frac{1}{w_{+}}\Bigr),\qquad r\geq R.

(9.3)

We now deduce the stated far-region decay. On
\{w\geq 1\}\cap\{t\geq 1\}\cap\{r\geq R\}
, we have
r+t\lesssim rt
and
v=t+r^{\ast}\lesssim r+t
, so (
9.3
) implies

\bigl|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}\bigr|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{v_{+}},\qquad r\geq R,\ w\geq 1.

(9.4)

On the complementary region
\{w\geq 1\}\cap\{t\leq 1\}\cap\{r\geq R\}
we have
|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}|\lesssim\mathcal{E}^{1/2}
by boundedness of the energy and compactness in time, hence

\bigl|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}\bigr|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{v_{+}},\qquad r\geq R,\ w\geq 1,\ t\leq 1.

(9.5)

For
w\leq-1
and
r\geq R
we have
r^{\ast}=\tfrac{1}{2}(v-w)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}(v+1)
and hence
r\gtrsim v_{+}
; inserting this in (
9.3
) again gives the bound
|F_{\hat{\mu}\hat{\nu}}|\lesssim\mathcal{E}^{1/2}/v_{+}
.
Altogether we obtain (
1.41
).

The estimate (
1.42
) follows by the same argument after time reversal (which interchanges
v
and
-w
), and therefore we omit the repetition.

9.2
Decay for the scalar field

The pointwise decay of
\phi
in the far region is obtained exactly as for the gauge field, using spherical Sobolev together with the
r^{p}
–hierarchy for commuted fields.

Lemma 9.3

(Spherical Sobolev for
\phi
)
.

For each fixed
(t,r)
with
r\geq R
,

r^{2}\,|\phi(t,r,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \sum_{|I|\leq 2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}\phi(t,r,\omega^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}.

(9.6)

Lemma 9.4

(Far-region
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
bounds for commuted
\phi
)
.

For every multi-index
I
with
|I|\leq 2
and every
r\geq R
,

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}\phi(t,r,\omega)\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}}{t_{+}^{2}}+\frac{\mathcal{E}}{w_{+}^{2}}.

(9.7)

Proof.

The proof is identical to Lemma
9.2
, using the
r^{p}
–hierarchy for
\phi
and the boundedness of the commuted conformal energies.
∎

Combining Lemma
9.3
and Lemma
9.4
yields

|\phi(t,r,\omega)|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{r}\Bigl(\frac{1}{t_{+}}+\frac{1}{w_{+}}\Bigr),\qquad r\geq R.

(9.8)

As in the proof of (
1.41
), we consider separately the regions
w\geq 1
and
w\leq-1
to conclude

|\phi|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{v_{+}},\qquad r\geq R.

(9.9)

In particular, (
1.43
) follows.

9.3
Decay estimate for
D\phi
in the far region

We now prove the remaining bound (
1.44
) of Corollary
1.16
.
The argument is parallel to the proof of (
1.43
), but applied to the covariant derivative
D\phi
.
Since
D\phi
is a covariant one–form, we apply the Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
componentwise in the stationary frame.
For each fixed
(t,r)
with
r\geq R
we have

r^{2}\,|D\phi(t,r,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \sum_{|I|\leq 2}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}(D\phi)(t,r,\omega^{\prime})\bigr|^{2}\,d\sigma^{\prime}.

(9.10)

Commuting
D
with rotations produces only lower–order curvature terms:
schematically,

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}(D\phi)=D(\Omega^{I}\phi)+\mathcal{O}\!\left((\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{\leq I}F)\,\Omega^{\leq I}\phi\right),\qquad|I|\leq 2,

and the right-hand side is controlled by the higher–order energies
\mathcal{E}_{k}
together with the already established decay for
(F,\phi)
.
In particular, the same conformal–energy argument used above for
\phi
yields, for all
|I|\leq 2
and all
r\geq R
,

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}\,\bigl|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}(D\phi)\bigr|^{2}(t,r,\omega^{\prime})\,d\sigma^{\prime}\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}}{t^{2}}+\frac{\mathcal{E}}{w^{2}},

(9.11)

where
\mathcal{E}:=\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
is the bootstrap energy from Section
8
.
Combining (
9.10
) and (
9.11
) and arguing as in the proof of (
1.43
)
(region splitting into
w\geq 1
,
w\leq-1
, and the compact set
|t|\leq 1
or
|w|\leq 1
)
gives the pointwise decay

|D\phi(w,v,\omega)|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}^{1/2}}{1+v_{+}},\qquad r\geq R.

Finally, using the uniform energy bound
\mathcal{E}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
from Corollary
1.15

yields (
1.44
). This completes the proof of Corollary
1.16
.

10
Schwarzschild model: decay in the trapped and near-horizon region

In this section we prove Corollary
1.17
, establishing pointwise bounds in the trapped and near-horizon region
2M<r\leq 3M
. The proof combines the redshift estimate of Section
8
with a dyadic decomposition in
v
and Sobolev inequalities on spacetime slabs of the form
[v-1,v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

10.1
Decay for
F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}
and
F_{e_{1}e_{2}}

We prove the first part of (
1.45
) in Corollary
1.17
.
Fix
(v,w)
with
2M<r(v,w)\leq 3M
and consider the unit
v
–slab

\{\bar{v}\in[v-1,v]\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
at fixed
w
.
Throughout this section we write
v_{+}:=\max\{1,v\}
.

We repeatedly use the following Sobolev estimate on the cylinder
[v-1,v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Lemma 10.1

(Sobolev on a unit
v
–slab)
.

Let
u
be a smooth scalar function on
\{\bar{v}\in[v-1,v]\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
(at fixed
w
).
Then

|u(v,w,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{v}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(|u|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}u|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}^{2}u|^{2}+|D_{\hat{v}}u|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}D_{\hat{v}}u|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}^{2}D_{\hat{v}}u|^{2}\Bigr)\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v},

(10.1)

where
\not{\nabla}
denotes the Levi–Civita connection on
(\mathbb{S}^{2},r^{2}d\sigma^{2})
and
D_{\hat{v}}
is the
\hat{v}
–directional covariant derivative along the stationary null frame.

Proof.

This is a standard Sobolev embedding
H^{2}([v-1,v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}
, written in a way adapted to the product structure.
A proof can be obtained by combining the Sobolev inequality on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with the fundamental theorem of calculus in the
\bar{v}
variable.
∎

We apply Lemma
10.1
to the scalar components

u=F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}
and
u=F_{e_{1}e_{2}}
.
The terms in (
10.1
) involving only
u
and angular derivatives of
u
are controlled directly by the commuted redshift flux

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}(w=w)(v-1\leq\bar{v}\leq v)
and Proposition
8.6
.
It remains to control the
D_{\hat{v}}u
terms.

For
u=F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}
we use the Maxwell equation
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
with current

J_{\nu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr)
.
In the stationary null frame, this yields the schematic identity

|D_{\hat{v}}F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|\ \lesssim\ |\not{\nabla}F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}|+|F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}|+|D_{\hat{v}}\phi|+|\phi|,

(10.2)

where repeated angular indices
A\in\{1,2\}
are summed.
Similarly, for
u=F_{e_{1}e_{2}}
the Bianchi identity
\nabla_{[\alpha}F_{\beta\gamma]}=0
gives

|D_{\hat{v}}F_{e_{1}e_{2}}|\ \lesssim\ |\not{\nabla}F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}|+|F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}|.

(10.3)

Squaring (
10.2
)–(
10.3
) and integrating over the slab,
all terms are controlled by the commuted redshift fluxes except for the
L^{2}
norm of
\phi
on the slab.

To estimate
\phi
in
L^{2}
we use the gauge–covariant identity

\partial_{w}|\phi|^{2}=2\Re(\bar{\phi}\,D_{\hat{w}}\phi)
(the connection term drops out after taking real part).
Hence, for the slab norm
\|\phi\|_{L^{2}([v-1,v]\times\mathbb{S}^{2})}
at fixed
w
,

\frac{d}{dw}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\ \lesssim\ \Bigl(\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}(w=w)\bigl(v-1\leq\bar{v}\leq v\bigr)\Bigr)^{1/2}.

(10.4)

By Proposition
8.6
we have

\mathcal{E}^{\hat{H}}(w=w)(v-1\leq\bar{v}\leq v)\lesssim v_{+}^{-2}\mathcal{E}
,
hence
\frac{d}{dw}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim v_{+}^{-1}\mathcal{E}^{1/2}
on the near-horizon region.
Integrating in
w
(from any fixed reference value, e.g.
w_{0}(v)
), we obtain

\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim(1+w_{+}/v_{+})\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}
, and therefore

\int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{v}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\phi|^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}\ \lesssim\ \left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{E}.

(10.5)

Combining Lemma
10.1
with the above bounds yields

|F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}(v,w,\omega)|^{2}+|F_{e_{1}e_{2}}(v,w,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\mathcal{E},

and therefore

|F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|+|F_{e_{1}e_{2}}|\ \lesssim\ \frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2},

which is the desired estimate.

10.2
Decay for
F_{\hat{v}e_{1}}
and
F_{\hat{v}e_{2}}

We now prove the remaining outgoing components in (
1.45
).
Applying Lemma
10.1
to
u=F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}
gives a pointwise bound in terms of

L^{2}
norms of
F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}
and its angular and
D_{\hat{v}}
derivatives on the slab.
The
L^{2}
norms without
D_{\hat{v}}
are controlled directly by the commuted redshift fluxes, as above.

To control
D_{\hat{v}}F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}
we use the Maxwell equation and Bianchi identity in the stationary frame,
which give schematically

|D_{\hat{v}}F_{\hat{v}e_{A}}|\ \lesssim\ |\not{\nabla}F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|+|\not{\nabla}F_{e_{1}e_{2}}|+|F_{\hat{v}\hat{w}}|+|F_{e_{1}e_{2}}|+|D\phi|+|\phi|.

(10.6)

All terms on the right-hand side are already controlled in
L^{2}
on the slab by Proposition
8.6
and (
10.5
).
Therefore,

|F_{\hat{v}e_{1}}|+|F_{\hat{v}e_{2}}|\ \lesssim\ \frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}.

10.3
Decay for
\sqrt{1-\mu}\,F_{\hat{w}e_{1}}
and
\sqrt{1-\mu}\,F_{\hat{w}e_{2}}

Finally, we estimate the ingoing angular components.
Near the horizon the nondegenerate quantity is
\sqrt{1-\mu}\,F_{\hat{w}e_{A}}
(cf. (
1.45
)).
Applying Lemma
10.1
to
u=\sqrt{1-\mu}\,F_{\hat{w}e_{A}}
,
the corresponding slab norms are controlled by the commuted redshift fluxes, and the same schematic bounds as in the previous subsections yield

\sqrt{1-\mu}\,\bigl(|F_{\hat{w}e_{1}}|+|F_{\hat{w}e_{2}}|\bigr)\ \lesssim\ \frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\,\mathcal{E}^{1/2}.

10.4
Decay estimate for
\phi

We now prove (
1.46
).
Applying Lemma
10.1
to
u=\phi
gives

|\phi(v,w,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{v}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(|\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}^{2}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{v}}\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}D_{\hat{v}}\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}^{2}D_{\hat{v}}\phi|^{2}\Bigr)\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}.

The
D_{\hat{v}}\phi
and angular derivative terms are controlled directly by the commuted redshift energy and Proposition
8.6
, contributing
\lesssim v_{+}^{-2}\mathcal{E}
.
The remaining
L^{2}
term
\int|\phi|^{2}
is controlled by (
10.5
).
Therefore,

|\phi(v,w,\omega)|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \left(1+\left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}\right)\mathcal{E},

which yields

|\phi(v,w,\omega)|\ \lesssim\ \sqrt{1+\left(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\right)^{2}}\ \mathcal{E}^{1/2},

as claimed.

10.5
Decay estimate for
D\phi
near the horizon

We now prove the covariant derivative bound (
1.47
) in Corollary
1.17
.
Fix
(v,w)
with
2M<r(v,w)\leq 3M
and consider the unit
v
–slab

\{\bar{v}\in[v-1,v]\}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
at fixed
w
.
Applying the Sobolev inequality on this slab to the components of
D\phi
in the stationary frame yields

\displaystyle|D\phi|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{\bar{v}=v}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(\begin{aligned} &|D\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}D\phi|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\not{\nabla}D\phi|^{2}\\
&\qquad+|D_{\hat{v}}(D\phi)|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}D_{\hat{v}}(D\phi)|^{2}+|\not{\nabla}\not{\nabla}D_{\hat{v}}(D\phi)|^{2}\end{aligned}\Bigr)\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}.

(10.7)

The first three terms on the right-hand side are controlled directly by the commuted redshift energy estimate of Proposition
8.6
.
In particular, they contribute at most a factor
(1+(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}})^{2})\,\mathcal{E}
, where
\mathcal{E}
is the bootstrap energy.

To treat the
D_{\hat{v}}(D\phi)
terms we use the gauge–covariant wave equation

D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(10.8)

together with the curvature commutator
[D,D]=iF
.
In the region
2M<r\leq 3M
all connection coefficients are bounded, so (
10.8
) implies schematically

|D^{2}\phi|^{2}\ \lesssim\ |\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})|^{2}+|F|^{2}\,|D\phi|^{2},

and similarly after commuting with up to two angular derivatives.
Using the already established near-horizon bounds for
F
together with the redshift control of
D\phi
, the
|F|^{2}|D\phi|^{2}
term is absorbed (for small data), so it remains to estimate the potential contributions.

Under Assumption
1.1
(polynomial-type potentials) one has, on each unit
v
–slab,

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{\bar{v}=v}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})|^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-1},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{V4}},\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{E},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{sine} or \eqref{toda}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{\bar{v}=v}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!|\not{\nabla}(\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}))|^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{n=2}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-2},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{V4}},\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{E},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{sine} or \eqref{toda}},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\int_{\bar{v}=v-1}^{\bar{v}=v}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\!|\not{\nabla}\not{\nabla}(\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}))|^{2}\,d\sigma^{2}\,d\bar{v}

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\sum\limits_{n=3}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-3},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{V4}},\\[2.0pt]
\mathcal{E},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{sine} or \eqref{toda}}.\end{cases}

Collecting the contributions in (
10.7
) and using Proposition
8.6
to account for the redshift weight yields the following convenient shorthand:

E_{4}\equiv\begin{cases}\bigl(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\bigr)^{2}\sum\limits_{n=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-1}+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-2}+\sum\limits_{n=3}^{N}\mathcal{E}^{2n-3}+{\mathcal{E}}~,&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{V4}}~,\\
\bigl(\frac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\bigr)^{2}{\mathcal{E}}+{\mathcal{E}},&\text{if $P$ is of the form \eqref{sine} or \eqref{toda}}.\end{cases}

(10.9)

With this notation, (
10.7
) gives

|D\phi|\ \lesssim\ \Bigl(1+\bigl(\tfrac{w_{+}}{v_{+}}\bigr)^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}\,E_{4}^{1/2},

(10.10)

which is precisely (
1.47
). This completes the proof of Corollary
1.17
.

10.6
Radiation fields and scattering

The pointwise decay estimates of Corollaries
1.16
–
1.17
can be upgraded to a genuine asymptotic statement describing the outgoing profile of small Maxwell–Higgs solutions in terms of
radiation fields
on future null infinity
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
In the present nonlinear and gauge-coupled setting, it is natural to define the scalar radiation field after a (null) parallel transport renormalization, while the Maxwell radiation field is expressed directly in terms of the gauge-invariant field strength
F
.

Remark 10.1

(Radiative regime and Coulomb subtraction)
.

All definitions and statements below concern the
radiative
Maxwell field.
In the coupled Maxwell–Higgs system treated in this paper we impose the charge-free condition on the initial data; the discussion of nonzero charges here is included only to single out the linear Coulomb sector for Maxwell fields.
If the initial data carry nonzero charges, one first subtracts the stationary Coulomb field
F_{Q}
as in Corollary
1.18
and works with the charge–free remainder
\widetilde{F}:=F-F_{Q}
.
For notational simplicity we continue to write
F
for the charge-free Maxwell field in this section.

10.7
Null hypersurfaces, null frame, and fluxes

We work in the double–null coordinates
(v,w,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
introduced in Section
1
,

v=t+r^{\ast},\qquad w=t-r^{\ast},\qquad\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2},

and we denote by
S_{w,v}
the spheres
\{w=\mathrm{const},\,v=\mathrm{const}\}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We set

L:=\partial_{v}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r^{\ast}}),\qquad\underline{L}:=\partial_{w}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r^{\ast}}),

so that
L
is tangent to outgoing null geodesics and
\underline{L}
is tangent to ingoing null geodesics.
For
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
we define the outgoing null hypersurface

\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}:=\{w=w_{0},\ v\geq v_{0}\},

where
v_{0}
is chosen so that
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
lies in the domain of existence of the solution.
We view future null infinity as the endpoint
v\to+\infty
along
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
.
We denote by
\mathrm{d}\omega
the standard area form on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

Let
T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]
be the Maxwell–Higgs energy–momentum tensor, and let
X
be a smooth vector field.
The associated current is
J^{X}_{\mu}:=T_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu}
and the flux through a hypersurface
\mathcal{S}
is

\mathcal{F}^{X}_{\mathcal{S}}[F,\phi]:=\int_{\mathcal{S}}J^{X}_{\mu}\,n_{\mathcal{S}}^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{S}},

where
n_{\mathcal{S}}
is the future directed unit normal (or null generator) and
\mathrm{d}\mu_{\mathcal{S}}
the induced measure.
In particular, the Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
yields conserved fluxes through spacelike slices
\Sigma_{t}
and controls the (nondegenerate) energy radiated through
\mathcal{H}^{+}
by the redshift estimates of Section
8.3
.

10.8
Radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}

We define the renormalized scalar field

\psi:=r\,\phi,

(10.11)

which is the natural quantity expected to admit a nontrivial limit on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
.
For the Maxwell field we use the tetrad/spin component formalism recalled in Section
8
:
in particular, the component

\Phi_{-1}=F(\hat{N},\hat{\bar{M}})

is the outgoing radiative component (Newman–Penrose “
\phi_{2}
”), which is expected to decay as
r^{-1}
.
Accordingly, we renormalize

\Psi_{F}:=r\,\Phi_{-1}.

(10.12)

A gauge-covariant normalization for
\psi
.

Since
\phi
is charged, the quantity
\psi=r\phi
depends on the gauge choice.
To define a canonical outgoing profile, we remove the
A_{L}
–phase along each outgoing null generator by parallel transport.

Definition 10.1

(Null parallel transport normalization)
.

Fix
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Let
U(w_{0},v,\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
be the solution of the ODE

LU=i\,A_{L}\,U,\qquad U(w_{0},v_{0},\omega)=1,

(10.13)

along the null generator
\{w=w_{0},\ \omega=\mathrm{const}\}
.
Define the parallel–transported scalar

\psi^{\sharp}:=U^{-1}\psi.

(10.14)

Then
|U|\equiv 1
and

L\psi^{\sharp}=U^{-1}D_{L}\psi,\qquad|L\psi^{\sharp}|=|D_{L}\psi|.

(10.15)

Definition 10.2

(Radiation fields at null infinity)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global solution arising from small admissible data.
We say that
(F,\phi)
admits radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
if for each
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
there exist limits

\displaystyle\psi_{\mathcal{I}}(w_{0},\omega)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v,\omega),

(10.16)

\displaystyle(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}}(w_{0},\omega)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v,\omega),

(10.17)

with convergence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\omega})
(and likewise for a finite number of angular derivatives, depending on
k
).

The key analytic input for Definition
10.2
is square–integrability of the outgoing derivatives
D_{L}\psi
and
L\Psi_{F}
along
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
.
We record a Cauchy criterion making this precise.

Proposition 10.2

(Cauchy criterion)
.

Fix
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Assume that the following bounds hold for the renormalized fields (
10.11
) and (
10.12
):

\int_{v_{0}}^{+\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bigl(|D_{L}\psi|^{2}+|L\Psi_{F}|^{2}\bigr)(w_{0},v,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v<+\infty.

(10.18)

Then the limits in Definition
10.2
exist in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\omega})
.
Moreover, for any
v_{2}>v_{1}\geq v_{0}
one has the quantitative estimates

\displaystyle\|\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v_{2},\cdot)-\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|D_{L}\psi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\right)^{1/2},

(10.19)

\displaystyle\|\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v_{2},\cdot)-\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v_{1},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|L\Psi_{F}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\right)^{1/2}.

(10.20)

Proof.

For the Maxwell field, the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v_{2},\omega)-\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v_{1},\omega)=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}(L\Psi_{F})(w_{0},v,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}v.

Taking the
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\omega})
norm and applying Cauchy–Schwarz in
v
yields (
10.20
).

For the scalar field, we use Definition
10.1
. By (
10.15
),

\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v_{2},\omega)-\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v_{1},\omega)=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}(L\psi^{\sharp})(w_{0},v,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}v=\int_{v_{1}}^{v_{2}}U^{-1}(D_{L}\psi)(w_{0},v,\omega)\,\mathrm{d}v.

Since
|U|\equiv 1
, we have
|U^{-1}D_{L}\psi|=|D_{L}\psi|
. Therefore, applying Cauchy–Schwarz in
v
and then integrating over
\omega
yields (
10.19
).
Assumption (
10.18
) implies that
\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v,\cdot)
and
\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v,\cdot)
are Cauchy in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
as
v\to+\infty
, hence converge.
∎

10.9
Null-infinity flux bounds and existence of the radiation fields

We now show that the decay theory proved earlier implies (
10.18
) and therefore yields radiation fields.

Proposition 10.3

(Outgoing null flux bounds)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global charge-free solution arising from admissible data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
with
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
k\geq 6
.
Then for each
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
one has

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bigl(|D_{L}\psi|^{2}+|L\Psi_{F}|^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(10.21)

In particular, the Cauchy criterion (
10.18
) holds for every
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
.

Proof.

We split the proof into the scalar and Maxwell parts.

(1) Scalar flux bound.

Write
D_{L}\psi=r\,D_{L}\phi+(Lr)\,\phi
.
Since
Lr=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\mu)
, we have
|Lr|\lesssim 1
on the exterior.
By the elementary inequality
|a+b|^{2}\leq 2|a|^{2}+2|b|^{2}
,

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|D_{L}\psi|^{2}\ \lesssim\ \int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}|D_{L}\phi|^{2}\ +\ \int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\phi|^{2}.

(10.22)

To estimate the first term, we apply the conserved
T
–energy identity (Section
8.1
) to a truncated domain bounded by
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
, the portion of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
up to time
t_{1}
, and the portion of
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
up to time
t_{1}
.
Since
T=\partial_{t}
is Killing, the bulk term vanishes and we obtain

\mathcal{F}^{T}_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}\cap\{t\leq t_{1}\}}[F,\phi]\leq E^{\hat{t}}[F,\phi](t_{0})\leq\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

The integrand of
\mathcal{F}^{T}_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}
is nonnegative and controls
T_{LL}[F,\phi]
, hence in particular controls
|D_{L}\phi|^{2}
.
Recalling that the induced measure on
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
is
r^{2}\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}\omega
, this yields

\int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}r^{2}|D_{L}\phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(10.23)

For the second term in (
10.22
), we split the
v
–integral into a compact piece and a far piece.
On the compact segment
\{v_{0}\leq v\leq v_{R}\}
the integral is controlled by the energy and regularity.
On the far segment
\{v\geq v_{R}\}
we use the pointwise decay (
1.43
) from Corollary
1.16
(with
r\geq R
) to obtain

\int_{v_{R}}^{+\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|\phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \int_{v_{R}}^{+\infty}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})}{(1+v)^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

Combining with (
10.23
) gives the desired bound for
\int_{\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}}|D_{L}\psi|^{2}
.

(2) Maxwell flux bound for
L\Psi_{F}
.

We use the transport equation for the outgoing Maxwell component, equation (
C.10
).
Since
\hat{L}=2L
, the identity (
C.10
) implies

L\Phi_{-1}=\frac{1-\mu}{2r^{2}}\,\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{0}\ +\ \mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{Max}}[\phi],

(10.24)

where the nonlinear term satisfies the pointwise bound

|\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{Max}}[\phi]|\ \lesssim\ \frac{1}{r}\,|\phi|\,|\not{D}\phi|

(10.25)

(up to harmless angular weights).
Multiplying (
10.24
) by
r
and using
L\Psi_{F}=L(r\Phi_{-1})=(Lr)\Phi_{-1}+r\,L\Phi_{-1}
, we obtain

|L\Psi_{F}|\ \lesssim\ |\Phi_{-1}|\ +\ \frac{1}{r}\,|\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{0}|\ +\ |\phi|\,|\not{D}\phi|.

(10.26)

We estimate each term on the right-hand side of (
10.26
) on
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
.
For the first term, Corollary
1.16
gives
|\Phi_{-1}|\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}(1+v)^{-1}
in the far region, which is square integrable in
v
.
For the second term, note that
\hat{\bar{M}}
is an angular derivative; using the same Sobolev argument as in Section
9
applied to the commuted fields
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}F
(with
|I|\leq 2
) gives the pointwise decay

|\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{0}|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{1+v}\qquad(r\geq R).

Since
r\sim\tfrac{1}{2}(v-w_{0})
on
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
in the far region, we have
r^{-1}\lesssim(1+v)^{-1}
and therefore

\frac{1}{r}\,|\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{0}|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}}{(1+v)^{2}},

which is square integrable.
Finally, by Corollary
1.16
,
|\phi|+|D\phi|\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}(1+v)^{-1}
in the far region, hence

|\phi|\,|\not{D}\phi|\ \lesssim\ \frac{\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})}{(1+v)^{2}},

which is square integrable as well.

Combining these bounds and integrating over
\mathbb{S}^{2}\times[v_{R},+\infty)
yields

\int_{v_{R}}^{+\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|L\Psi_{F}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

The remaining compact segment
v\in[v_{0},v_{R}]
is controlled by regularity and the energy bound.
This completes the proof of (
10.21
).
∎

Theorem 10.4

(Existence of radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global charge-free solution arising from admissible data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
with
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
k\geq 6
.
Then
(F,\phi)
admits radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
in the sense of Definition
10.2
.
Moreover, for each
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
the radiation fields satisfy the a priori bound

\|\psi_{\mathcal{I}}(w_{0},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\|(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}}(w_{0},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

Proof.

Proposition
10.3
yields (
10.18
) for every
\mathcal{N}_{w_{0}}
.
Applying Proposition
10.2
gives existence of the limits (
10.16
)–(
10.17
) in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
.
The stated a priori bound follows by taking
v_{1}=v_{0}
and letting
v_{2}\to+\infty
in (
10.19
)–(
10.20
).
∎

10.10
Radiation fields on the event horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}

To obtain a scattering description on the
full future characteristic boundary
of the exterior,
we complement the null–infinity radiation fields with radiation data on the future event horizon.
It is convenient to use ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates
(v,r,\omega)
, where
v=t+r^{\ast}
.
In these coordinates the Schwarzschild metric takes the regular form

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=-\Bigl(1-\frac{2M}{r}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}v^{2}+2\,\mathrm{d}v\,\mathrm{d}r+r^{2}\mathrm{d}\Omega^{2},

which extends smoothly to
r=2M
.
The future event horizon is the null hypersurface

\mathcal{H}^{+}:=\{r=2M,\ v\geq v_{0}\}\cong[v_{0},+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2},

generated by
L_{\mathcal{H}}:=\partial_{v}
(which agrees with
L=\partial_{v}
from Section
10.7
).

Gauge-covariant scalar horizon field.

As on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, the complex scalar requires a gauge-covariant normalization along horizon generators.

Definition 10.3

(Horizon parallel transport normalization)
.

Fix
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Let
U_{\mathcal{H}}(v,\omega)\in\mathbb{C}
solve the ODE along the horizon generator
\{r=2M,\ \omega=\mathrm{const}\}
:

\partial_{v}U_{\mathcal{H}}=i\,A_{L}\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\,U_{\mathcal{H}},\qquad U_{\mathcal{H}}(v_{0},\omega)=1.

(10.27)

Define the horizon-renormalized scalar

\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(v,\omega):=r\,U_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(v,\omega)\,\phi\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\omega)=(2M)\,U_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\phi\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}.

(10.28)

Then
|U_{\mathcal{H}}|\equiv 1
and

\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}=U_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}\,D_{L}(r\phi)\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}},\qquad|\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}|=|D_{L}\psi|\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}.

(10.29)

Maxwell horizon field.

For the Maxwell field, the natural horizon radiation quantity is the extreme component tangential to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
In the stationary tetrad of Section
8
, this is
\Phi_{1}=F(\hat{L},\hat{M})
with
\hat{L}=2L
.
We set the horizon renormalization

(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}(v,\omega):=r\,\Phi_{1}\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\omega)=(2M)\,\Phi_{1}\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}.

(10.30)

Proposition 10.5

(Horizon flux bounds)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global charge-free solution arising from admissible data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
with
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
k\geq 6
.
Then the horizon radiation fields defined in (
10.28
) and (
10.30
) satisfy

\int_{v_{0}}^{+\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(|\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}|^{2}+|(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}|^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(10.31)

The same bound holds after commuting with angular momenta up to order
k-2
.

Proof.

Since
T=\partial_{t}
is Killing, the
T
–energy identity of Section
8.1
applied to a truncated domain bounded by
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
,
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
, a large-
r
timelike cylinder
\{r=R\}
(sent to
R\to+\infty
), and the portion of
\mathcal{H}^{+}
with
v\leq v_{1}
yields

\mathcal{F}^{T}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\{v\leq v_{1}\}}[F,\phi]\ \leq\ E^{\hat{t}}[F,\phi](t_{0})\ \leq\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

after dropping the nonnegative flux terms through
\{r=R\}
and
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
.
On
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, the generator
\partial_{v}
is null and tangent, and
T=\partial_{t}=\partial_{v}
in ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates.
Therefore the
T
–flux density equals
T_{LL}[F,\phi]
.
For the scalar part,
T_{LL}[\phi]=|D_{L}\phi|^{2}
; for the Maxwell part,
T_{LL}[F]=|F(L,\not{\cdot})|^{2}
, which in the tetrad notation is comparable to
|\Phi_{1}|^{2}
.
Since
r=2M
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, this yields

\int_{v_{0}}^{v_{1}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl(|D_{L}\psi|^{2}+|r\,\Phi_{1}|^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{F}^{T}_{\mathcal{H}^{+}\cap\{v\leq v_{1}\}}[F,\phi]\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

where we used
D_{L}\psi=r\,D_{L}\phi
on
\mathcal{H}^{+}
.
Finally, (
10.29
) implies
|\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}|=|D_{L}\psi|\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}
, so the desired estimate (
10.31
) follows after letting
v_{1}\to+\infty
.
The commuted bounds are identical, applying the same argument to the commuted system and using
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
to control the corresponding commuted
T
–energy.
∎

Theorem 10.6

(Existence of horizon radiation fields)
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
10.5
, the horizon traces
(\psi_{\mathcal{H}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}})
are well-defined as functions on
\mathcal{H}^{+}\cong[v_{0},+\infty)\times\mathbb{S}^{2}
, and they satisfy the a priori flux bound (
10.31
).

Proof.

For smooth solutions the restrictions to
\mathcal{H}^{+}
are defined in ingoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates.
Proposition
10.5
yields the stated
L^{2}
control, and hence
\psi_{\mathcal{H}}\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([v_{0},+\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}))
with
\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}\in L^{2}_{v,\omega}
, while
(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}\in L^{2}_{v,\omega}
.
∎

10.11
Linear comparison theory and full nonlinear scattering

Theorems
10.4
and
10.6
provide radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
, respectively.
In this subsection we upgrade these asymptotic statements to a
full forward scattering theory
.
For the massless sector (in particular, for the Maxwell field and for scalars with
m^{2}=0
) the natural future boundary is the two-ended characteristic set

\partial_{+}\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}.

When
m^{2}>0
, the Klein–Gordon component admits an additional timelike/hyperboloidal channel.
We denote the corresponding extended future boundary by

\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup i^{+},

(10.32)

where
i^{+}
denotes future timelike infinity.
We will encode the
i^{+}
–channel through a timelike scattering state in the
T
–energy topology, and relate it to the Dollard-modified asymptotic completeness results for the linear massive Klein–Gordon equation (cf.
[
6
,
7
]
).
We then compare the nonlinear Maxwell–Higgs dynamics to its decoupled linearization about the trivial vacuum
(F,\phi)=(0,0)
.

Definition 10.4

(Linearized comparison system and radiation data)
.

Let
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
satisfy Assumption
1.1
, and let
m^{2}\geq 0
be as in (
1.7
).
The
