linear comparison system
consists of a Maxwell field
F^{\mathrm{lin}}
and a complex scalar
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
on
(\mathcal{D},g)
solving

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}F^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0,\qquad\nabla^{\alpha}{}^{\star}F^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\alpha\beta}=0,

(10.33)

\displaystyle\square_{g}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle=m^{2}\,\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},

(10.34)

with smooth charge–free finite–energy Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
We define the renormalized linear fields

\psi^{\mathrm{lin}}:=r\,\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},\qquad\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=r\,\Phi_{-1}[F^{\mathrm{lin}}].

The (future) linear radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
are

\psi_{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{lin}}(w,\omega):=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\psi^{\mathrm{lin}}(w,v,\omega),\qquad(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{I}}(w,\omega):=\lim_{v\to+\infty}\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}}(w,v,\omega),

whenever the limits exist.
On the horizon
\mathcal{H}^{+}
we define the linear horizon fields by restriction:

\psi_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{lin}}(v,\omega):=\psi^{\mathrm{lin}}\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\omega)=(2M)\,\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}},\qquad(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{H}}(v,\omega):=r\,\Phi_{1}[F^{\mathrm{lin}}]\!\restriction_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}(v,\omega).

We call the quadruple

\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{lin}},(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{I}},\psi_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{lin}},(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr)

the
full forward linear radiation data
on
\partial_{+}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Theorem 10.7

(Full linear scattering on Schwarzschild)
.

Assume
m^{2}=0
in (
10.34
) (equivalently
m^{2}=0
).
Let
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
be a smooth charge–free finite–energy solution of (
10.33
)–(
10.34
).
Then:

(i)

The limits defining
(\psi_{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{lin}},(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{I}})
exist in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})
for each fixed
w\in\mathbb{R}
, and the horizon traces
(\psi_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{lin}},(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{H}})
belong to
L^{2}_{v,\omega}
.

(ii)

The map from linear Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
to the full radiation data
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}
is injective and admits a continuous inverse on its range.

Full linear scattering for the wave equation and Maxwell fields on Schwarzschild is classical; see for instance
[
4
,
2
,
3
]
.

Remark 10.2

(Massive scalars and the timelike channel)
.

When
m^{2}>0
, characteristic data on
\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
do not by themselves give a complete asymptotic description of the Klein–Gordon component.
It is therefore natural to enlarge the future boundary to

\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup i^{+},

where
i^{+}
denotes future timelike infinity.
In the linear theory this reflects the long-range
r^{-1}
term in the Regge–Wheeler potential for massive Klein–Gordon; as shown by Bachelot, the corresponding wave operators are classical at the horizon and Dollard-modified at infinity, and strong asymptotic completeness holds
[
7
]
(building on the time-dependent approach of Dimock–Kay
[
6
]
).

In our nonlinear setting we implement the
i^{+}
–channel by constructing, in addition to the characteristic radiation fields of Theorems
10.4
and
10.6
, an outgoing timelike scattering state for the scalar in the
T
–energy topology.
The Maxwell part (being massless) continues to admit a characteristic scattering theory on
\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
.

Timelike scattering state for the scalar field.

For the massive Klein–Gordon component it is natural to formulate scattering in the (nondegenerate)
T
–energy topology on the Schwarzschild foliation.

Definition 10.5

(Scalar energy space)
.

Let
m^{2}\geq 0
.
For smooth compactly supported Cauchy data
(f,g)
on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
we define

\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}^{2}:=\int_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\bigl(|\nabla_{\Sigma}f|^{2}+m^{2}|f|^{2}+|g|^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},

(10.35)

where
\nabla_{\Sigma}
is the induced covariant derivative on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}
the completion in this norm.
For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we set

\|(f,g)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{\ell}}^{2}:=\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\bigl\|\bigl(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}f,\ \mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}g\bigr)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}^{2},

(10.36)

where
T=\partial_{t}
and
\Omega
are the angular momenta.

Lemma 10.8

(Inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon energy inequality)
.

Let
u
be a sufficiently regular complex scalar solving

(\square_{g}-m^{2})u=G

(10.37)

on
\mathcal{D}
with Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
.
Then for any
t_{2}\geq t_{1}\geq t_{0}
one has

\|\bigl(u,\nabla_{n}u\bigr)(t_{2})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\leq\|\bigl(u,\nabla_{n}u\bigr)(t_{1})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\ +\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|G(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(10.38)

where
n
is the future unit normal to
\Sigma_{t}
.
The same estimate holds after commuting (
10.37
) with
T
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

This is the standard energy estimate obtained by contracting the Klein–Gordon stress–energy tensor with the Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
and applying Stokes’ theorem between
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
and
\Sigma_{t_{2}}
.
The source term contributes
\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{s}}\Re(G\,\overline{Tu})\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{s}}\mathrm{d}s
, which is bounded by Cauchy–Schwarz.
Commutation with
T
and
\Omega
follows since
T
is Killing and
\Omega
are angular Killing fields.
∎

Proposition 10.9

(Integrable nonlinear forcing for the scalar)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global solution as in Corollary
1.15
, with
m^{2}\geq 0
.
In Lorenz gauge (
7.1
) the scalar satisfies

(\square_{g}-m^{2})\phi=\mathcal{N}_{\phi},\qquad\mathcal{N}_{\phi}:=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\phi+\bigl(\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})-m^{2}\phi\bigr).

(10.39)

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
(depending on
M,k
) such that if
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, then

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(10.40)

The same bound holds after commuting with
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
for
|I|+j\leq k-3
.

Proof.

We sketch the standard Cook-type estimate.
Using the pointwise decay of Corollary
1.16
together with Sobolev on
\Sigma_{t}
, we have

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\bigl(\|A(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}+\|\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

(10.41)

since
\|A\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim(1+t)^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}
.

Moreover, the Morawetz/ILD estimates of Section
8.3
together with the far-region decay hierarchy imply

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\nabla\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

(10.42)

and similarly for a finite number of commuted derivatives.

We now estimate each contribution in (
10.39
).
For the first term we use Cauchy–Schwarz in
t
:

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|A\cdot\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

For the quadratic potential term we have

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|A^{2}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\|\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

using boundedness of the
T
–energy.

Finally, by gauge invariance
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
one has (
1.12
)–(
1.13
).
In particular, by (
1.13
)–(
1.14
) one has the global bound

|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})-m^{2}\phi|=|\mathcal{R}_{P}(\phi)|\leq C_{P,0}\,(1+|\phi|^{2N_{P}})\,|\phi|^{3}.

Thus

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\Bigl(\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}+\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2N_{P}+2}\Bigr)\,\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using boundedness of the
T
–energy and the decay estimate
\|\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim(1+t)^{-1}\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}
, all time integrals on the right-hand side are finite, and for sufficiently small
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})
one obtains

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

The commuted bounds follow identically, using higher-order energy control and replacing
\phi
by
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}\phi
.
∎

Integrable forcing for the Maxwell equation.

In the wave-operator constructions below we will also need an
L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
control on the Maxwell source current.

Lemma 10.10

(Inhomogeneous Maxwell energy inequality)
.

Let
G
be a sufficiently regular charge–free Maxwell field solving

\nabla^{\alpha}G_{\alpha\beta}=J_{\beta},\qquad\nabla^{\alpha}{}^{\star}G_{\alpha\beta}=0

(10.43)

on
\mathcal{D}
.
Then for any
t_{2}\geq t_{1}\geq t_{0}
one has the energy estimate

E^{\hat{t}}[G](t_{2})^{1/2}\ \leq\ E^{\hat{t}}[G](t_{1})^{1/2}\ +\ \int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|J(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(10.44)

where
E^{\hat{t}}[G](t)
denotes the nondegenerate
T
–energy of
G
on
\Sigma_{t}
(cf. Section
8.1
).
The same estimate holds after commuting (
10.43
) with
T
and the angular momenta
\Omega
.

Proof.

Contract the Maxwell stress–energy tensor
T_{\mu\nu}[G]
with the Killing field
T=\partial_{t}
and apply Stokes’ theorem between
\Sigma_{t_{1}}
and
\Sigma_{t_{2}}
.
Using
\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}[G]=-G_{\nu\alpha}J^{\alpha}
for (
10.43
), one obtains

E^{\hat{t}}[G](t_{2})-E^{\hat{t}}[G](t_{1})=-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\Sigma_{s}}G(T,\cdot)\cdot J\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{s}}\mathrm{d}s.

By Cauchy–Schwarz on
\Sigma_{s}
and the equivalence between
E^{\hat{t}}[G](s)
and
\|G(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}
, we infer

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}E^{\hat{t}}[G](s)^{1/2}\ \lesssim\ \|J(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}.

Integrating in
s
gives (
10.44
).
Commutation with
T
and
\Omega
follows since
T
and
\Omega
are Killing fields on Schwarzschild.
∎

Proposition 10.11

(Integrable current forcing for Maxwell)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be a global charge–free solution as in Corollary
1.15
.
Then the scalar current
J[\phi]_{\mu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\mu}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\mu}\phi}\bigr)
satisfies

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|J[\phi](t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}).

(10.45)

The same estimate holds after commuting with
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
for
|I|+j\leq k-3
.

Proof.

Using
|J[\phi]|\lesssim|\phi|\,|D\phi|
and Sobolev on
\Sigma_{t}
, we obtain

\|J[\phi](t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\ \lesssim\ \|\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\|D\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}.

Integrating in
t
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz yields

\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|J[\phi](t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\ \lesssim\ \left(\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{+\infty}\|D\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}.

The first factor is controlled by (
10.41
).
The second factor is controlled by the Morawetz/ILD estimate (
10.42
) (and the corresponding commuted bounds).
This proves (
10.45
).
∎

Proposition 10.12

(Existence of a timelike scattering state for
\phi
)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Corollary
1.15
and let
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Then there exists a unique pair of Cauchy data
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
and a unique global solution
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
of the linear Klein–Gordon equation (
10.34
) such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\bigl\|\bigl(\phi,\nabla_{n}\phi\bigr)(t)-\bigl(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr)(t)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}=0.

(10.46)

Moreover,

\|(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})^{1/2}.

(10.47)

Proof.

Write the scalar equation in the form (
10.39
).
Fix
T\geq t_{0}
and let
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T}
be the unique solution of (
10.34
) with Cauchy data matching the nonlinear scalar at time
T
:

\bigl(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T}\bigr)(T)=\bigl(\phi,\nabla_{n}\phi\bigr)(T).

Then the difference
u_{T}:=\phi-\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T}
satisfies

(\square_{g}-m^{2})u_{T}=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
with vanishing Cauchy data at
t=T
.
Applying Lemma
10.8
on
[T,t]
yields

\|\bigl(u_{T},\nabla_{n}u_{T}\bigr)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\leq\int_{T}^{t}\|\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

By Proposition
10.9
, the right-hand side tends to
0
uniformly in
t\geq T
as
T\to+\infty
.
In particular, for any
T_{2}\geq T_{1}
we have

\|\bigl(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T_{2}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T_{2}}\bigr)(t_{0})-\bigl(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T_{1}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}_{T_{1}}\bigr)(t_{0})\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\lesssim\int_{T_{1}}^{T_{2}}\|\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

so the sequence of linear data at
t_{0}
is Cauchy in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}
.
Let
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}
denote its limit, and let
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
be the unique global linear solution with this data.
Passing to the limit in the above estimates yields (
10.46
).
The higher-order statement in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
follows by applying the same argument to commuted fields.
Finally, (
10.47
) follows from boundedness of the nonlinear energy and the triangle inequality.
Uniqueness of
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
and
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
is immediate from uniqueness of the linear Cauchy problem.
∎

Theorem 10.13

(Full nonlinear scattering to the linear theory)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Corollary
1.15
and let
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Let
(F,\phi)
be the resulting global Maxwell–Higgs solution and let
(\psi_{\mathcal{I}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}})
and
(\psi_{\mathcal{H}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}})
denote its radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{+}
and
\mathcal{H}^{+}
given by Theorems
10.4
and
10.6
.
Then:

(i)

The full forward nonlinear radiation data

\mathcal{R}_{+}:=\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{I}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}},\psi_{\mathcal{H}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr)

are well-defined and satisfy the a priori bound

\|\psi_{\mathcal{I}}(w,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\|(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}}(w,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}^{2}+\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bigl(|\partial_{v}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}|^{2}+|(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}|^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\omega\,\mathrm{d}v\ \lesssim\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0}),

uniformly in
w
.

(ii)

Maxwell comparison state.

There exists a unique charge–free solution
F^{\mathrm{lin}}
of the free Maxwell system (
10.33
) such that its characteristic radiation data agree with the nonlinear Maxwell data:

\bigl((\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{I}},(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr)=\bigl((\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr).

(iii)

Scalar timelike scattering state.

Let
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
and
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
be given by Proposition
10.12
.
In particular,

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\bigl\|\bigl(\phi,\nabla_{n}\phi\bigr)(t)-\bigl(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr)(t)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}=0.

(10.48)

Moreover, along future null infinity, for each fixed
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\|\psi^{\sharp}(w_{0},v,\cdot)-\psi^{\mathrm{lin}}(w_{0},v,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=0,

(10.49)

and on the horizon one has convergence of the radiation fields in the tail:

\lim_{V\to+\infty}\int_{V}^{+\infty}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bigl|\partial_{v}\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{H}}-\psi_{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr)\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v=0.

(10.50)

(iv)

Full nonlinear scattering on the extended future boundary
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.

Let
\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=r\,\Phi_{-1}[F^{\mathrm{lin}}]
and
\psi^{\mathrm{lin}}:=r\,\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
.
Then, for each fixed
w_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{v\to+\infty}\|\Psi_{F}(w_{0},v,\cdot)-\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}}(w_{0},v,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2})}=0,

and on the horizon

\int_{\mathcal{H}^{+}}\!\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\bigl|(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}}-(\Psi_{F}^{\mathrm{lin}})_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\mathrm{d}v=0.

In addition, the scalar scatters to
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
at future timelike infinity
i^{+}
in the sense of (
10.48
).
The same conclusions hold after commuting with angular momenta up to order
k-2
.

Proof.

Statement (i) is Theorems
10.4
and
10.6
.

For the Maxwell part, (ii) follows from the (massless) linear characteristic scattering theory on Schwarzschild for the Coulomb–subtracted Maxwell field; see, e.g.,
[
2
,
3
]
.

For the scalar part, Proposition
10.12
gives a unique linear Klein–Gordon comparison solution
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
satisfying (
10.48
).
Let
u:=\phi-\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
.
Then
u
satisfies an inhomogeneous Klein–Gordon equation with source
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
, and by construction its Cauchy energy tends to
0
as
t\to+\infty
.
Repeating the argument of Theorem
10.4
for
u
(using Proposition
10.3
and the integrability of
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
from Proposition
10.9
) yields that the null radiation field of
u
vanishes, which is (
10.49
).
Similarly, applying the horizon flux identity to
u
gives (
10.50
).

Finally, (iv) follows by combining (ii) and (iii).
The commuted statements follow by applying the same argument to
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}(F,\phi)
with
|I|+j\leq k-2
.
∎

Remark 10.3

(Extended nonlinear scattering map)
.

Theorem
10.13
defines a forward nonlinear scattering map on the (possibly extended) future boundary.
For the massless sector one has the two-ended characteristic boundary
\partial_{+}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
, while for
m^{2}>0
the natural completion is
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}\cup i^{+}
as in (
10.32
).
In our nonlinear formulation, the
i^{+}
–channel is encoded by the timelike scattering state
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
.
Thus,

\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}:\ \text{(small charge--free Cauchy data on $\Sigma_{t_{0}}$)}\longmapsto\Bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{I}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}},\psi_{\mathcal{H}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}},\ \phi_{+},\pi_{+}\Bigr).

When
m^{2}=0
, the pair
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
is not needed for completeness and the map reduces to the two-ended characteristic scattering map.

Corollary 10.14

(Dollard-modified infinity data for the nonlinear scalar)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
10.13
with
m^{2}>0
.
Let
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
be the timelike scattering state produced in Proposition
10.12
, and let
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
be the associated linear Klein–Gordon solution.
Let
W_{\infty}^{+}
denote the (Dollard-modified) wave operator at spatial infinity constructed in
[
7
]
for the linear massive Klein–Gordon evolution on Schwarzschild.
Then the datum

f_{+}:=(W_{\infty}^{+})^{-1}(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})

(10.51)

is well-defined in the corresponding free energy space at infinity and depends continuously on the initial nonlinear data.
Moreover, if
U_{\infty}(t)
denotes the Dollard-modified free evolution and
J_{\infty}
the identification operator (in the notation of
[
7
]
), then

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\bigl\|(\phi,\nabla_{n}\phi)(t)-J_{\infty}U_{\infty}(t)f_{+}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}=0.

In particular, for
m^{2}>0
the scalar asymptotics at
i^{+}
can be formulated either in terms of the Schwarzschild timelike scattering state
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
or in terms of the equivalent Dollard-modified free datum
f_{+}
.

Proof.

By Theorem
10.13
(iii) one has

\bigl\|(\phi,\nabla_{n}\phi)(t)-(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})(t)\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}\to 0\qquad(t\to+\infty).

On the other hand,
[
7
]
constructs Dollard-modified wave operators at infinity and proves asymptotic completeness for the linear massive Klein–Gordon flow on Schwarzschild.
In particular, for the linear solution
\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}
one has

\bigl\|(\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}},\nabla_{n}\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})(t)-J_{\infty}U_{\infty}(t)f_{+}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}}\to 0\qquad(t\to+\infty),

with
f_{+}
defined by (
10.51
).
Combining the two limits yields the claim.
∎

Remark 10.4

(Interpretation of the
i^{+}
datum in the massive case)
.

When
m^{2}>0
, the additional state
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
captures the timelike/hyperboloidal channel for the Klein–Gordon component.
Corollary
10.14
shows that, in the sense of the linear asymptotic completeness results of Bachelot for the massive Klein–Gordon equation on Schwarzschild
[
7
]
, this timelike state can equivalently be encoded by a unique
Dollard-modified
free scattering datum
f_{+}
at spatial infinity.

10.12
Two–sided scattering and the nonlinear scattering operator

The preceding results describe the
forward
asymptotic dynamics on
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.
In many applications (and in particular in the quantum-field-theoretic interpretation of classical scattering on black holes) one seeks a
two–sided
theory relating past and future asymptotic data.
In this subsection we record a clean two–sided formulation and define the associated nonlinear scattering operator.

Past boundary.

Let
\mathcal{I}^{-}
denote past null infinity and
\mathcal{H}^{-}
the past event horizon (white-hole horizon in the maximal Kruskal extension).
We set

\partial_{-}\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{I}^{-}\cup\mathcal{H}^{-},\qquad\partial_{-}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{I}^{-}\cup\mathcal{H}^{-}\cup i^{-},

where
i^{-}
denotes past timelike infinity.

Time reversal and past radiation fields.

The Maxwell–Higgs system in Lorenz gauge is invariant under time reversal on the static Schwarzschild exterior.
As a consequence, the decay analysis of Corollaries
1.16
–
1.17
and the flux constructions of this section apply equally well in the past direction.

To describe
\mathcal{I}^{-}
we fix an advanced time
v_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and consider the incoming null hypersurface

\underline{\mathcal{N}}_{v_{0}}:=\{v=v_{0},\ w\leq w_{0}\},\qquad\underline{L}:=\partial_{w},

so that past null infinity corresponds to
w\to-\infty
along
\underline{\mathcal{N}}_{v_{0}}
.
As for
\mathcal{I}^{+}
, we remove the
A_{\underline{L}}
phase by parallel transport along the null generators of
\underline{\mathcal{N}}_{v_{0}}
.
Thus one obtains past scalar radiation fields

\psi_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(v_{0},\omega):=\lim_{w\to-\infty}\psi^{\flat}(w,v_{0},\omega),\qquad\psi^{\flat}:=(U_{-})^{-1}\psi,

\underline{L}U_{-}=iA_{\underline{L}}U_{-},\qquad U_{-}(w_{0},v_{0},\omega)=1,\qquad|U_{-}|\equiv 1.

with convergence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\omega})
.
For the Maxwell field, the incoming radiative component at infinity is the extreme tetrad component
\Phi_{1}
(cf. Section
8
), and we define the past Maxwell radiation field by

(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}^{-}}(v_{0},\omega):=\lim_{w\to-\infty}r\,\Phi_{1}(w,v_{0},\omega)\qquad\text{in }L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{2}_{\omega}).

Similarly, one defines horizon radiation fields
(\psi_{\mathcal{H}^{-}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}^{-}})
on the past horizon
\mathcal{H}^{-}
by restriction (in Kruskal or outgoing Eddington–Finkelstein coordinates).
When
m^{2}>0
one additionally obtains a
past
timelike scattering state
(\phi_{-},\pi_{-})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
encoding the
i^{-}
channel.

Definition 10.6

(Full past nonlinear radiation data)
.

For a global small charge–free solution
(F,\phi)
we define the full past data by

\mathcal{R}_{-}:=\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\psi_{\mathcal{H}^{-}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}^{-}}\bigr),

and, when
m^{2}>0
, the extended past data by

\mathcal{R}_{-}^{(m)}:=\bigl(\psi_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{I}^{-}},\psi_{\mathcal{H}^{-}},(\Psi_{F})_{\mathcal{H}^{-}},\ \phi_{-},\pi_{-}\bigr).

Theorem 10.15

(Two–sided nonlinear scattering)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Corollary
1.15
and let
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Then every small admissible charge–free Cauchy datum on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
generates a unique global solution
(F,\phi)
defined for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover, the past and future characteristic radiation data
\mathcal{R}_{\pm}
on

\partial_{\pm}^{(0)}\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}

are well-defined.
When
m^{2}>0
, the corresponding timelike scattering states
(\phi_{-},\pi_{-})
and
(\phi_{+},\pi_{+})
are also well-defined in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
.
Equivalently, the full asymptotic data
\mathcal{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}
are well-defined on the extended scattering boundary
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
(Definition
10.7
).
In particular, we obtain two maps

\mathscr{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{full}},\ \mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}:\ \text{(small Cauchy data)}\longrightarrow\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}_{\pm},&m^{2}=0,\\
\mathcal{R}_{\pm}^{(m)},&m^{2}>0,\end{cases}

which are continuous with respect to the natural energy topologies.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of a global smooth solution for
t\geq t_{0}
is Corollary
1.15
.
To extend the solution to
t\leq t_{0}
, we use time reversal on the static Schwarzschild exterior: introducing the reversed time variable
\tilde{t}:=-t
(so that
\partial_{\tilde{t}}=-\partial_{t}
), the Schwarzschild metric is unchanged and the Lorenz-gauge Maxwell–Higgs equations retain the same hyperbolic form.
Therefore the same small-data global existence argument applied with initial hypersurface
\Sigma_{\tilde{t}_{0}}
(where
\tilde{t}_{0}=-t_{0}
) produces a unique solution for
\tilde{t}\geq\tilde{t}_{0}
, i.e.
t\leq t_{0}
.
Gluing the two evolutions by uniqueness on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
yields a global solution on all slices
\Sigma_{t}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

The past decay and flux estimates are obtained by applying Corollaries
1.16
–
1.17
and Propositions
10.3
–
10.5
to the time-reversed solution in the
\tilde{t}
-future direction. Translating back to the original
t
-coordinates yields the stated bounds.
This yields the existence of the past radiation data on the past scattering boundary
\partial_{-}^{(0)}\mathcal{D}
(that is, on
\mathcal{I}^{-}\cup\mathcal{H}^{-}
) and, when
m^{2}>0
, the past timelike state
(\phi_{-},\pi_{-})
in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{k-2}
, with the same a priori bounds.
Equivalently, the full past asymptotic data are well-defined on the extended boundary
\partial_{-}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.
The corresponding maps
\mathscr{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{full}}
and
\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}
are continuous because each component of the radiation/timelike data is obtained as a limit controlled by the uniform energy bounds and the flux estimates.
∎

10.12.1
Nonlinear wave operators and asymptotic completeness

The maps
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
constructed above send small charge–free Cauchy data to asymptotic data on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
.
We now upgrade this to a genuine
small-data asymptotic completeness
statement:
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{full}}
admit continuous inverses on a neighbourhood of the origin.
Equivalently, one can prescribe small asymptotic data on
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
and solve the corresponding nonlinear final-state problem.

Definition 10.7

(Linear asymptotic data spaces)
.

Fix
k\geq 6
and
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Let
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
denote the chosen initial hypersurface in the foliation (in the Schwarzschild model one has
\tau=t
, while on Kerr we take
\tau=t^{\star}
). Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin}}^{k-2}
denote the Hilbert space of charge–free Cauchy data for the linear comparison system (
10.33
)–(
10.34
) on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
, with norm given by the
(k-2)
–times commuted
nondegenerate
energy flux through
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
(e.g. the redshift
N
–energy in the package
\mathsf{Lin}_{k-2}
; in Schwarzschild one may take the
T
–energies of Definition
10.5
).

Let
\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}
denote the linear scattering map from linear Cauchy data to linear asymptotic data on the (possibly extended) boundary
\partial_{\pm}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
:
for
m^{2}=0
this is the characteristic radiation data on
\mathcal{I}^{\pm}\cup\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
(as provided by the linear package; in the Schwarzschild model see Theorem
10.7
and its time reverse), while for
m^{2}>0
one adjoins the timelike/Dollard datum at
i^{\pm}
as in
[
6
,
7
]
.
We set

\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}:=\mathrm{Ran}\bigl(\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr),\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}(\varepsilon):=\bigl\{\mathcal{R}\in\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}:\ \|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}\leq\varepsilon\bigr\},

and equip
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}
with the norm

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)}}:=\bigl\|(\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}})^{-1}\mathcal{R}\bigr\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin}}^{k-2}}.

When we wish to emphasize the dependence on the background Kerr parameters, we write

\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin},M,a}^{k-2}:=\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{lin}}^{k-2},\qquad\mathscr{S}_{\pm,M,a}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=\mathscr{S}_{\pm}^{\mathrm{lin}},\qquad\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}:=\mathfrak{R}_{\pm}^{(m)},

and similarly for the associated norms and the neighbourhoods
\mathfrak{R}_{\pm,M,a}^{(m)}(\varepsilon)
.

Definition 10.8

(Wave-operator solution space and norm)
.

Fix
\ell:=k-2
.
We write
X^{\ell}=X^{\ell}([t_{0},\infty))
for the space of pairs
(a,u)
such that

a
is a Lorenz
1
–form on
\mathcal{D}
with charge-free curvature
G:=\mathrm{d}a
and
u
is a complex scalar field, for which the norm

\displaystyle\|(a,u)\|_{X^{\ell}}:=\;

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\Bigl(\|G(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}^{\ell}}+\|(u,\nabla_{n}u)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{\ell}}\Bigr)

(10.52)

\displaystyle+\Biggl(\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\Biggr)^{1/2}

(10.53)

\displaystyle+\Biggl(\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\Bigl(\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}a(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\Biggr)^{1/2},

(10.54)

is finite, where

\|G(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}^{\ell}}^{2}:=\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}E^{\hat{t}}\!\big[\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}G\big](t).

We write
B_{\rho}\subset X^{\ell}
for the closed ball of radius
\rho
centered at the origin.

Lemma 10.16

(Linear inhomogeneous estimate in
X^{\ell}
)
.

Fix
\ell:=k-2\geq 2
and
m^{2}\geq 0
.
Let
J
be a
1
–form and
H
a complex scalar on
\mathcal{D}
such that for every commuted field

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
with
|I|+j\leq\ell
one has

\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J\in L^{1}\bigl([t_{0},\infty);L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr),\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H\in L^{1}\bigl([t_{0},\infty);L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr).

Let
(a,u)
solve the linear inhomogeneous Lorenz-gauge systems

\nabla^{\alpha}(\mathrm{d}a)_{\alpha\beta}=J_{\beta},\qquad\nabla^{\alpha}{}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}a)_{\alpha\beta}=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}a_{\mu}=0,

(10.55)

and

(\square_{g}-m^{2})u=H,

(10.56)

on
t\geq t_{0}
, with
vanishing forward asymptotic data
on
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}

(i.e. zero radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
and vanishing
i^{+}
channel when
m^{2}>0
).
Then there exists a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(M,k)
such that

\|(a,u)\|_{X^{\ell}}\leq C_{0}\Biggl(\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t\Biggr).

(10.57)

Proof.

We sketch the argument and indicate where the estimates in earlier sections enter.

Energy part.

Let
G:=\mathrm{d}a
.
Commuting (
10.55
)–(
10.56
) with
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
(
|I|+j\leq\ell
) and applying
Lemmas
10.10
and
10.8
on
[t,T]
yields, for any
T\geq t\geq t_{0}
,

\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}G)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}}\lesssim\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}G)(T)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}}+\int_{t}^{T}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and

\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u,\nabla_{n}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\lesssim\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u,\nabla_{n}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)(T)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}+\int_{t}^{T}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

The forward vanishing asymptotic data imply that the energy terms at time
T
vanish as
T\to+\infty
.
Taking
T\to+\infty
and then the supremum over
t\geq t_{0}
gives the first line in (
10.52
) bounded by the right-hand side of (
10.57
).

Morawetz/ILD part for
u
.

Applying the Morawetz identity of Section
8.3
to the commuted Klein–Gordon equation

(\square_{g}-m^{2})\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u=\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H

produces the integrated local energy bound (schematically)

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u,\nabla_{n}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(t)\|_{L^{2}}\,\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using Cauchy–Schwarz in
t
and absorbing yields

\displaystyle\left(\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u,\nabla_{n}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(t)\|_{L^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

Combining this with the energy bound already obtained gives the second line in (
10.52
) controlled by the right-hand side of (
10.57
).

L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}
part.

In Lorenz gauge and in vacuum Schwarzschild, (
10.55
) implies the component wave equation

\square_{g}a_{\beta}=J_{\beta}
.
Applying the far-region
r^{p}
hierarchy and Sobolev arguments of Section
9
(which are based on linear divergence identities) to

\square_{g}(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}a)=\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J

and

(\square_{g}-m^{2})(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)=\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H

yields pointwise bounds, for
|I|+j\leq\ell
, of the schematic form

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}a(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+t)^{-1}\sup_{s\geq t}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}G(s)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{\infty}(1+s)^{-1}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+t)^{-1}\sup_{s\geq t}\|(\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u,\nabla_{n}\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}u)(s)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{t}^{\infty}(1+s)^{-1}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(Here the integrals arise from the source terms in the
r^{p}
divergence identity and are treated by Cauchy–Schwarz.)
Since
\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}(1+t)^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
, squaring and integrating in
t
gives the last line in (
10.52
) bounded by

\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\Bigl(\|G(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}^{\ell}}+\|(u,\nabla_{n}u)(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{\ell}}\Bigr)+\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\Bigl(\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}H(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Combining with the already established energy bounds completes the proof of (
10.57
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

We indicate the forward construction; the backward one follows by the time-reversal argument in Theorem
10.15
.

Fix
\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{+}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})
and let
(F^{\mathrm{lin}},\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}})
be the corresponding linear solution.
Choose a Lorenz-gauge potential
A^{\mathrm{lin}}
for the free Maxwell field
F^{\mathrm{lin}}
, so that

\nabla^{\mu}A^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\mu}=0,\qquad\mathrm{d}A^{\mathrm{lin}}=F^{\mathrm{lin}},\qquad\square_{g}A^{\mathrm{lin}}_{\nu}=0.

We seek a nonlinear Lorenz-gauge solution
(A,\phi)
of the form

A=A^{\mathrm{lin}}+a,\qquad F=F^{\mathrm{lin}}+G\ \ \text{with}\ \ G:=\mathrm{d}a,\qquad\phi=\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u,

where
(a,u)
has
zero forward asymptotic data
(vanishing radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
and vanishing
i^{+}
channel in the massive case), and where we impose the Lorenz condition
\nabla^{\mu}a_{\mu}=0
.
Subtracting the linear comparison system from the Maxwell–Higgs system in Lorenz gauge, we find that the correction
(G,u)
solves

\nabla^{\alpha}G_{\alpha\beta}=J_{\beta}[\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u;\,A^{\mathrm{lin}}+a],\qquad\nabla^{\alpha}{}^{\star}G_{\alpha\beta}=0,

(10.58)

and

(\square_{g}-m^{2})u=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}\bigl(A^{\mathrm{lin}}+a,\ \varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u\bigr),

(10.59)

where
J_{\beta}[\phi;A]:=-i\bigl(D_{\beta}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\beta}\phi}\bigr)
with
D_{\mu}=\nabla_{\mu}-iA_{\mu}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
is the nonlinear forcing in (
10.39
).
We solve the nonlinear final-state problem by a Picard iteration in the Banach space
X^{\ell}
from Definition
10.8
.
For
\rho>0
to be chosen, consider the closed ball
B_{\rho}\subset X^{\ell}
.

Step 1: the fixed-point map.

Given
(a^{(n)},u^{(n)})\in B_{\rho}
, set

A^{(n)}:=A^{\mathrm{lin}}+a^{(n)},\qquad\phi^{(n)}:=\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u^{(n)}.

Define the sources

J^{(n)}_{\beta}:=J_{\beta}[\phi^{(n)};A^{(n)}],\qquad\mathcal{N}^{(n)}:=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(A^{(n)},\phi^{(n)}).

Let
(a^{(n+1)},u^{(n+1)})
be the unique solution of the linear final-state problems

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}(\mathrm{d}a^{(n+1)})_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=J^{(n)}_{\beta},\qquad\nabla^{\alpha}{}^{\star}(\mathrm{d}a^{(n+1)})_{\alpha\beta}=0,\qquad\nabla^{\mu}a^{(n+1)}_{\mu}=0,

(10.60)

\displaystyle(\square_{g}-m^{2})u^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}^{(n)},

(10.61)

with
vanishing forward asymptotic data
on
\partial_{+}^{(m)}\mathcal{D}
(i.e. zero radiation fields on
\mathcal{I}^{+}\cup\mathcal{H}^{+}
and zero
i^{+}
channel when
m^{2}>0
).
Existence and uniqueness of such solutions follow from the linear scattering theory in Theorem
10.7
together with the massive Dollard-modified asymptotic completeness results invoked in Definition
10.7
.
This defines the fixed-point map
\mathcal{T}:B_{\rho}\to X^{\ell}
by
\mathcal{T}(a^{(n)},u^{(n)})=(a^{(n+1)},u^{(n+1)})
.

Step 2: a priori estimate for the linear final-state map.

Let
G^{(n+1)}:=\mathrm{d}a^{(n+1)}
.
Applying Lemmas
10.10
and
10.8

backwards from
+\infty
gives the energy bounds (for each commuted field
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
with
|I|+j\leq\ell
)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\|G^{(n+1)}(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{Mx}}^{\ell}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}J^{(n)}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(10.62)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq t_{0}}\|(u^{(n+1)},\nabla_{n}u^{(n+1)})(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}^{\ell}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|I|+j\leq\ell}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}\mathcal{N}^{(n)}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(10.63)

Indeed, for example, (
10.38
) with
t_{1}=t
and
t_{2}=T
gives

\|(u^{(n+1)},\nabla_{n}u^{(n+1)})(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}\leq\|(u^{(n+1)},\nabla_{n}u^{(n+1)})(T)\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{KG}}}+\int_{t}^{T}\|\mathcal{N}^{(n)}(s)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

and the first term vanishes as
T\to+\infty
by the imposed zero forward data; the Maxwell estimate is identical.
The remaining spacetime terms in (
10.52
) are controlled by Lemma
10.16
, which packages the inhomogeneous Morawetz/
r^{p}
estimates for (
10.60
)–(
10.61
).
By Lemma
10.16
applied to (
10.60
)–(
10.61
) with
J=J^{(n)}
and
H=\mathcal{N}^{(n)}
, there is a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(M,k)
such that (
10.57
) holds.

Step 3: source estimates in
L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}
.

We estimate the right-hand side of (
10.57
) using only the norms appearing in (
10.52
).
First, using
|J[\phi;A]|\lesssim|\phi|\,|D\phi|
and
|D\phi|\lesssim|\nabla\phi|+|A|\,|\phi|
, we have for each
t\geq t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|J^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\phi^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\Bigl(\|\nabla\phi^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|A^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\|\phi^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\Bigr).

(10.64)

Integrating in
t
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz yields

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|J^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\nabla\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

(10.65)

\displaystyle\quad+\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\,\|A^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}.

Next, from the explicit form (
10.39
) of
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
(with
A=A^{(n)}
and
\phi=\phi^{(n)}
) and the potential bound (
1.14
) we obtain the pointwise estimate

|\mathcal{N}^{(n)}|\lesssim|A^{(n)}|\,|\nabla\phi^{(n)}|+|A^{(n)}|^{2}|\phi^{(n)}|+(1+|\phi^{(n)}|^{2N_{P}})\,|\phi^{(n)}|^{3}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{N}^{(n)}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\lesssim\|A^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\nabla\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

(10.66)

\displaystyle\quad+\Bigl(\|A^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+(1+\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2N_{P}})\,\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}\Bigr)\,\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}.

Here
\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}
is controlled by the energy component of the
X^{\ell}
–norm via Sobolev on
\Sigma_{t}
since
\ell\geq 2
(and hence is
\lesssim\rho
on the ball
B_{\rho}
).The same inequalities hold after commuting with
\mathcal{L}_{\Omega}^{I}T^{j}
,
|I|+j\leq\ell
, by standard product/Moser estimates and the fact that
k\geq 6
provides enough regularity for Sobolev on
\Sigma_{t}
.

Step 4:
\mathcal{T}
maps
B_{\rho}
to itself.

By linear scattering, the fixed linear solution satisfies

\|A^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\ \lesssim\ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}.

For
(a^{(n)},u^{(n)})\in B_{\rho}
, Definition
10.8
implies

\|a^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|u^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla u^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|u^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\ \lesssim\ \rho.

Hence the full fields satisfy the schematic bounds

\|A^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\nabla\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\phi^{(n)}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\ \lesssim\ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho.

Inserting these into (
10.57
)–(
10.66
) yields

\|\mathcal{T}(a^{(n)},u^{(n)})\|_{X^{\ell}}\ \leq\ C_{1}\,(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)^{2},

(10.67)

for a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(M,k)
.
Choosing
\rho:=2C_{1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}
and then
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}
sufficiently small (so that
C_{1}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)\leq 1
), we obtain
\|\mathcal{T}(a^{(n)},u^{(n)})\|_{X^{\ell}}\leq\rho
, i.e.
\mathcal{T}(B_{\rho})\subseteq B_{\rho}
.

Step 5: contraction estimate.

Let
(a,u),(\tilde{a},\tilde{u})\in B_{\rho}
and write

A:=A^{\mathrm{lin}}+a,\quad\tilde{A}:=A^{\mathrm{lin}}+\tilde{a},\qquad\phi:=\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u,\quad\tilde{\phi}:=\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+\tilde{u}.

Set
\Delta a:=a-\tilde{a}
and
\Delta u:=u-\tilde{u}
(so
\Delta\phi=\Delta u
and
\Delta A=\Delta a
).
Using the bilinear structure of
J_{\beta}[\phi;A]=-i(D_{\beta}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\beta}\phi})
(equivalently
2\Im(\bar{\phi}D_{\beta}\phi)
) and the pointwise bound
|D\phi-D\tilde{\phi}|\lesssim|\nabla\Delta\phi|+|A|\,|\Delta\phi|+|\Delta A|\,|\tilde{\phi}|
, we obtain for each
t
,

\displaystyle\|\Delta J(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|D\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|D\phi(t)-D\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}

(10.68)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta\phi(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|D\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla\Delta\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}+\|\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|A(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta\phi(t)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\|\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Delta A(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\tilde{\phi}(t)\|_{L^{2}}.

Integrating in
t
and applying Cauchy–Schwarz gives

\|\Delta J\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}\ \lesssim\ (\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)\,\|(\Delta a,\Delta u)\|_{X^{\ell}}.

(10.69)

Similarly, expanding
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(A,\phi)-\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(\tilde{A},\tilde{\phi})
using (
10.39
) yields

\displaystyle\|\Delta\mathcal{N}\|_{L^{1}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta A\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\nabla\phi\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\tilde{A}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\nabla\Delta\phi\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl(\|A\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}+\|\tilde{A}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\bigr)\|\Delta A\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}\,\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}+\|\tilde{A}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}\,\|\Delta\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\quad+\bigl(\|\phi\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}+\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x}}^{2}\bigr)\,\|\Delta\phi\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}L^{2}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)\,\|(\Delta a,\Delta u)\|_{X^{\ell}},

(10.70)

where in the last step we used
(a,u),(\tilde{a},\tilde{u})\in B_{\rho}
.
Applying the linear final-state estimate (
10.57
) to the difference of the iterates (which solves the same linear equations with sources
(\Delta J,\Delta\mathcal{N})
and zero forward data) and using (
10.69
)–(
10.12.1
), we obtain

\|\mathcal{T}(a,u)-\mathcal{T}(\tilde{a},\tilde{u})\|_{X^{\ell}}\ \leq\ C_{2}\,(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)\,\|(a,u)-(\tilde{a},\tilde{u})\|_{X^{\ell}}.

(10.71)

Choosing
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}
sufficiently small (and recalling
\rho=2C_{1}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}
), we ensure
C_{2}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}}+\rho)<\tfrac{1}{2}
and hence
\mathcal{T}
is a strict contraction on
B_{\rho}
.
By the Banach fixed-point theorem, there exists a unique fixed point
(a,u)\in B_{\rho}
with
\mathcal{T}(a,u)=(a,u)
, which solves (
10.58
)–(
10.59
) and has vanishing forward asymptotic data by construction.

Setting
(F,\phi)=(F^{\mathrm{lin}}+G,\varphi^{\mathrm{lin}}+u)
yields a global nonlinear solution with
\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}(F,\phi)=\mathcal{R}_{+}^{\mathrm{lin}}
.
The scattering statements (matching of radiation fields and the
i^{+}
channel) follow from the vanishing asymptotic data of
(G,u)
combined with Theorem
10.13
.
Uniqueness in the small-data class follows by applying the same energy inequalities to the difference of two solutions with identical forward data, which again yields a fixed point with zero data and hence the trivial solution.

Continuity of
\mathscr{W}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}
and of
\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}
follows from continuous dependence of the fixed point on the datum and from the stability estimates behind the contraction argument.
∎

Corollary 10.17

(Two–sided nonlinear scattering operator)
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.14
, the two–sided nonlinear scattering operator

\mathscr{S}^{\mathrm{full}}:=\mathscr{S}_{+}^{\mathrm{full}}\circ\bigl(\mathscr{S}_{-}^{\mathrm{full}}\bigr)^{-1}:\ \mathfrak{R}_{-}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{R}_{+}^{(m)}(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{sc}})

is well-defined on a neighbourhood of
0
and is a homeomorphism onto its image (with inverse obtained by swapping
+
and
-
).

Appendix A
Global existence on Schwarzschild spacetimes

This appendix provides a self-contained and referee-readable record of the
global existence
mechanism for the Lorenz-gauge Maxwell–Higgs
Cauchy problem on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
.
No new estimates are proved here; the goal is to isolate the standard local well-posedness, constraint propagation, and continuation steps
which turn the
a priori
higher-order energy bounds proved in the main text (Corollary
1.15
) into global existence.

Throughout we fix
M>0
and an integer
k\geq 6
.
We work in the charge-free regime (Definition
1.1
) so that, after subtracting the stationary Coulomb component,
the Maxwell field is exact and admits a global potential
A
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
on
\mathcal{D}_{M,0}
.

A.1
Geometric setup, Sobolev norms, and admissible data

Let
t
denote the standard Schwarzschild time function on the exterior region
\{r>2M\}
and let

\Sigma_{t}:=\{t=\mathrm{const}\}
be the associated spacelike hypersurfaces.
We write
n=n_{\Sigma_{t}}
for the future unit normal and
\gamma
for the induced Riemannian metric on
\Sigma_{t}
.
For an integer
s\geq 0
we write
H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})
for the (intrinsic) Sobolev space defined using
\gamma
,
and we extend the notation componentwise to tensor fields on
\Sigma_{t}
.

Since the Schwarzschild exterior is stationary and the slices
\Sigma_{t}
are translates of
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
under the Killing flow of
T=\partial_{t}
,
the constants in the Sobolev embeddings and Moser/product estimates on
\Sigma_{t}
can be chosen uniformly in
t
.
We record the only analytic input we need.

Lemma A.1

(Uniform Sobolev/Moser estimates on
\Sigma_{t}
)
.

Let
s\geq 3
.
Then
H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{t})
and
H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})
is an algebra.
In particular, for smooth functions
f,g
on
\Sigma_{t}
one has the product estimate

\|fg\|_{H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma_{t})}\|g\|_{H^{s}(\Sigma_{t})},

with an implicit constant depending only on
(M,s)
(and not on
t
).

Proof.

This is standard on
3
–dimensional asymptotically flat hypersurfaces with bounded geometry.
Uniformity in
t
follows from stationarity: all
\Sigma_{t}
are isometric to
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
∎

Admissible Lorenz-gauge data.

A smooth Cauchy data set on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
for the Lorenz-reduced system consists of

\bigl(A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\ \nabla_{n}A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\ \phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}},\ D_{n}\phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\bigr),

as in Section
7.2
.
We call such data
Lorenz-compatible
if the constraints from Section
7.2
hold, namely

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\big|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}=0,\qquad\nabla_{n}\bigl(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\bigr)\big|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}=0,\qquad\nabla^{i}E_{i}=-\,J_{\mu}n^{\mu}\ \ \text{on }\Sigma_{t_{0}},

where
E_{i}:=F(n,e_{i})
is the electric field and
\nabla^{i}
is the Levi–Civita connection of
(\Sigma_{t_{0}},\gamma)
.

A.2
Lorenz reduction, current conservation, and constraint propagation

We impose the Lorenz gauge condition

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0.

(A.1)

We recall the standard reduction of Maxwell–Higgs to a semilinear wave system for
(A,\phi)
on a Ricci-flat background.

Lemma A.2

(Wave system for the potential)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be Ricci flat and let
F=\mathrm{d}A
.
Then the Maxwell equation
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=j_{\nu}
with source
j_{\nu}
is equivalent to

\square_{g}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})=j_{\nu}.

(A.2)

In particular, if the Lorenz gauge condition
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
holds, then

\square_{g}A_{\nu}=j_{\nu}.

(A.3)

Proof.

Since
F_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}A_{\mu}
, we compute

\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=\nabla^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\nabla^{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}A_{\mu}=\square_{g}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})-(\mathrm{Ric})_{\nu}{}^{\mu}A_{\mu}.

On a Ricci-flat background the Ricci term vanishes, giving (
A.2
).
The Lorenz specialization is immediate.
∎

For Maxwell–Higgs, the source is the gauge current

J_{\nu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr),\qquad D_{\nu}:=\nabla_{\nu}-iA_{\nu}.

The scalar field satisfies

D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

with
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
gauge invariant (Assumption
1.1
).

Lemma A.3

(Current conservation)
.

Let
(A,\phi)
be a smooth pair and define the Maxwell–Higgs current

J_{\nu}:=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr).

Then

\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=2\,\Im\!\bigl(\bar{\phi}\,D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi\bigr).

(A.4)

In particular, if
\phi
solves the scalar equation
D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
with gauge-invariant potential
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
,
then
\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0
.

Proof.

Set
Q_{\nu}:=\bar{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi
.
Under the gauge transformation (
2.1
) one has
Q_{\nu}\mapsto Q_{\nu}
, so
Q_{\nu}
is gauge invariant (and thus uncharged).
Consequently, its divergence may be computed using either
\nabla
or the gauge-covariant derivative
D
:

\nabla^{\nu}Q_{\nu}=D^{\nu}Q_{\nu}.

Since
J_{\nu}=-i(Q_{\nu}-\overline{Q_{\nu}})
, we obtain

\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=-i\bigl(\nabla^{\nu}Q_{\nu}-\overline{\nabla^{\nu}Q_{\nu}}\bigr)=-i\bigl(D^{\nu}Q_{\nu}-\overline{D^{\nu}Q_{\nu}}\bigr).

Using the Leibniz rule for
D
on scalar fields,

D^{\nu}Q_{\nu}=D^{\nu}(\bar{\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi)=(D^{\nu}\bar{\phi})\,D_{\nu}\phi+\bar{\phi}\,D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi.

The first term
(D^{\nu}\bar{\phi})\,D_{\nu}\phi=\overline{D^{\nu}\phi}\,D_{\nu}\phi
is real-valued, hence contributes no imaginary part.
Therefore

\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=-i\Bigl(\bar{\phi}\,D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi-\phi\,\overline{D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi}\Bigr)=2\,\Im\!\bigl(\bar{\phi}\,D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi\bigr),

which is (
A.4
).
If
D^{\nu}D_{\nu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})
and
P(\phi,\bar{\phi})=\widetilde{P}(|\phi|^{2})
, then
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P=\widetilde{P}^{\prime}(|\phi|^{2})\phi
and hence

\bar{\phi}\,\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P=\widetilde{P}^{\prime}(|\phi|^{2})|\phi|^{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, so the imaginary part vanishes.
∎

We now record propagation of the Lorenz constraint.

Lemma A.4

(Propagation of the Lorenz constraint)
.

Let
(A,\phi)
solve the Lorenz-reduced Maxwell–Higgs system on
(\mathcal{D}_{M,0},g_{M,0})
,

\square_{g_{M,0}}A_{\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(A.5)

and set
u:=\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}
.
Then
u
satisfies the homogeneous wave equation

\square_{g_{M,0}}u=0.

(A.6)

Consequently, if the Lorenz compatibility conditions

u|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}=0
and
\nabla_{n}u|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}=0
hold on one Cauchy slice, then
u\equiv 0
on the entire domain of existence.

Proof.

By Lemma
A.2
, the Maxwell equation
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}
with
F=\mathrm{d}A
is equivalent to

\square_{g_{M,0}}A_{\nu}-\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})=J_{\nu}.

Taking the divergence and using
\nabla^{\nu}\nabla_{\nu}(\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu})=\square_{g_{M,0}}u
yields

\square_{g_{M,0}}u=\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}.

By Lemma
A.3
we have
\nabla^{\nu}J_{\nu}=0
whenever
\phi
solves the scalar equation with gauge-invariant potential,
hence (
A.6
).
Uniqueness for the scalar wave equation (
A.6
) with vanishing Cauchy data gives
u\equiv 0
.
∎

A.3
Local well-posedness in Lorenz gauge

We now give a detailed local well-posedness statement for the Lorenz-reduced system.
(For the nonlinear scattering problem in the main text we always work with smooth data; the Sobolev formulation below is included to make the continuation argument precise.)

Proposition A.5

(Local well-posedness in Lorenz gauge on Schwarzschild)
.

Fix
k\geq 6
and a time
t_{0}
.
Let the initial data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
be Lorenz-compatible in the sense of Section
7.2
and satisfy

A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\in H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{t_{0}}),\qquad\nabla_{n}A|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\in H^{k}(\Sigma_{t_{0}}),\qquad\phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\in H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{t_{0}}),\qquad D_{n}\phi|_{\Sigma_{t_{0}}}\in H^{k}(\Sigma_{t_{0}}).

(A.7)

Then there exists
T=T(M,k,\text{data})>0
and a unique solution
(A,\phi)
of (
A.5
) on the slab
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
such that

A\in C^{0}\!\bigl([t_{0},t_{0}+T];H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr)\cap C^{1}\!\bigl([t_{0},t_{0}+T];H^{k}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr),

\phi\in C^{0}\!\bigl([t_{0},t_{0}+T];H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr)\cap C^{1}\!\bigl([t_{0},t_{0}+T];H^{k}(\Sigma_{t})\bigr).

Moreover:

(i)

the Lorenz condition (
A.1
) propagates, i.e.
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
holds on
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
,

(ii)

with
F=\mathrm{d}A
, the pair
(F,\phi)
solves the original Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) on
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
.

Proof.

We outline the standard energy/Picard iteration argument for semilinear wave systems on a fixed globally hyperbolic background.

Step 1: Linear energy estimate.

Let
u
solve
\square_{g_{M,0}}u=f
on
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
with given Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
.
Commuting with
\nabla
up to order
k
and applying the standard
T
–energy identity for each commuted equation yields

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]}\Bigl(\|u(t)\|_{H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|\nabla_{n}u(t)\|_{H^{k}(\Sigma_{t})}\Bigr)\lesssim\|u(t_{0})\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|\nabla_{n}u(t_{0})\|_{H^{k}}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}+T}\|f(s)\|_{H^{k}(\Sigma_{s})}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(A.8)

with an implicit constant depending only on
(M,k)
.
The same estimate holds componentwise for
1
-forms. The same argument also applies to the Klein–Gordon equation
(\square_{g_{M,0}}-m^{2})u=f
with constant
m^{2}\geq 0
, with an implicit constant depending additionally on
m
(equivalently, on the potential through
m^{2}
).

Step 2: Picard iteration.

Define
(A^{(0)},\phi^{(0)})
to be the solution of the homogeneous linear system with the given data.
Given
(A^{(m)},\phi^{(m)})
, define
(A^{(m+1)},\phi^{(m+1)})
to be the solution of the linear inhomogeneous system

\square_{g_{M,0}}A^{(m+1)}_{\nu}=-i\Bigl(D^{(m)}_{\nu}\phi^{(m)}\,\overline{\phi^{(m)}}-\phi^{(m)}\,\overline{D^{(m)}_{\nu}\phi^{(m)}}\Bigr),\qquad(\square_{g_{M,0}}-m^{2})\phi^{(m+1)}=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}\bigl(A^{(m)},\phi^{(m)}\bigr),

with the same initial data, where
D^{(m)}=\nabla-iA^{(m)}
.
Let
m^{2}\geq 0
be as in Assumption
1.1
and define

\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(A,\phi):=2iA^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\phi+A^{\mu}A_{\mu}\phi+\bigl(\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi})-m^{2}\phi\bigr).

Thus the scalar equation in Lorenz gauge takes the Klein–Gordon form
(\square_{g_{M,0}}-m^{2})\phi=\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(A,\phi)
.
Moreover, by (
1.13
)–(
1.14
) the potential remainder
\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P-m^{2}\phi
is at least cubic in
\phi
, so
\mathcal{N}_{\phi}
is a smooth combination of terms at most quadratic in
(A,\nabla\phi)
and at least cubic in
\phi
(thanks to (
1.14
)).

Step 3: Uniform bound for small
T
.

Fix
M_{0}>0
and consider the Banach space
X_{T}
of pairs
(A,\phi)
with the regularity stated in the proposition and norm

\|(A,\phi)\|_{X_{T}}:=\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]}\Bigl(\|A(t)\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|\nabla_{n}A(t)\|_{H^{k}}+\|\phi(t)\|_{H^{k+1}}+\|D_{n}\phi(t)\|_{H^{k}}\Bigr).

By Lemma
A.1
, for
k\geq 6
the nonlinearities are locally Lipschitz on bounded subsets of
X_{T}
:
for
(A,\phi)\in X_{T}
with
\|(A,\phi)\|_{X_{T}}\leq M_{0}
one has, where
J[\phi;A]
denotes the current
J_{\nu}=-i\bigl(D_{\nu}\phi\,\bar{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{\nu}\phi}\bigr)
,

\|J[\phi;A]\|_{H^{k}}+\|\mathcal{N}_{\phi}(A,\phi)\|_{H^{k}}\leq C(M_{0})\,\|(A,\phi)\|_{X_{T}}^{2},

(A.9)

with
C(M_{0})
depending only on
(M,k,M_{0})
and finitely many bounds for
\widetilde{P}
on bounded sets.
Applying (
A.8
) to the iteration and choosing
T>0
so that
T\,C(M_{0})\ll 1
yields a uniform bound

\|(A^{(m)},\phi^{(m)})\|_{X_{T}}\leq M_{0}
for all
m
.

Step 4: Contraction and convergence.

Applying the linear estimate to the difference of two iterates and using the Lipschitz property from Lemma
A.1
,
one obtains for
T
sufficiently small

\|(A^{(m+1)}-A^{(m)},\ \phi^{(m+1)}-\phi^{(m)})\|_{X_{T}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\|(A^{(m)}-A^{(m-1)},\ \phi^{(m)}-\phi^{(m-1)})\|_{X_{T}}.

Hence
(A^{(m)},\phi^{(m)})
converges in
X_{T}
to a limit
(A,\phi)
which solves (
A.5
).
Uniqueness and continuous dependence follow similarly by applying the same estimate to the difference of two solutions.

Step 5: Propagation of constraints and equivalence to Maxwell–Higgs.

By Lemma
A.4
, Lorenz compatibility of the initial data implies
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0
on
\mathcal{R}(t_{0},t_{0}+T)
.
With
F=\mathrm{d}A
, Lemma
A.2
then shows that
(F,\phi)
satisfies the Maxwell equation (
1.5
), while the scalar equation is the second equation in (
A.5
).
This proves (i) and (ii).
∎

A.4
Continuation criterion

We now make the continuation mechanism explicit.
Let
(A,\phi)
be the maximal smooth Lorenz-gauge solution arising from smooth Lorenz-compatible data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
, defined on a maximal forward interval
[t_{0},t_{+})
.

Lemma A.6

(Continuation criterion)
.

Assume that the higher-order energy
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
from (
1.39
) stays finite on
[t_{0},t_{+})
:

\sup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{+})}\ \mathcal{E}_{k}(t)\ <\ \infty.

(A.10)

Then
t_{+}=+\infty
.
An analogous statement holds backward in time.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.5
, the existence time for the Lorenz-reduced system can be chosen to depend only on the size of the Sobolev norm
(
A.7
) of the Cauchy data at the restart time.
For a smooth Lorenz-gauge solution, the energy fluxes appearing in
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
control these Sobolev norms on
\Sigma_{t}

(because
F=\mathrm{d}A
controls first derivatives of
A
, and higher derivatives are controlled by commuting with
\partial_{t}
and
\Omega_{i}

together with the wave equations and the constraints; see Section
7.2
and the discussion surrounding (
1.39
)).
Hence the bound (
A.10
) implies that the Sobolev size of the Cauchy data at time
t
stays uniformly bounded for
t<t_{+}
,
so the local existence time has a uniform positive lower bound.
Iterating the local theory therefore extends the solution beyond
t_{+}
unless
t_{+}=+\infty
.
∎

A.5
Small-data global existence

We finally record the global existence consequence for small Schwarzschild data.

Proposition A.7

(Global existence for small Schwarzschild data)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Corollary
1.15
.
In particular, let the charge-free Lorenz-compatible Schwarzschild data on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}
satisfy
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t_{0})\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, with
\varepsilon_{0}
as in Corollary
1.15
.
Then the corresponding Lorenz-gauge solution
(A,\phi)
exists globally on
\mathcal{D}_{M,0}
(both to the future and the past),
and the associated field pair
(F,\phi)
is smooth and satisfies the uniform boundedness and decay estimates stated in Corollary
1.15
.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.5
there exists a unique smooth Lorenz-gauge solution on a nontrivial initial time slab.
Corollary
1.15
provides a uniform bound for
\mathcal{E}_{k}(t)
on the maximal interval of existence, hence (
A.10
) holds.
Lemma
A.6
therefore yields
t_{+}=+\infty
.
The backward global existence statement follows by applying the same argument to the time-reversed evolution (or by using the backward statement in Corollary
1.15
).
∎

Appendix B
Global existence on Kerr spacetimes

This appendix records the global existence mechanism for the Lorenz-gauge Maxwell–Higgs Cauchy problem on a fixed subextremal Kerr exterior

(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
with
|a|<M
.
As in Appendix
A
, we do not prove any new decay estimates here; we only isolate the local well-posedness,
constraint propagation, and continuation steps which, combined with the
a priori
energy bounds of Theorem
1.1
,
yield global existence in the Kerr setting.

Throughout we fix
M>0
,
|a|<M
, and
k\geq 6
.
We work in the charge-free regime (Definition
1.1
), so that after Coulomb subtraction the Maxwell field is exact and admits a global potential; see Remark
2.1
.

B.1
Geometric setup and uniform Sobolev estimates

We work with the Kerr–star time function
t^{\star}
and the horizon-regular spacelike foliation

\Sigma_{\tau}:=\{t^{\star}=\tau\},

as in (
1.3
).
Let
n=n_{\Sigma_{\tau}}
be the future unit normal and
\gamma
the induced Riemannian metric on
\Sigma_{\tau}
.

For an integer
s\geq 0
we denote by
H^{s}(\Sigma_{\tau})
the intrinsic Sobolev space defined using
\gamma
,
again extended componentwise to tensor fields.
Because Kerr is stationary and the slices
\Sigma_{\tau}
are translates under the flow of the stationary Killing field,
the Sobolev embeddings and Moser estimates hold with constants that are uniform in
\tau
(depending only on
(M,a,s)
).

Lemma B.1

(Uniform Sobolev/Moser estimates on
\Sigma_{\tau}
)
.

Let
s\geq 3
.
Then
H^{s}(\Sigma_{\tau})\hookrightarrow W^{1,\infty}(\Sigma_{\tau})
and
H^{s}(\Sigma_{\tau})
is an algebra, with constants uniform in
\tau
.

Proof.

The Kerr–star slices form a smooth stationary family of
3
–manifolds with bounded geometry on compact sets and asymptotically Euclidean ends.
The standard Sobolev/Moser estimates are therefore uniform under time translation.
∎

Redshift coercivity.

A key distinction from Schwarzschild is the presence of an ergoregion, where the stationary Killing field
T
is spacelike.
For energy estimates and continuation it is therefore convenient to work with the globally timelike
redshift
vector field
N

from Definition
1.3
.
The associated energy flux controls the full
H^{1}
size of
(F,\phi)
on each slice.

Lemma B.2

(Coercivity of the
N
–flux)
.

Let
(F,\phi)
be smooth on
\Sigma_{\tau}
and let
N
be the admissible redshift vector field.
Then

E^{N}[F,\phi](\tau)=\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}T_{\mu\nu}[F,\phi]\,N^{\nu}\,n^{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}}

is comparable to the
H^{1}
–type norm of
(F,\phi)
on
\Sigma_{\tau}
:

E^{N}[F,\phi](\tau)\simeq\int_{\Sigma_{\tau}}\Bigl(|F(n,\cdot)|_{\gamma}^{2}+|F|_{\gamma}^{2}+|D_{n}\phi|^{2}+|D\phi|_{\gamma}^{2}+P(\phi,\bar{\phi})\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\Sigma_{\tau}},

with constants depending only on
(M,a)
(and uniform in
\tau
).

Proof.

By construction,
N
is future-directed timelike and uniformly comparable to
n
on the Kerr–star foliation,
so the quadratic form
T_{\mu\nu}N^{\nu}n^{\mu}
is positive definite in
(F,D\phi)
and dominates the potential term
P\geq 0
.
Uniformity in
\tau
follows from stationarity.
∎

B.2
Lorenz reduction and constraint propagation

In Lorenz gauge,

\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0,

(B.1)

the Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) reduces on Kerr to the semilinear wave system

\square_{g_{M,a}}A_{\nu}=J_{\nu},\qquad D^{\mu}D_{\mu}\phi=\partial_{\bar{\phi}}P(\phi,\bar{\phi}),

(B.2)

as stated in (
2.3
).
The proofs of Lemmas
A.2
and
A.3

use only Ricci flatness and gauge invariance of
P
, and therefore apply verbatim on Kerr.

In particular, for a solution of (
B.2
) the current
J_{\nu}
is divergence free and the Lorenz constraint propagates.

Lemma B.3

(Propagation of the Lorenz constraint on Kerr)
.

Let
(A,\phi)
solve (
B.2
) on
(\mathcal{D}_{M,a},g_{M,a})
and set
u:=\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}
.
Then
u
satisfies
\square_{g_{M,a}}u=0
.
In particular, if the Lorenz compatibility conditions (
2.6
) hold on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
, then
\nabla^{\mu}A_{\mu}\equiv 0
on the domain of existence.

Proof.

This is the Kerr analogue of Lemma
A.4
and follows by taking the divergence of the wave equation for
A

and using current conservation.
∎

B.3
Local well-posedness in Lorenz gauge

We now formulate local well-posedness for the Lorenz-reduced system on Kerr.
The statement is completely standard for semilinear wave systems on a fixed globally hyperbolic background; we include it to make the continuation argument explicit.

Proposition B.4

(Local well-posedness in Lorenz gauge on Kerr)
.

Fix
k\geq 6
and an initial slice
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
.
Let the initial data (
2.4
) be Lorenz-compatible in the sense of (
2.6
), and assume that

A|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\in H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}),\qquad\nabla_{n}A|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\in H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}),\qquad\phi|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\in H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}),\qquad D_{n}\phi|_{\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}}\in H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}).

(B.3)

Then there exists
T=T(M,a,k,\text{data})>0
and a unique solution
(A,\phi)
of (
B.2
) on the slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T)
such that

A\in C^{0}\!\bigl([\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T];H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau})\bigr)\cap C^{1}\!\bigl([\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T];H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau})\bigr),

\phi\in C^{0}\!\bigl([\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T];H^{k+1}(\Sigma_{\tau})\bigr)\cap C^{1}\!\bigl([\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T];H^{k}(\Sigma_{\tau})\bigr).

Moreover, the Lorenz condition propagates and with
F=\mathrm{d}A
the pair
(F,\phi)
solves the original Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) on
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the same Picard iteration as in Proposition
A.5
.
The linear energy estimate is obtained by commuting
\square_{g_{M,a}}u=f
with
\nabla
up to order
k
and applying the standard hyperbolic energy identity on the globally hyperbolic slab
\mathcal{R}(\tau_{0},\tau_{0}+T)
.
The nonlinear estimates use Lemma
B.1
.
Propagation of Lorenz gauge is provided by Lemma
B.3
.
∎

B.4
Continuation criterion and global existence

Let
(A,\phi)
be the maximal smooth Lorenz-gauge solution arising from smooth Lorenz-compatible data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
, defined on a maximal forward interval
[\tau_{0},\tau_{+})
.

Lemma B.5

(Continuation criterion)
.

Assume that the order-
k
redshift energy flux from Definition
2.1
stays finite on
[\tau_{0},\tau_{+})
:

\sup_{\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{+})}\ \mathcal{E}^{N}_{k,M,a}(\tau)\ <\ \infty,

(B.4)

where
\mathcal{E}^{N}_{k,M,a}(\tau)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq k}E^{N}[\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}}F,\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}\phi](\tau)
.
Then
\tau_{+}=+\infty
.
An analogous statement holds backward in time.

Proof.

By Proposition
B.4
, the solution can be restarted at any time
\tau_{1}<\tau_{+}
with an existence time that depends only on the Sobolev size
(
B.3
) of the Cauchy data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{1}}
.
To relate this Sobolev size to the redshift energy, we note first that Lemma
B.2
shows that
E^{N}[F,\phi](\tau)
controls the
H^{1}
size of
(F,\phi)
on
\Sigma_{\tau}
.
Commuting the field equations with
\mathcal{Z}
and applying the same coercivity gives control of higher derivatives, which is exactly the content of the norm

\mathcal{E}^{N}_{k,M,a}(\tau)
.
Thus (
B.4
) yields a uniform bound for the Sobolev norms entering (
B.3
) along the evolution.
Consequently the restart time has a uniform positive lower bound and the maximal time
\tau_{+}
must be infinite.
∎

Proposition B.6

(Global existence for small Kerr data)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
1.1
.
In particular, let the charge-free Lorenz-compatible Kerr data on
\Sigma_{\tau_{0}}
satisfy

\|(A,\phi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{M,a}(\Sigma_{\tau_{0}})}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}}

for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{Kerr}}
sufficiently small.
Then the corresponding Lorenz-gauge solution
(A,\phi)
exists globally on
\mathcal{D}_{M,a}
(both to the future and the past),
and the associated field pair
(F,\phi)
is smooth and satisfies the uniform boundedness and decay estimates stated in Theorem
1.1
.

Proof.

By Proposition
B.4
there exists a unique smooth Lorenz-gauge solution on a nontrivial initial time slab.
The a priori energy bounds in Theorem
1.1
imply the uniform estimate (
B.4
) for the maximal solution.
Lemma
B.5
therefore yields
\tau_{+}=+\infty
.
The backward global existence statement follows by applying the same argument to the time-reversed evolution (or equivalently by using the backward statement in Theorem
1.1
).
∎

Appendix C
Deferred computations and proofs

For readability, the main text emphasizes the geometric structure of the argument and the resulting estimates. Some longer component-level computations are recorded here.

C.1
Explicit expressions for
E^{\hat{K}}
and
J^{\hat{K}}

For the reader’s convenience we record here coordinate expressions for the conformal energy flux (
8.35
) and Morawetz bulk term (
8.34
) associated to the conformal vector field
\hat{K}=-w^{2}\partial_{w}-v^{2}\partial_{v}
.
Throughout this subsection we use the double-null coordinates

v=t+r^{\ast},\qquad w=t-r^{\ast},

and we write
d\sigma^{2}=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi
for the standard area element on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

\displaystyle E^{\hat{K}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\Bigl[w^{2}\Bigl(\frac{|F_{w\theta}|^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{|F_{w\varphi}|^{2}}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}+|D_{w}\phi|^{2}\Bigr)+v^{2}\Bigl(\frac{|F_{v\theta}|^{2}}{r^{2}}+\frac{|F_{v\varphi}|^{2}}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}+|D_{v}\phi|^{2}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+(v^{2}+w^{2})\Bigl(\frac{|F_{vw}|^{2}}{(1-\mu)^{2}}+\frac{1-\mu}{4r^{4}\sin^{2}\theta}\,|F_{\theta\varphi}|^{2}+\Re\bigl(D_{v}\phi\,\overline{D_{w}\phi}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1-\mu}{4r^{2}}\,|D_{\theta}\phi|^{2}+\frac{1-\mu}{4r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\,|D_{\varphi}\phi|^{2}+\frac{1-\mu}{2}\,P(\phi)\Bigr)\Bigr]\,r^{2}\,dr^{\ast}\,d\sigma^{2}.

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\hat{K}}\bigl(t_{i}\leq t\leq t_{i+1}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\!\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\!\int_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}4t\,r^{2}(1-\mu)\,\Bigl[\Bigl(\frac{|F_{vw}|^{2}}{(1-\mu)^{2}}+\frac{|F_{\theta\varphi}|^{2}}{4r^{4}\sin^{2}\theta}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl(2+(3\mu-2)\frac{r^{\ast}}{r}\Bigr)+\Bigl(\frac{|D_{\theta}\phi|^{2}}{2r^{2}}+\frac{|D_{\varphi}\phi|^{2}}{2r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\Bigl(1+\mu\frac{r^{\ast}}{r}\Bigr)-2\frac{r^{\ast}}{r}\,\Re\bigl(D_{v}\phi\,\overline{D_{w}\phi}\bigr)+\Bigl(1-\frac{r^{\ast}}{r}\Bigr)P(\phi)\Bigr]\,d\sigma^{2}\,dr^{\ast}\,dt.

(C.2)

C.2
Conventions for the appendix

Throughout this appendix we work on the Schwarzschild exterior region
\{r>2M\}
and use the standard coordinates
(t,r^{\ast},\theta,\varphi)
, where
r^{\ast}
is the Regge–Wheeler coordinate. (In some intermediate computations we also write
r*
for
r^{\ast}
, following the notation in the main text.) We also use the null coordinates

v=t+r^{\ast},\qquad w=t-r^{\ast}.

Index convention.

We use the coordinate index convention

x^{0}=t,\qquad x^{1}=r^{\ast},\qquad x^{2}=\theta,\qquad x^{3}=\varphi,

so that
\partial_{0}=\partial_{t}
,
\partial_{1}=\partial_{r^{\ast}}
,
\partial_{2}=\partial_{\theta}
, and
\partial_{3}=\partial_{\varphi}
.
Tensor components such as
F_{02}
are taken with respect to this coordinate basis, and we use the antisymmetry
F_{\alpha\beta}=-F_{\beta\alpha}
.

Sphere convention.

On
\mathbb{S}^{2}
we use the area element
d\sigma^{2}=\sin\theta\,d\theta\,d\varphi
, and we write
\not{\nabla}
for the Levi–Civita connection of the unit round metric.

Null derivatives.

Finally,

\partial_{v}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}+\partial_{r^{\ast}}),\qquad\partial_{w}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\partial_{t}-\partial_{r^{\ast}}).

C.3
Proof of Proposition
8.2

Proof of Proposition
8.2
.

We work on the Schwarzschild exterior
(\mathcal{M},g_{M})
and use the stationary null tetrad

\hat{L}:=\partial_{t}+\partial_{r^{\ast}},\qquad\hat{N}:=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r^{\ast}},\qquad\hat{M}:=\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi},\qquad\hat{\bar{M}}:=\partial_{\theta}-\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{\varphi}.

(C.3)

Following
[
24
]
, we introduce the (complex) Maxwell spin components

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{r^{2}}{1-\frac{2M}{r}}\,F(\hat{L},\hat{M})+F(\hat{\bar{M}},\hat{M})\right),

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle:=F(\hat{L},\hat{M}),

\displaystyle\Phi_{-1}

\displaystyle:=F(\hat{N},\hat{\bar{M}}).

(C.4)

Step 1: energy in terms of tetrad components.

In this tetrad the standard
\hat{t}
–energy can be written in terms of the scalar field derivatives and the spin components as

\displaystyle E^{\hat{t}}[F,\phi](t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}(1-\mu)\,r^{2}\Bigl(|D_{\hat{t}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{\theta}}\phi|^{2}+|D_{\hat{\varphi}}\phi|^{2}+|P(\phi)|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(|\Phi_{-1}|^{2}+|\Phi_{1}|^{2}+\Bigl|\frac{1-\mu}{r^{2}}\Phi_{0}\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\,dr^{\ast}\,d\sigma^{2}.

(C.5)

Similarly, commuting with a rotation
\Omega_{j}
and expanding the corresponding commuted energy yields

\displaystyle E^{\hat{t}}_{\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma_{t}}(1-\mu)\,r^{2}\Bigl(|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}(D_{\hat{t}}\phi)|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}(D_{\hat{\theta}}\phi)|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}(D_{\hat{\varphi}}\phi)|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}P(\phi)|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\Phi_{-1}|^{2}+|\mathcal{L}_{\Omega_{j}}\Phi_{1}|^{2}+\Bigl|\frac{1-\mu}{r^{2}}\not{\nabla}\Phi_{0}\Bigr|^{2}\Bigr)\Bigr)\,dr^{\ast}\,d\sigma^{2}.

(C.6)

Step 2: a Newman–Penrose form of Maxwell–Higgs.

The Maxwell–Higgs system (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) is equivalent to a system of transport equations for the spin components.

Lemma C.1
.

The pair
(F,\phi)
satisfies the Maxwell–Higgs equations (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) if and only if the spin components
(\Phi_{-1},\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1})
satisfy

\displaystyle\hat{N}\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r^{2}}\hat{M}\Phi_{0}+i\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r}\bigl(D_{2}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{2}\phi}\bigr)+i\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{\sin\theta}\bigl(D_{3}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{3}\phi}\bigr),

(C.7)

\displaystyle\hat{L}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{1}+ir^{2}\bigl(D_{0}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{0}\phi}\bigr)+ir^{2}\bigl(D_{1}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{1}\phi}\bigr),

(C.8)

\displaystyle\hat{N}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=-\hat{M}_{1}\Phi_{-1}+ir^{2}\bigl(D_{0}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{0}\phi}\bigr)+ir^{2}\bigl(D_{1}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{1}\phi}\bigr),

(C.9)

\displaystyle\hat{L}\Phi_{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r^{2}}\hat{\bar{M}}\Phi_{0}+i\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r}\bigl(D_{2}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{2}\phi}\bigr)+i\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{\sin\theta}\bigl(D_{3}\phi\,\overline{\phi}-\phi\,\overline{D_{3}\phi}\bigr),

(C.10)

where
\hat{M}_{1}:=\hat{M}+\cot\theta
.

Proof.

We sketch the derivation of (
C.7
); the remaining identities follow by the same component computation.
Using the coordinate index convention from Appendix
C.2
, one computes

\displaystyle F(\hat{L},\hat{M})

\displaystyle=F_{02}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{03}+F_{12}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{13},

\displaystyle F(\hat{L},\hat{N})

\displaystyle=2F_{10},

\displaystyle F(\hat{\bar{M}},\hat{M})

\displaystyle=\frac{2i}{\sin\theta}F_{23},

\displaystyle F(\hat{N},\hat{\bar{M}})

\displaystyle=F_{02}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{30}+F_{21}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{13}.

Hence

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=F_{02}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{03}+F_{12}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{13},

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{r^{2}}{1-\frac{2M}{r}}F_{10}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{23},

\displaystyle\Phi_{-1}

\displaystyle=F_{02}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{30}

\displaystyle\quad+F_{21}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}F_{13}.

Applying
\hat{N}=\partial_{t}-\partial_{r^{\ast}}
gives

\displaystyle\hat{N}\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}F_{02}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{0}F_{03}+\partial_{0}F_{12}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{0}F_{13}

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{1}F_{20}+\partial_{1}F_{21}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{1}F_{30}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{1}F_{31}.

(C.11)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r^{2}}\hat{M}\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{2}F_{10}+\frac{i}{\sin\theta}\partial_{3}F_{10}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{i(1-\frac{2M}{r})}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\,\cot\theta\,F_{32}+\frac{i(1-\frac{2M}{r})}{r^{2}\sin\theta}\partial_{2}F_{23}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1-\frac{2M}{r}}{r^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}\partial_{3}F_{32}.

(C.12)

Using the Bianchi identity
\nabla_{[\alpha}F_{\beta\gamma]}=0
to substitute

\partial_{2}F_{10}=\partial_{0}F_{12}+\partial_{1}F_{20}

and

\partial_{3}F_{10}=\partial_{0}F_{13}+\partial_{1}F_{30},

and using the Maxwell equation
\nabla^{\mu}F_{\mu\nu}=J_{\nu}(\phi)
to eliminate the remaining terms, we obtain (
C.7
).
∎
